\section{Provenance and conventions of Part~III}\label{cyc:sec:intro}

\subsection{One manuscript, one addition}\label{cyc:sec:source}
Part~III is batch-42 manuscript~01, \emph{A One-Defect Law for Cyclotomic
Catalan Hankel Recurrences}, subtitled \emph{Complete single-root
classification, a necessary sign parameter, and the central exception}, a
research article prepared with OpenAI ChatGPT for Vladimir Reshetnikov's
ProveIt research program and dated 29 September 2026 (22 A4 pages as
delivered).  It arrived as the archive
\nolinkurl{ProveIt_Cyclotomic_Catalan_Recurrences.zip} (inner directory
\nolinkurl{cyclotomic_catalan/}, main file \nolinkurl{article.tex}) in
ProveIt commit \texttt{8315d24e3} and was placed in \texttt{3609d0473}.  It
was written against ProveIt commit
\texttt{9754e83603e223811b7515eb892f22c847144593}, reading this report's
combined \nolinkurl{article.tex} (Git blob
\texttt{b29ea42fc5bba69d23c3b036bee25357e202f356}), its provenance notes
and the directory listing at that commit; its source notes add that the
standalone Part~II manuscript was read as well.  The report's files are
unchanged between the pin and the placement commit (the same blob), so
every statement the manuscript makes about ``the repository report'' or
``the prior report'' refers to Parts~I and~II as printed above.

The manuscript's code and data are shipped beside the earlier files with
the prefix \texttt{03-cyclotomic-} (Part~I's unprefixed files count as~01,
Part~II's as~02), and its source and comparison notes, delivered as
\nolinkurl{SOURCES.md}, as \nolinkurl{03-cyclotomic-SOURCES.md} at the
report root.  Its manuscript, delivery README and PDF are not shipped;
they survive in the arrival commit.  Section~\ref{cyc:sec:verification}
lists the shipped files.

The manuscript contributes, in the order printed here: the repository
question, what is already known, and why grouping equal rates is not
enough (Section~\ref{cyc:sec:scope}); the definitions, the complete
noncentral classification with its elementary sign test, and the central
theorem (Section~\ref{cyc:sec:main}); a self-contained derivation of the
confluent Christoffel identity in its normalization
(Section~\ref{cyc:sec:orthogonal}); an exact subset expansion of every
canonical sector (Section~\ref{cyc:sec:sectors}); the leading coefficient,
the first centred correction and an exact reflection of the sector
polynomials (Section~\ref{cyc:sec:coefficients}); the one-defect proof
(Section~\ref{cyc:sec:proofmain}); closed orders, the exceptional locus
and its count, the reflection $c\mapsto4-c$ and quadratic growth in the
multiplicity (Section~\ref{cyc:sec:orders}); the central exception via
Cigler's known product, with a self-contained condensation proof
(Section~\ref{cyc:sec:central}); a root-free factorization
(Section~\ref{cyc:sec:algorithm}); worked examples and the classification
of all rational roots (Section~\ref{cyc:sec:examples}); its verification
record and rebuild instructions (Section~\ref{cyc:sec:verification});
eight research directions (Section~\ref{cyc:sec:future}); a conclusion; a
normalization and proof audit (Section~\ref{cyc:app:audit}); and a source
and package concordance (Section~\ref{cyc:app:concordance}).  Every result,
proof, example, remark, limitation and question of the manuscript is
printed.  Where it re-derives something Parts~I or~II already prove, the
overlap is marked (Section~\ref{cyc:sec:merge}).

The manuscript's own abstract reads:
\begin{quote}\small
Let $H_N^{(t)}(c)$ be the size-$N$ Hankel determinant of the Catalan
moments multiplied by $(x-c)^t$, with $c\ne0,4$.  Write
$c=2+z+z^{-1}$ and let $L$ be the order of $z^2$, possibly infinite.
The nonresonant minimal order $t+1+\binom{t+1}{3}$ is already established
in the ProveIt report that motivates this work.  We determine the
minimal recurrence at every remaining specialization.

For $3\le L\le t$, put $\eta=\ind_{t\equiv L\pmod2}$.  The exact order is
\[
 \boxed{\qquad R(t,c)=\frac{L(3t^2-L^2+13-3\eta)}{12}-\delta,
                    \qquad \delta\in\{0,1\}.\qquad}
\]
The defect occurs precisely when $\eta=1$ and
$(-1)^{L(t+1)/2}z^{Lt}=-1$.  We determine the multiplicity of every
characteristic root, not only their total.  The proof isolates the
first two coefficients of each canonical exponential sector using an
exact signed-Vandermonde identity.  A collided sector can lose just
one degree away from $c=2$; at $c=2$ an entire group can disappear.
The latter case is governed by a product formula already proved by
Cigler for middle-binomial Hankel determinants, which we credit and
rederive with the required normalization.

The answer cannot depend on $t$ and $L$ alone: for $t=3$, $c=1$ and
$c=3$ both have $L=3$, but their minimal orders are $7$ and $6$.
Consequences include a complete rational-parameter classification,
an exact count of exceptional parameters, an eventual quadratic
quasipolynomial law in $t$, and a root-free factorization computed with
$O(t)$ scalar field operations.  A standard-library Python audit supplies
22,124 successful exact checks, including independent determinant
computations in cyclotomic integer rings.
\end{quote}

\begin{center}
\fcolorbox{muted}{lightblue}{\parbox{\dimexpr\textwidth-2\fboxsep-2\fboxrule\relax}{%
\textbf{Status (manuscript~01).}
This is an unrefereed, AI-assisted manuscript with ordinary mathematical
proofs, not a Lean formalization.  The proposed extension is the
noncentral cancellation classification and its coefficient mechanism:
the explicit first centred sector coefficient, the noncentral one-defect
theorem, the minimal characteristic polynomial at every cyclotomic
specialization and the resulting sign-sensitive order laws, with the
exceptional-parameter count, the reflection law and the rational-input
classification as corollaries.  Classical determinant identities (the
Christoffel formula and its confluent form, Andr\'eief's identity,
Desnanot--Jacobi condensation), general rationality and the
order-at-most-$2^t$ construction~\cite{Krattenthaler2023}, Part~II's generic
degree law and finite recurrence-recovery algorithm, and Cigler's central
product~\cite{Cigler2021} are not presented as new; the signed Vandermonde
evaluation and the shift-operator minimality lemma are elementary uses of
established techniques, not standalone priority claims.  First-discovery
priority has not been established: a specialized equivalent result may
exist in Wronskian, orthogonal-polynomial or symmetric-function notation,
the targeted searches that returned unrelated results do not establish
absence from the literature, and independent expert review and a broader
priority audit are still appropriate.  The comparison with this report is
targeted, not a proof audit of every report in ProveIt.  The manuscript
does not solve multi-root resonance, does not claim uniform collision
asymptotics, and does not transfer its characteristic-zero minimality
theorem unchanged to positive characteristic; the endpoints $c=0,4$ are
outside its theorems.  Its 22,124 exact assertions are finite supporting
checks, not a universal proof.  No proof assistant was run, nothing in
Part~III is formalized, and its placement beside a Lean project confers no
formal status.  (Sources: the manuscript, its delivery README and its
source notes, \nolinkurl{03-cyclotomic-SOURCES.md}.)}}
\end{center}

\subsection{Reading conventions}\label{cyc:sec:reading}
Inside Sections~\ref{cyc:sec:scope}--\ref{cyc:app:concordance}, ``this
article'' and ``the present paper'' mean manuscript~01 as printed in
Part~III; ``the repository report'', ``the ProveIt report'', ``the prior
report'' and ``the preceding repository work'' mean Parts~I and~II at the
pin \texttt{9754e8360}; and ``the older root-collisions manuscript'' is
Part~II.  The manuscript cites Part~II's standalone section numbers:
its Section~12.1 and Section~12.2 are Sections~\ref{cyc:x:rcq1}
and~\ref{cyc:x:rcq2} here (``Closed classification of cyclotomic
resonances'' and ``Pairwise resonance and cancellation order'').  ``The
archive'' or ``the package'' means the delivered package, whose code and
data are shipped under the prefixed names listed in
Section~\ref{cyc:sec:verification}.  The manuscript's two appendices are
printed as Sections~\ref{cyc:app:audit} and~\ref{cyc:app:concordance}, so
that Part~I's appendices keep their letters.  The manuscript cites
Krattenthaler's Ramanujan Journal paper through arXiv:2101.04225,
version~5 of 10 September 2021, and points to its Theorem~1, its
confluence discussion and Section~8; that is Part~I's
reference~\cite{Krattenthaler2023}.  Its Barry reference is Part~I's
\cite{Barry2020}, cited as background only.  Bracketed paragraphs
beginning ``Merge note'' are this merge's, not the manuscript's.

\subsection{Notation of Part~III}\label{cyc:sec:notation}
Part~III keeps the manuscript's symbols.  Within
Sections~\ref{cyc:sec:scope}--\ref{cyc:app:concordance} every symbol has
its Part~III meaning, except in merge notes and where a sentence names
Part~I or~II or cites one of their equations.  The single multiplier is
$(x-c)^t$ with one root $c\notin\{0,4\}$; in Part~II's notation this is
$q=\gamma x^m(x-4)^\ell\prod_i(x-c_i)^{t_i}$ with $\gamma=1$, $m=\ell=0$,
$s=1$, $t_1=t$, $c_1=c$, $z_1=z$, and the manuscript's
$\cH Nt(c)$ is Part~II's $H_N((x-c)^t)$ \eqref{rc:eq:HN}.
Table~\ref{cyc:tab:notation} lists the symbols that a reader of Parts~I
and~II is likely to misread, and the notions that need care.  No
symbol was renamed and no normalization changed: the manuscript's rates
$\lambda_k$ are Part~II's $\lambda_{\bk}$ for one root, and its canonical
sector polynomials $P_{t,k}$ have Part~II's normalization
(Section~\ref{cyc:sec:merge}).

   cyclotomic polynomial, Euler's function, the exceptional set
   \eqref{cyc:eq:exceptionalset}
 & Part~II's $M_N$ and $M$; Part~I's $M_{m,\ell}$; $\E$ the shift.\\
\end{longtable}
\endgroup

\subsection{What Part~III answers, and how it corrects the question}\label{cyc:sec:answers}
Part~II's first research question (Section~\ref{cyc:x:rcq1}) asked, for a single repeated root, for the minimal recurrence
``as an explicit function of $t$ and the order of $z^2$'', with a uniform
closed form for $H_N((x-2)^t)$ and an explanation of the parity effects.
Part~III answers it for one repeated root away from the endpoints:
Theorem~\ref{cyc:thm:main} gives every characteristic multiplicity for
$c\ne2$, Theorem~\ref{cyc:thm:central} the central root (a known product
of Cigler, credited), and Theorem~\ref{cyc:thm:rootfree} a root-free
factorization.  It also shows that the question's framing was incomplete:
$t$ and $L=\ord(z^2)$ do \emph{not} determine the answer in odd parity,
and the sign of $z^L$ is a necessary further datum.  The smallest
instance is $t=L=3$, where $c=1$ and $c=3$ have minimal orders $7$ and $6$
(Corollary~\ref{cyc:cor:c-reflect} and \eqref{cyc:eq:c1c3a}--\eqref{cyc:eq:c1c3b}).
The corrected statement of the answer is therefore a function of $t$, $L$
and $z^L\in\{1,-1\}$, and the parity effects are explained by the tied
pair of Lemma~\ref{cyc:lem:tie} and the sign $\rho$ of \eqref{cyc:eq:rho}.
Part~II's second question (``Pairwise resonance and cancellation order'')
is answered only for the tied groups of one repeated root
(Section~\ref{cyc:sec:proofmain}); collisions between distinct roots, and
endpoint factors, remain open (Section~\ref{cyc:sec:future}).

\emph{Consistency with Part~II's data} (an observation of this merge,
checked by a separate exact computation not shipped with the report).
Part~II's \nolinkurl{data/02-root-collisions-resonances.json} records
twelve single-root cases, $c=1,2,3$ with $t=1,\ldots,4$, each with its
minimal order, full characteristic polynomial and initial values.  All
twelve characteristic polynomials, not only the orders, coincide with
those given by Theorems~\ref{cyc:thm:main}, \ref{cyc:thm:central}
and~\ref{cyc:thm:rootfree}, and the orders coincide with the rows $t\le4$
of \nolinkurl{data/03-cyclotomic-orders_c1_c2_c3.csv}: $0$ mismatches.
Seven of the twelve lie below the generic order ($c=1,3$ at $t=3,4$ and
$c=2$ at $t=2,3,4$); the other five ($t=1$ for all three roots, and $t=2$
for $c=1,3$) have $L>t$, so they are nonresonant by
Corollary~\ref{cyc:cor:locus} and have the generic order, although that
file is described as recording resonant parameters.  The case $(x-2)^3$ of
\nolinkurl{data/02-root-collisions-rational_input_examples.json}, with
characteristic polynomial $(X^2+1)^3$, is the case $m=1$ of
\eqref{cyc:eq:centraloddchar}.  The central products at $t=2,4$ are
$B_{1,1}(N)=N+1$ and $B_{2,2}(N)=(N+1)(N+2)^2(N+3)/12$, which are
\eqref{rc:eq:squarecentral} and \eqref{rc:eq:centreproduct}; the
manuscript's residue-class formulas \eqref{cyc:eq:c3residues} and the
table for $c=1$ in Section~\ref{cyc:sec:examples} agree with direct
determinant evaluation.

\subsection{Where the merge had to choose}\label{cyc:sec:merge}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
\item \emph{Placement.}  Part~III is printed after Part~II and before
 Part~I's appendices, so that no section, equation, theorem or table
 number of Parts~I and~II moves (checked against the \texttt{.aux} file of
 a build of the committed text).  Its sections continue the numbering
 (Sections~\ref{cyc:sec:intro} to~\ref{cyc:app:concordance}); the
 manuscript's Sections~1--13 are
 Sections~\ref{cyc:sec:scope}--\ref{cyc:sec:conclusion}, and its
 Appendices~A and~B are Sections~\ref{cyc:app:audit}
 and~\ref{cyc:app:concordance}.
\item \emph{Labels.}  Every label of Part~III carries the prefix
 \texttt{cyc:}; without it, eight of the manuscript's labels,
 \texttt{sec:main}, \texttt{sec:examples}, \texttt{thm:main},
 \texttt{lem:Christoffel}, \texttt{lem:minimal}, \texttt{eq:Christoffel},
 \texttt{eq:lambda} and \texttt{eq:measure}, would have collided with
 Part~I's bare labels.  Two labels were added to Part~II's research subsections so that
 they can be cited; no existing label was changed.
\item \emph{Overlaps with Parts~I and~II.}  The manuscript re-derives,
 without assuming the repository report correct, several things already
 proved there.  Printed with a pointer and kept: the Catalan measure,
 orthogonality and $\det(C_{i+j})=1$ (a third route, after Part~I's
 Section~\ref{sec:coeffproof} and Part~II's Section~\ref{rc:sec:classical});
 the confluent Christoffel identity (Lemma~\ref{cyc:lem:Christoffel} is
 Part~II's \eqref{rc:eq:Christoffel} for $q=(x-c)^t$, and Part~I's
 Lemma~\ref{lem:Christoffel} and Proposition~\ref{prop:confluent} are
 other special cases); the minimal-annihilator lemma
 (Lemma~\ref{cyc:lem:minimal} restates Part~I's Lemma~\ref{lem:minimal} and
 Part~II's Lemma~\ref{rc:lem:annihilator}, with a third proof and the
 generating-function statement); the exact sector degree $k(t-k)$ and the
 generic order (Part~II's Theorem~\ref{rc:thm:main} and

 order-fifteen example (the table before \eqref{rc:eq:chi15}, at $z=2$)
 and the two constants of \eqref{rc:eq:squarecentral}: the two sector
 normalizations agree.  No statement of Parts~I or~II was changed.
\item \emph{A third route to half of the central product} (an observation
 of this merge).  The neighbouring report
 \nolinkurl{cigler-conjecture-8-schur} (same directory) proves, for
 Cigler's polynomials $b_m(t)$ with $b_m(1)=\binom m{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}$,
 an even-shift Hankel--Schur identity (its Theorem~3.1) and, at $t=1$, the
 dimension product $\prod_{i,j\le k}(n+i+j-1)/(i+j-1)$ (its
 Proposition~7.2).  Together they give $D_{2k}(n)=B_{k,k}(n)$, the even case
 of Cigler's product used in Section~\ref{cyc:sec:central}, by a
 Schur-function route; the odd case is not covered there.  That report and
 its two siblings concern conjectures of the same Cigler
 paper~\cite{Cigler2021}; Part~III uses its proved Theorem~1, not a

\section{The repository question and the scope of the answer}\label{cyc:sec:scope}

The relevant ProveIt package is \emph{Shifted Catalan Hankel
Polynomials}, whose Part~II incorporates \emph{Root Collisions and
Sharp Recurrences for Polynomially Weighted Catalan Hankel
Determinants} (Parts~I and~II above; the manuscript's bibliography
entry for this repository report is replaced by
Section~\ref{cyc:sec:source}).  The selected source was checked against
repository commit
\begin{center}\small\texttt{9754e83603e223811b7515eb892f22c847144593}.\end{center}
Its directory is
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright
SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/hankel-determinants/\allowbreak
catalan-and-ballot/\allowbreak shifted-catalan-hankel-polynomials/.
\end{quote}
The combined \texttt{article.tex} has Git blob identifier
\texttt{b29ea42fc5bba69d23c3b036bee25357e202f356}.

For a single nonendpoint root of multiplicity $t$, that report gives a
canonical expansion into $t+1$ exponential sectors, of respective
polynomial degrees $k(t-k)$ for $0\le k\le t$.  Distinct exponential
rates therefore give the exact order
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:generic}
 \Rgen(t)=\sum_{k=0}^{t}\bigl(k(t-k)+1\bigr)
         =t+1+\binom{t+1}{3}.
\end{equation}
At resonances it provides a finite exact algorithm, but its research
agenda asks for a structural classification when $z$ is a root of
unity.  It also asks for a uniform central-root formula and an
explanation of parity, beyond its separately certified examples at
multiplicities two and four.  These are the local research questions
addressed here.

\noindent[Merge note: in this report the canonical expansion and its
degrees are Part~II's Theorem~\ref{rc:thm:main}, the generic order
\eqref{cyc:eq:generic} is its Corollary~\ref{rc:cor:single}, the finite
exact algorithm is Theorems~\ref{rc:thm:resonant} and~\ref{rc:thm:finite},
the research agenda is Section~\ref{rc:sec:research}, and the certified
central examples at multiplicities two and four are
\eqref{rc:eq:squarecentral} and \eqref{rc:eq:centreproduct}.]

There are two distinct conclusions.  First, the noncentral cyclotomic
problem admits a closed answer: at most one collided rate has a
leading cancellation, and that cancellation loses exactly one
multiplicity.  Second, the suggested input ``multiplicity and order of
$z^2$'' is insufficient in odd parity.  The sign of $z^L$ is necessary.
Thus the result both completes a classification and corrects the
parameter list needed to state it.

\subsection{What is already known}
The Christoffel determinant formula, its confluent version, and general
constant-coefficient recurrences for polynomially modified Catalan
moments are classical.  Krattenthaler~\cite{Krattenthaler2023} treats these
identities and, in Section~8, gives the familiar order-at-most-$2^t$
recurrence construction.  Barry's note~\cite{Barry2020} is part of the
background for the repository's shifted-linear-multiplier work.
Neither rationality alone nor the existence of an exponential expansion
is a novelty claim here.

The central product used in Section~\ref{cyc:sec:central} is also not new.
Cigler's Theorem~1, equation~(23), in~\cite{Cigler2021} evaluates the
shifted Hankel determinants of $\binom n{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}$.
A translation and a row--column sign change give exactly the
$c=2$ product required here.  His proof also uses condensation.
Recognizing this prior result closes the repository's central subproblem
without assigning it new priority.

The additional content is the explicit first centered sector
coefficient, the noncentral one-defect theorem, the minimal
characteristic polynomial at every cyclotomic specialization, and the
resulting sign-sensitive order laws.  These statements are not present
in the selected repository source.  A broader literature equivalence
in different orthogonal-polynomial or symmetric-function notation has
not been ruled out.  The comparison is not a proof audit of every report
in ProveIt.

\subsection{Why the problem is not solved by identifying equal rates}
An exponential polynomial
$\sum_k P_k(N)\lambda_k^N$ must first be grouped by equal numerical
values of $\lambda_k$.  The largest degree within one group is only an
upper bound for the degree of its sum.  Equal leading coefficients can
cancel, and symmetry can make the whole sum zero.  The prior report's
central square already illustrates the latter phenomenon
[\eqref{rc:eq:squarecentral}].

The main proof computes one more coefficient than the degree law.
That additional coefficient is sufficient everywhere except at the
single central value $c=2$.  This is what turns a finite procedure for
each example into a uniform classification.

\section{Definitions and the complete classification}\label{cyc:sec:main}

Write $\NN=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$ and
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:Hdef}
 C_n=\frac1{n+1}\binom{2n}{n},\qquad
 \cH Nt(c)=\det_{0\le i,j<N}
 \left(\sum_{a=0}^{t}\binom ta(-c)^{t-a}C_{i+j+a}\right),
 \qquad \cH0t(c)=1.
\end{equation}
Throughout the main classification, $t\ge1$ and $c\in\CC\setminus\{0,4\}$.
Choose a root $z$ of
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:zparam}
 z+z^{-1}=c-2.
\end{equation}
Then $z\ne0,1,-1$.  The two choices are reciprocal and give the same
answers after relabeling.

A monic polynomial $g(X)=\sum_{j=0}^{R}g_jX^j$ is an annihilator when
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:annihilate}
 \sum_{j=0}^{R}g_j\cH{N+j}t(c)=0\qquad\text{for every }N\ge0.
\end{equation}
The least possible degree is denoted $R(t,c)$, and the monic polynomial
of that degree by $\chi_{t,c}$.  All characteristic roots occurring below
are nonzero; consequently the same minimal polynomial is necessary even
for recurrences required only after a finite initial segment.

Let $L=\ord(z^2)\in\{2,3,\ldots\}\cup\{\infty\}$.  Define
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:lambda}
 \lambda_k=(-1)^t z^{2k-t}\qquad(0\le k\le t).
\end{equation}
If $L<\infty$, equality of these rates means exactly equality of the
indices modulo $L$.

\begin{theorem}[Minimal recurrence at every noncentral parameter]
\label{cyc:thm:main}
Assume $c\notin\{0,2,4\}$.

If $L>t$, including $L=\infty$, then
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:nonreschar}
 \chi_{t,c}(X)=\prod_{k=0}^{t}(X-\lambda_k)^{k(t-k)+1},
 \qquad R(t,c)=\Rgen(t).
\end{equation}

If $3\le L\le t$, define, for $0\le r<L$,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:resclasses}
 I_r=\{k:0\le k\le t,\ k\equiv r\pmod L\},\qquad
 d_r=\max_{k\in I_r}k(t-k),\qquad \eta=\ind_{t\equiv L\pmod2}.
\end{equation}
When $\eta=1$, also put
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:tieparams}
 k_-=(t-L)/2,\qquad k_+=(t+L)/2,\qquad r_*\equiv k_-\pmod L,
 \quad 0\le r_*<L,
\end{equation}
and set
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:defect}
 \delta=
 \begin{cases}
  1,&\eta=1\ \text{and }(-1)^{L(t+1)/2}z^{Lt}=-1,\\
  0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
The exponent $L(t+1)/2$ is an integer whenever it is used.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:mainchar}
 \boxed{\quad
 \chi_{t,c}(X)=\prod_{r=0}^{L-1}(X-\lambda_r)^{e_r},\qquad
 e_r=d_r+1-\delta\ind_{r=r_*}.
 \quad}
\end{equation}
When $\eta=0$, the subtracted term means zero and no $r_*$ is needed.
A factor with exponent zero is omitted.  In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:mainorder}
 \boxed{\qquad
 R(t,c)=\frac{L(3t^2-L^2+13-3\eta)}{12}-\delta.
 \qquad}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[The meaning of one defect]\label{cyc:rem:onedefect}
The baseline in~\eqref{cyc:eq:mainorder} is
$\Rgrp=\sum_r(d_r+1)$, not the larger generic order
$\Rgen(t)$.  Merging many rates can already cause a large decrease
from $\Rgen$.  The theorem says that \emph{further cancellation after
this grouping} decreases the order by either zero or one.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[An elementary version of the defect test]\label{cyc:cor:sign}
In the resonant range $3\le L\le t$, the defect is one exactly in one
of the following two disjoint cases:
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:signcases}
 \begin{aligned}
 &t,L\text{ even},\qquad L\equiv2\pmod4;\\
 &t,L\text{ odd},\qquad z^L=(-1)^{(t-1)/2}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
There is no defect when $t$ and $L$ have different parity.
\end{corollary}

The proof of the theorem occupies Sections~\ref{cyc:sec:orthogonal}--\ref{cyc:sec:orders}.
The remaining parameter is governed by a known product.

\begin{theorem}[Central root; Cigler's product with Catalan normalization]
\label{cyc:thm:central}
For $a,b\in\NN$ put
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:rectangle}
 B_{a,b}(N)=\prod_{i=1}^{a}\prod_{j=1}^{b}
                    \frac{N+i+j-1}{i+j-1}.
\end{equation}
Every empty product is one.  For $m\in\NN$,
\begin{align}
 \cH N{2m}(2)&=B_{m,m}(N),\label{cyc:eq:centraleven}\\
 \cH N{2m+1}(2)&=(-1)^{N(N+1)/2}B_{m,m+1}(N).
 \label{cyc:eq:centralodd}
\end{align}
Their minimal polynomials are
\begin{align}
 \chi_{2m,2}(X)&=(X-1)^{m^2+1},\label{cyc:eq:centralevenchar}\\
 \chi_{2m+1,2}(X)&=(X^2+1)^{m(m+1)+1}.
 \label{cyc:eq:centraloddchar}
\end{align}
The even formula includes the trivial multiplier $t=0$.
\end{theorem}

Together these results classify every single repeated nonendpoint
root, not only rational roots or a bounded list of multiplicities.
The endpoints $c=0,4$ are intentionally outside the theorem; they
require endpoint jets and have different triangular degree laws.

\subsection{Immediate generating-function and scaling consequences}
If the distinct characteristic roots and their exponents are
$(\lambda_r,e_r)$, the reduced denominator of
$\sum_{N\ge0}\cH Nt(c)u^N$ is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:GFden}
 \prod_r(1-\lambda_r u)^{e_r}.
\end{equation}
There is a numerator of degree less than $R(t,c)$ and constant term one.
Thus the theorem determines all poles and their exact orders as well
as the recurrence.  For the multiplier $\gamma(x-c)^t$, $\gamma\ne0$,
all determinants are multiplied by $\gamma^N$.  Its monic minimal
polynomial is $\gamma^{R(t,c)}\chi_{t,c}(X/\gamma)$.

\section{Catalan orthogonality and the confluent determinant}\label{cyc:sec:orthogonal}

We derive the needed determinant identity rather than assuming the
correctness of an unrefereed repository lemma.  These are classical
arguments; their role here is normalization and self-containment.

\subsection{A convenient monic orthonormal family}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:measure}
 \dd\mu(x)=\frac1{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{4-x}{x}}\,\dd x\qquad(0<x<4).
\end{equation}
Its moments are $C_n$.  For example, the substitution $x=4v$
gives a beta integral; integration by parts gives successive moment
ratio $2(2n+1)/(n+2)$, with zeroth moment one, exactly the Catalan ratio.

Let
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:prec}
 p_0(x)=1,\quad p_1(x)=x-1,\quad
 p_{n+1}(x)=(x-2)p_n(x)-p_{n-1}(x)\quad(n\ge1).
\end{equation}
All $p_n$ are monic.  At $x=2+2\cos\theta$, $0<\theta<\pi$,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:ptrig}
 p_n(x)=\frac{\sin((n+\tfrac12)\theta)}{\sin(\theta/2)},\qquad
 \dd\mu=\frac2\pi\sin^2(\theta/2)\,\dd\theta,
\end{equation}
where the orientation of the integral is reversed as necessary.
The elementary sine orthogonality integral therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:orthonormal}
 \int_0^4p_n(x)p_m(x)\,\dd\mu(x)=\ind_{n=m}.
\end{equation}
A triangular monic change of polynomial basis has determinant one.
Consequently the unweighted size-$N$ Catalan Hankel determinant is one.

\subsection{The determinant formula and its sign}
For variables $x_1,\ldots,x_N$ set
$\Delta_N(x)=\prod_{i<j}(x_j-x_i)$.
Expansion of two determinants and integration term by term gives
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:andreief}
 \det\left(\int x^{i+j}q(x)\,\dd\mu(x)\right)_{0\le i,j<N}
 =\frac1{N!}\int\Delta_N(x)^2\prod_{j=1}^{N}q(x_j)\,\dd\mu(x_j).
\end{equation}
This is the usual Andr\'eief identity in the particular form needed here.
It follows directly because the double sum over permutations contains
$N!$ equal copies of the determinant on the left.

Take distinct $a_1,\ldots,a_t$ and $q(x)=\prod_{i=1}^t(x-a_i)$.
The mixed Vandermonde obeys
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:mixedV}
 \Delta_{N+t}(x,a)=\Delta_N(x)\Delta_t(a)
                   \prod_{j=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{t}(a_i-x_j).
\end{equation}
Replace its monomial columns by the monic $p_0,\ldots,p_{N+t-1}$
without changing the determinant.  In its Laplace expansion along the
first $N$ rows, integrate against
$\det(p_{i-1}(x_j))_{j,i=1}^N$.  Orthogonality kills every choice of
$N$ columns other than $p_0,\ldots,p_{N-1}$.  That choice contributes
$N!$ times the remaining evaluation determinant, with positive Laplace
sign.  Combining~\eqref{cyc:eq:andreief} and~\eqref{cyc:eq:mixedV} yields
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:distinctChristoffel}
 \det\left(\int x^{i+j}\prod_{u=1}^t(x-a_u)\,\dd\mu(x)\right)
 =(-1)^{Nt}\frac{\det(p_{N+j}(a_i))_{1\le i\le t,\ 0\le j<t}}
                         {\Delta_t(a)}.
\end{equation}
The sign is caused by $x-a$ in the multiplier and $a-x$ in the mixed
Vandermonde.  The left side is polynomial in all $a_i$, so the quotient
has a well-defined confluent limit.

\begin{lemma}[Confluent Christoffel identity]\label{cyc:lem:Christoffel}
For every $N,t\in\NN$ and every $c\in\CC$,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:Christoffel}
 \cH Nt(c)=(-1)^{Nt}
       \det_{0\le r,j<t}\left(\frac{p_{N+j}^{(r)}(c)}{r!}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
In~\eqref{cyc:eq:distinctChristoffel}, let $a_i=c+\varepsilon_i$ and expand
each evaluation row by Taylor's formula.  The least possible distinct
Taylor orders are $0,1,\ldots,t-1$.  Their determinant is
$\Delta_t(\varepsilon)$ times the determinant of the normalized
jets $p_{N+j}^{(r)}(c)/r!$.  All higher Taylor terms vanish after division
by the Vandermonde and passage to the common limit.  This proves the
formula, including the empty determinant cases.
\end{proof}

No division by $\cH Nt(c)$ occurred.  Some of those weighted
determinants can be zero; the proof and the classification remain valid.

\noindent[Merge note: Lemma~\ref{cyc:lem:Christoffel} is Part~II's
\eqref{rc:eq:Christoffel} for $q=(x-c)^t$, derived here a second time;
Part~I's Lemma~\ref{lem:Christoffel} and Proposition~\ref{prop:confluent}
are further special cases, and the measure and orthogonality above repeat
Part~I's Section~\ref{sec:coeffproof} and Part~II's
Section~\ref{rc:sec:classical}.  The recursion \eqref{cyc:eq:prec} is
Part~II's \eqref{rc:eq:prec}.  In \eqref{cyc:eq:distinctChristoffel} the
manuscript prints the multiplier as $\prod_{a=1}^t(x-a_a)$; the product
index is written $u$ here (as in Part~II), since $i$ is a row index.]

\section{An exact subset expansion for every sector}\label{cyc:sec:sectors}

Fix $t\ge1$ and abbreviate
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:TK}
 T=\binom t2,\qquad A_u=\prod_{j=0}^{u-1}j!,\qquad
 K_t(z)=\frac{(z/(z^2-1))^T}{(z-1)^tA_t}.
\end{equation}
For a subset $S\subseteq J:=\{0,\ldots,t-1\}$ define
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:subsetnotation}
 \sigma(S)=\sum_{j\in S}j,\qquad
 \Delta(S)=\prod_{\substack{i,j\in S\\i<j}}(j-i),\qquad S^c=J\setminus S.
\end{equation}
Empty products equal one.

\begin{theorem}[Canonical sector formula]\label{cyc:thm:subset}
For $0\le k\le t$, put $h=t-k$ and define
\begin{align}
 F_{t,k}(N;z)
 &=\sum_{\substack{S\subseteq J\\|S|=k}}
 (-1)^{T+h-\sigma(S)}z^{2\sigma(S)+k-T}\Delta(S)\Delta(S^c)
 \prod_{\substack{i\in S\\j\in S^c}}(2N+i+j+1),
 \label{cyc:eq:rawsector}\\
 P_{t,k}(N;z)&=K_t(z)F_{t,k}(N;z).
 \label{cyc:eq:Psector}
\end{align}
Then the all-index identity
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:sectorH}
 \cH Nt(c)=\sum_{k=0}^{t}P_{t,k}(N;z)\lambda_k^N\qquad(N\ge0)
\end{equation}
holds.  Each $P_{t,k}$ is polynomial in $N$ of degree at most $k(t-k)$.
The definition remains meaningful when the numerical rates coincide.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Locally write $z=e^u$ and $x=2+2\cosh u$.  Formula~\eqref{cyc:eq:prec} gives
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:pexp}
 p_n(x)=\frac{z^{n+1}-z^{-n}}{z-1}
 =g(u)\bigl(e^{(n+1/2)u}-e^{-(n+1/2)u}\bigr),\qquad
 g(u)=\frac1{z^{1/2}-z^{-1/2}}.
\end{equation}
Also $\dd u/\dd x=z/(z^2-1)$.  For a Wronskian of $t$ functions,
change of variable multiplies its determinant by $(\dd u/\dd x)^T$:
row $r$ has leading derivative term $(\dd u/\dd x)^r\partial_u^r$,
and all other terms are combinations of earlier rows.  A common
factor $g$ in all functions similarly multiplies the Wronskian by
$g^t$, by the Leibniz rule and a triangular row transformation.
The normalization of row $r$ by $r!$ contributes $A_t^{-1}$.

Set $v_j=N+j+\tfrac12$.  After these row transformations the
Wronskian in~\eqref{cyc:eq:Christoffel} has columns
\[
 \bigl(v_j^r e^{v_ju}-(-v_j)^r e^{-v_ju}\bigr)_{r=0}^{t-1}.
\]
Expand each column by its two summands.  Let $S$ be the plus columns
and $S^c$ the minus columns.  The exponential factor is
$z^{(2k-t)N+2\sigma(S)-T+(2k-t)/2}$.
The remaining determinant is the Vandermonde in the signed $v_j$.
Within each sign block its magnitude is $\Delta(S)\Delta(S^c)$;
between the blocks it contributes
$\prod_{i\in S,j\in S^c}(v_i+v_j)$.

There are $h$ signs from choosing the minus summand, $\binom h2$
from pairs of minus columns, and one for each plus column preceding a
minus column.  The number of the latter pairs is
$k(t-1)-\sigma(S)-\binom k2$.  Their total is exactly
$T+h-\sigma(S)$.  Finally $g(u)^t=z^{t/2}/(z-1)^t$ cancels all
half-integral powers, leaving $z^{2\sigma(S)+k-T}$ outside
$z^{(2k-t)N}$.  The sign $(-1)^{Nt}$ in the Christoffel identity
is absorbed into $\lambda_k^N$.
This proves~\eqref{cyc:eq:rawsector}--\eqref{cyc:eq:sectorH}.
There are $kh$ linear factors in each product, giving the stated bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[No branch choice in the final formula]
The auxiliary local logarithm and square root in the proof disappear
from~\eqref{cyc:eq:rawsector}.  Only integer powers of $z$ remain.
Since the result is a rational identity in $z$, valid off $0,\pm1$,
no global logarithm or continuation convention is required.
\end{remark}

This expression is useful computationally: $F_{t,k}$ has Laurent
polynomial coefficients in $z$ with integer coefficients.  In a
cyclotomic integer ring it can be evaluated without any division.
The common nonzero factor $K_t(z)$ has no effect on degrees or on
cancellation among sectors.

\section{The first two sector coefficients and reflection}\label{cyc:sec:coefficients}

The exact degree law alone is inherited from the preceding repository
work.  The additional coefficient calculated here distinguishes a
one-degree loss from an arbitrarily long cancellation.
[Merge note: the degree law is Part~II's Theorem~\ref{rc:thm:main};
Theorem~\ref{cyc:thm:two} below re-proves it for one root, and its
leading coefficient \eqref{cyc:eq:ell} is the one-root case of Part~II's
Theorem~\ref{rc:thm:leading} (Section~\ref{cyc:sec:merge}).]

\subsection{A signed finite Vandermonde identity}
\begin{lemma}\label{cyc:lem:W}
For $h=t-k$ and an indeterminate $q$,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:W}
 W_{t,k}(q):=
 \sum_{\substack{S\subseteq J\\|S|=k}}
 (-1)^{\sigma(S)}\Delta(S)\Delta(S^c)q^{\sigma(S)}
 =(-1)^{\binom k2}A_kA_h q^{\binom k2}(1-q)^{kh}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Take the $t\times t$ matrix with rows indexed by $j=0,\ldots,t-1$.
Its first $k$ columns are $j^a q^j$, $0\le a<k$, and its last $h$
columns are $j^b$, $0\le b<h$.  The convention $j^0=1$ includes $j=0$.
Laplace expansion along the first $k$ columns gives the left side
of~\eqref{cyc:eq:W} multiplied by $(-1)^{\binom k2}$.

To compute the same determinant, start with columns of derivatives
of $w^j$ at the two nodes $q,1$.  The ordinary confluent Vandermonde
is $A_kA_h(1-q)^{kh}$.  Replacing $\partial_w^a$ by
$(w\partial_w)^a$ is triangular in the derivative order and multiplies
the determinant by $q^{0+1+\cdots+(k-1)}$ in the first block and one
in the second.  Its columns are exactly those of our matrix.
Equating the two evaluations proves the identity.
\end{proof}

For $q\ne0,1$, logarithmic differentiation gives
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:Wmean}
 \frac{qW_{t,k}'(q)}{W_{t,k}(q)}
 =\binom k2-\frac{khq}{1-q}.
\end{equation}
This is an algebraic weighted mean of $\sigma(S)$, not a positivity
or probability assertion.  The weights have signs and can be complex.

\subsection{Leading coefficient and the decisive correction}
\begin{theorem}[Exact degree and first centered correction]\label{cyc:thm:two}
Let $h=t-k$, $d=kh$, and $w=N+t/2$.  The leading coefficient of
$P_{t,k}$ is
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:ell}
 \ell_{t,k}(z)=
 (-1)^{h(h+1)/2}
 \frac{2^{kh}A_kA_h z^{k^2}}
 {A_t(z-1)^t(z^2-1)^{\binom k2+\binom h2}},
 \qquad \ell_{t,k}(z)\ne0.
\end{equation}
In particular $\deg_N P_{t,k}=kh$.  If $d\ge1$, then
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:twocoeffs}
 P_{t,k}(N;z)=\ell_{t,k}(z)
 \left(w^d+\frac{kh(h-k)(z^2+1)}{4(z^2-1)}w^{d-1}\right)
       +Q_{t,k}(w),
 \qquad \deg Q_{t,k}\le d-2.
\end{equation}
A polynomial of degree at most $-1$ means zero.  If $d=0$, the sector
is the constant $\ell_{t,k}(z)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Every product in~\eqref{cyc:eq:rawsector} has leading coefficient $2^d$.
Its weighted sum is, by~\eqref{cyc:eq:W},
\[
 [N^d]F_{t,k}
 =(-1)^{T+h+\binom k2}2^d A_kA_h
       z^{k^2-T}(1-z^2)^d.
\]
Multiply by $K_t(z)$.  Since
$T=\binom k2+\binom h2+kh$, collecting the signs and powers gives
exactly~\eqref{cyc:eq:ell}.  All factors are nonzero under the standing
hypotheses, proving the exact degree.

For the next coefficient, rewrite the mixed factor as
\[
 2N+i+j+1=2w+i+j+1-t.
\]
For a fixed subset $S$, the sum of its $d$ offsets equals
\begin{align}
 B(S)&=\sum_{i\in S,j\in S^c}(i+j+1-t)\notag\\
     &=(h-k)\sigma(S)+kT-kh(t-1).
 \label{cyc:eq:offsetsum}
\end{align}
Indeed $\sum_{j\in S^c}j=T-\sigma(S)$, which proves the second line
by direct summation.  The relative coefficient of $w^{d-1}$ in this
product is $B(S)/2$.

The weighted sum uses the same weights as $W_{t,k}(z^2)$.
By~\eqref{cyc:eq:Wmean}, its mean offset is
\begin{align}
 \overline B
 &=(h-k)\left(\binom k2-\frac{khq}{1-q}\right)
       +kT-kh(t-1)\notag\\
 &=\frac{kh(h-k)(q+1)}{2(q-1)},\qquad q=z^2.
 \label{cyc:eq:Bmean}
\end{align}
Dividing by two gives the coefficient in~\eqref{cyc:eq:twocoeffs}.
Only lower powers remain, proving the assertion.
\end{proof}

It is useful to isolate the correction as
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:b}
 b_{t,k}(z)=\frac{k(t-k)(t-2k)(z^2+1)}{4(z^2-1)}.
\end{equation}
Then $b_{t,t-k}=-b_{t,k}$.  Except for the trivial factors
$k=0,t$ or $k=t/2$, its only zero away from the endpoints is
$z^2=-1$, namely $c=2$.

\subsection{An exact reflection of the whole sector polynomial}
\begin{proposition}[Centered sector reflection]\label{cyc:prop:reflection}
For every $0\le k\le t$, with $h=t-k$,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:reflection}
 P_{t,h}(N;z)=(-1)^{t(t+1)/2}z^{(h-k)t}P_{t,k}(-N-t;z).
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:ellratio}
 \frac{\ell_{t,h}(z)}{\ell_{t,k}(z)}
 =(-1)^{(h-k)(t+1)/2}z^{(h-k)t}.
\end{equation}
The exponent in the sign is always an integer.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
In the subset formula replace $S$ by the complement of
$\{t-1-j:j\in S\}$.  This new subset has size $h$ and sum
$T-k(t-1)+\sigma(S)$.  Its two Vandermonde magnitudes are unchanged,
its power of $z$ gains $(h-k)t$, and replacing $N$ by $-N-t$
changes the sign of every one of the $kh$ mixed linear factors.
The remaining sign simplifies to $(-1)^{t(t+1)/2}$.
This is a termwise bijection of the two sums and proves
\eqref{cyc:eq:reflection}.  Alternatively the same reflection comes from
$p_{-n-1}=-p_n$ and reversal of the columns of the Wronskian.

Comparison of the leading coefficients gives
$(-1)^{t(t+1)/2+kh}z^{(h-k)t}$.  Its sign equals the one
in~\eqref{cyc:eq:ellratio}; the difference of the two exponents is even.
One can also obtain~\eqref{cyc:eq:ellratio} immediately by dividing the
products in~\eqref{cyc:eq:ell}.
\end{proof}

The center $-t/2$ is therefore natural, rather than a convenient
numerical fit.  At a colliding symmetric pair, the two leading terms
are either equal or opposite, and the first centered corrections have
opposite relative signs.  That is the algebraic source of the
classification.

\section{Why the noncentral cancellation has exactly one defect}\label{cyc:sec:proofmain}

\subsection{Minimal annihilators of exponential polynomials}
\begin{lemma}\label{cyc:lem:minimal}
Suppose $f(N)=\sum_{j=1}^{b}Q_j(N)\alpha_j^N$, where the
$\alpha_j$ are distinct and nonzero and each $Q_j$ is a nonzero
polynomial of degree $d_j$.  The minimal annihilator of $f$ is
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:minlemma}
 \prod_{j=1}^{b}(X-\alpha_j)^{d_j+1}.
\end{equation}
The same conclusion holds for eventual annihilators.  Its generating
function has reduced denominator $\prod_j(1-\alpha_j u)^{d_j+1}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
On $Q(N)\alpha^N$, the operator $\E-\alpha$, where
$(\E f)(N)=f(N+1)$, gives
$\alpha^{N+1}(Q(N+1)-Q(N))$.  It lowers the polynomial degree by
exactly one until the term becomes zero.  In contrast, $\E-\beta$
with $\beta\ne\alpha$ preserves the degree and has a nonzero leading
coefficient.  Distinct exponential blocks are independent: applying
the annihilators of all but one block isolates a nonzero polynomial
multiple of the remaining exponential.  A nonzero polynomial cannot
vanish at every sufficiently large integer.  It follows that any
annihilator, even an eventual one, must contain every factor in
\eqref{cyc:eq:minlemma} to the displayed multiplicity.  Their product
clearly suffices.

For generating functions, $(u\,\dd/\dd u)^a(1-\alpha u)^{-1}$
is the series of $N^a\alpha^N$.  A polynomial multiplier of degree
$d$ therefore gives a pole of exact order $d+1$ at $u=\alpha^{-1}$.
Distinct poles cannot cancel.  This proves the denominator assertion.
\end{proof}

\noindent[Merge note: Lemma~\ref{cyc:lem:minimal} restates Part~I's
Lemma~\ref{lem:minimal} and Part~II's Lemma~\ref{rc:lem:annihilator};
the manuscript's proof is kept as a third proof.]

The nonresonant case of Theorem~\ref{cyc:thm:main} now follows from
Theorems~\ref{cyc:thm:subset} and~\ref{cyc:thm:two}.  It remains to determine
the degree of each grouped polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:groupP}
 G_r(N)=\sum_{k\in I_r}P_{t,k}(N;z).
\end{equation}

\subsection{The geometry of a residue class}
\begin{lemma}[The unique possible top tie]\label{cyc:lem:tie}
Assume $2\le L\le t$.  In every class $I_r$, the maximum of
$k(t-k)$ has a unique maximizer except possibly in one class.
A tied class exists exactly when $t\equiv L\pmod2$.  Its two
maximizers are $k_-,k_+$ from~\eqref{cyc:eq:tieparams}; they are consecutive
members of that class, separated by $L$, and
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:tiedegree}
 d_*:=k_-(t-k_-)=k_+ (t-k_+)=(t^2-L^2)/4.
\end{equation}
Any other index in that class has degree at most $d_*-2L^2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The identity
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:parabola}
 k(t-k)=\frac{t^2-(2k-t)^2}{4}
\end{equation}
shows that one must choose the members of the arithmetic progression
$I_r$ nearest $t/2$.  There are at most two.  A tie requires two
consecutive members to be symmetric about $t/2$, so their difference
is $L$ and their sum is $t$.  They are consequently the two indices
in~\eqref{cyc:eq:tieparams}, and exist exactly under the parity condition.
Their values $2k-t$ are $-L,L$.  Any further index in that same class
has $|2k-t|\ge3L$, so~\eqref{cyc:eq:parabola} decreases its degree by at
least $((3L)^2-L^2)/4=2L^2$.
\end{proof}

A group with a unique maximizer has degree $d_r$, since its unique
leading coefficient is nonzero by~\eqref{cyc:eq:ell}.  Thus the only
remaining issue is the one tied group.

\subsection{The tied group and the nonvanishing next term}
Assume the parity condition and use $k=k_-$, $h=k_+$, so $h-k=L$.
By~\eqref{cyc:eq:ellratio}, the ratio of their leading coefficients is
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:rho}
 \rho=(-1)^{L(t+1)/2}z^{Lt}.
\end{equation}
Since $(z^L)^2=1$, this is either $1$ or $-1$.  When $\rho=1$,
the leading coefficient of $G_{r_*}$ is $2\ell_{t,k}\ne0$ and
there is no defect.

Suppose $\rho=-1$.  If $d_*=0$, then $t=L$, the two indices are
$0,t$, and they are the only indices in their class.  Their sectors
are opposite constants, so $G_{r_*}=0$.  The class contributes no
characteristic factor rather than the naive exponent one.

If $d_*>0$, apply~\eqref{cyc:eq:twocoeffs} in the centered variable
$w=N+t/2$.  The leading terms cancel, while the coefficient of
$w^{d_*-1}$ in their sum is
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:nextsurvives}
 \ell_{t,k}b_{t,k}+(-\ell_{t,k})(-b_{t,k})
 =\frac{\ell_{t,k}\,d_*L(z^2+1)}{2(z^2-1)}.
\end{equation}
It is nonzero when $c\ne2$.  The lower members of the same residue
class cannot affect this coefficient, by Lemma~\ref{cyc:lem:tie}.
Therefore $\deg G_{r_*}=d_*-1$ exactly.

We have now proved all the exponents in~\eqref{cyc:eq:mainchar}, including
the disappearing constant case.  Applying Lemma~\ref{cyc:lem:minimal}
proves their minimality and the generating-function assertion.  The
total order formula will follow from a finite sum of squares.

\begin{corollary}[Only a real characteristic root can lose multiplicity]
\label{cyc:cor:realdefect}
Whenever the noncentral defect occurs, its characteristic root is
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:defectroot}
 \lambda_*=(-1)^t z^{-L}=(-1)^t z^L\in\{1,-1\}.
\end{equation}
No other characteristic root loses multiplicity beyond grouping.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Substitute $2k_--t=-L$ into~\eqref{cyc:eq:lambda} and use $z^{2L}=1$.
The degree proof above excludes every other group.
\end{proof}

\section{Closed orders, exceptional parameters, and reflection}\label{cyc:sec:orders}

\subsection{Summing the grouped degrees}
For each residue $r$, select a nearest centered value
$a_r=2k-t$ at a maximizer.  In a tie either choice gives the same square.
When $t$ and $L$ have different parity, their squares are the squares
of the complete list
\[
 -(L-1),\;-(L-3),\;\ldots,\;L-3,\;L-1.
\]
When the parities agree, they are the squares of
\[
 -L,\;-L+2,\;\ldots,\;L-4,\;L-2.
\]
The finite interval $[-t,t]$ contains all chosen representatives
because $t\ge L$.  Summing squares in these arithmetic progressions
gives
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:squares}
 \sum_{r=0}^{L-1}a_r^2=\frac{L(L^2-1)}3+L\eta.
\end{equation}
Using~\eqref{cyc:eq:parabola},
\begin{align}
 \Rgrp(t,L)
 &=\sum_{r=0}^{L-1}(d_r+1)
  =L+\frac{Lt^2-\sum_r a_r^2}{4}\notag\\
 &=\frac{L(3t^2-L^2+13-3\eta)}{12}.
 \label{cyc:eq:grouporder}
\end{align}
The loss established in the preceding section is exactly $\delta$,
proving~\eqref{cyc:eq:mainorder}.

For completeness, Corollary~\ref{cyc:cor:sign} follows without any complex
arithmetic.  If $t,L$ are even, $z^{Lt}=1$ and the parity of
$L(t+1)/2$ is that of $L/2$.  If both are odd, $z^{Lt}=z^L$ and
the condition $\rho=-1$ is equivalent to
$z^L=(-1)^{(t-1)/2}$.  This proves all the sign cases.

\subsection{An exact finite exceptional locus}
\begin{corollary}[Every nongeneric nonendpoint parameter]\label{cyc:cor:locus}
For fixed $t\ge1$, the parameters $c\ne0,4$ at which
$R(t,c)<\Rgen(t)$ are exactly
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:exceptionalset}
 \mathcal E_t=
 \left\{2+2\cos\frac{\pi a}{L}:
       2\le L\le t,\quad 1\le a<L,\quad \gcd(a,L)=1\right\}.
\end{equation}
In particular all exceptional parameters are real and lie in $(0,4)$,
and
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:exceptionalcount}
 |\mathcal E_t|=\sum_{L=2}^{t}\varphi(L),
\end{equation}
where $\varphi(L)$ is the number of integers $1\le a<L$ coprime to $L$.
At the parameter represented by $a/L$, one has $z^L=(-1)^a$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A collision of two rates means $z^{2(k-j)}=1$ with
$1\le|k-j|\le t$.  This is equivalent to finite $L\le t$.
Then $z$ is a root of unity, so $c=2+z+z^{-1}$ is real in $(0,4)$.
Choose the reciprocal of $z$ as needed to write
$z=e^{\pi ia/L}$ with $1\le a<L$ and $\gcd(a,L)=1$.
Conversely any such parameter has exactly the stated order $L$.

At a collision, grouping replaces at least two positive contributions
$d_k+1$ by at most their maximum, strictly decreasing the total generic
order.  Further cancellations cannot undo that decrease.  In the
absence of collisions, the nonzero leading coefficients give the
full generic order, including $t=1,c=2$.
Finally $\cos\theta$ is injective on $(0,\pi)$, and the fractions
$a/L$ in~\eqref{cyc:eq:exceptionalset} are reduced.  No two listed
parameters coincide.  Counting them proves~\eqref{cyc:eq:exceptionalcount}.
\end{proof}

Thus the algebraic position of a root, not its numerical proximity to
another parameter, determines whether an exact recurrence drops order.
There is no open interval of exceptional values.

\subsection{The missing sign datum and reflection of the root}
\begin{corollary}[Order under $c\mapsto4-c$]\label{cyc:cor:c-reflect}
For $c\ne0,2,4$, the orders at $c$ and $4-c$ differ exactly when
$L\le t$ and $L,t$ are both odd.  In that case they differ by one:
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:c-reflect}
 R(t,4-c)-R(t,c)=2\delta-1.
\end{equation}
In every other case they are equal.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The replacement $z\mapsto-z$ sends $c$ to $4-c$ without changing
$z^2$ or $L$.  For odd $L$ it changes the sign of $z^L$; for even
$L$ it does not.  Corollary~\ref{cyc:cor:sign} shows that the two defects
are complementary precisely when $t,L$ are odd and $L\le t$.
Their grouped orders are identical.  In the nonresonant case both
orders are generic.
\end{proof}

The example $t=L=3$, $z=e^{2\pi i/3}$ versus $z=e^{\pi i/3}$,
is the smallest instance.  The two input roots are $c=1$ and $c=3$.
The orders $7$ and $6$ show that $t$ and $\ord(z^2)$ alone cannot
parametrize the answer.  The additional sign is invariant under the
allowed choice $z\leftrightarrow z^{-1}$, so it is genuine information
about $c$, not an artifact of a branch convention.

\subsection{Quadratic quasipolynomial growth in the multiplicity}
\begin{corollary}\label{cyc:cor:quasipoly}
Fix a noncentral cyclotomic parameter with $L\ge3$.  For $t\ge L$,
$R(t,c)$ is a quadratic quasipolynomial in $t$, with period dividing
four and leading coefficient $L/4$.  In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:quadratic}
 R(t,c)=\frac L4t^2+O_L(1),
\end{equation}
whereas the nonresonant order is $t^3/6+O(t)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
In~\eqref{cyc:eq:mainorder}, $\eta$ depends only on parity and $\delta$
depends only on $t$ modulo four once $L$ and $z^L$ are fixed.
The remaining term is $Lt^2/4$ plus a constant depending on $L$.
Equation~\eqref{cyc:eq:generic} gives the comparison.
\end{proof}

Here the asymptotic variable is the root multiplicity $t$, not the
Hankel size $N$.  This distinction matters: the determinant itself is
an exact quasipolynomial in $N$ at a cyclotomic parameter, but its
recurrence order is a different function.

\section{The central exception and the known product}\label{cyc:sec:central}

\subsection{Reduction to middle-binomial moments}
Set $y=x-2$ in~\eqref{cyc:eq:measure}.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:arcsine}
 \dd\mu=(1-y/2)\,\dd\alpha(y),\qquad
 \dd\alpha(y)=\frac{\dd y}{\pi\sqrt{4-y^2}},\quad -2<y<2.
\end{equation}
The arcsine moments are zero in odd degree and
$\int y^{2r}\,\dd\alpha=\binom{2r}{r}$, as follows from
$y=2\cos\theta$.  Consequently the centered Catalan moments are
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:centeredmoments}
 \mu_n:=\int(x-2)^n\,\dd\mu(x)
 =(-1)^n\binom n{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}.
\end{equation}
Indeed the odd moment is
$-\frac12\binom{2r+2}{r+1}=-\binom{2r+1}{r}$.

A monic translation of the polynomial basis leaves the determinant
unchanged, giving $\cH Nt(2)=\det(\mu_{i+j+t})$.  If
\[
 D_t(N)=\det_{0\le i,j<N}\binom{i+j+t}{\lfloor(i+j+t)/2\rfloor},
\]
row and column signs yield
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:Ciglertranslation}
 \cH Nt(2)=(-1)^{Nt}D_t(N).
\end{equation}
Cigler's Theorem~1~\cite{Cigler2021} states
$D_t(N)=(-1)^{t\binom N2}B_{\lfloor t/2\rfloor,\lceil t/2\rceil}(N)$.
Equation~\eqref{cyc:eq:Ciglertranslation} is therefore precisely
\eqref{cyc:eq:centraleven}--\eqref{cyc:eq:centralodd}.  In particular, the
uniform central formula requested by the repository
[Section~\ref{cyc:x:rcq1}] is already a
result established in a 2021 preprint after this change of normalization.

\subsection{A self-contained condensation proof}
We include the proof to keep the classification independent of an
external determinant evaluation.  It uses the same classical
condensation mechanism as Cigler's argument, not a new method.
Write $h_N^{(t)}=\det(\mu_{i+j+t})$ and set $h_0^{(t)}=1$.
The Desnanot--Jacobi determinant identity gives, for $N\ge2$,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:condensation}
 h_N^{(t)}h_{N-2}^{(t+2)}
 =h_{N-1}^{(t)}h_{N-1}^{(t+2)}-(h_{N-1}^{(t+1)})^2.
\end{equation}
It is a polynomial determinant identity and does not require any
minor to be nonzero.

We verify the proposed products in~\eqref{cyc:eq:centraleven} and
\eqref{cyc:eq:centralodd}, and then induct on $N$ simultaneously for all
$t\ge0$.  At $N=0$ the products equal one.  At $N=1$, cancellation
in~\eqref{cyc:eq:rectangle} gives
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:B1}
 B_{a,b}(1)=\prod_{i=1}^{a}\frac{i+b}{i}=\binom{a+b}{a},
\end{equation}
so the products give exactly the moments~\eqref{cyc:eq:centeredmoments}.

For the induction put $n=N-1\ge1$.  Four ratios suffice.  Direct
cancellation of the rectangular products yields
\begin{align}
 \frac{B_{m,m}(n)B_{m+1,m+1}(n)}{B_{m,m+1}(n)^2}
 &=\frac{n+2m+1}{2m+1},\label{cyc:eq:rat-even1}\\
 \frac{B_{m,m}(n+1)B_{m+1,m+1}(n-1)}{B_{m,m+1}(n)^2}
 &=\frac{n}{2m+1},\label{cyc:eq:rat-even2}\\
 \frac{B_{m,m+1}(n)B_{m+1,m+2}(n)}{B_{m+1,m+1}(n)^2}
 &=\frac{n+2m+2}{2(n+m+1)},\label{cyc:eq:rat-odd1}\\
 \frac{B_{m,m+1}(n+1)B_{m+1,m+2}(n-1)}{B_{m+1,m+1}(n)^2}
 &=\frac{n}{2(n+m+1)}.\label{cyc:eq:rat-odd2}
\end{align}
For example, adding the last column of a rectangle gives
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:addcol}
 \frac{B_{a,b+1}(n)}{B_{a,b}(n)}
 =\prod_{i=1}^{a}\frac{n+i+b}{i+b},
\end{equation}
and increasing $n$ telescopes within each row:
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:addN}
 \frac{B_{a,b}(n+1)}{B_{a,b}(n)}
 =\prod_{i=1}^{a}\frac{n+i+b}{n+i}.
\end{equation}
Applying~\eqref{cyc:eq:addcol} and~\eqref{cyc:eq:addN}, or their inverses,
to the four ratios cancels all interior factors and gives the displayed
right sides.  These identities also cover $m=0$.

For even $t=2m$, divide the product version of
\eqref{cyc:eq:condensation} by $B_{m,m+1}(n)^2$.  The squared odd sign
is one, and the identity becomes
\[
 \frac n{2m+1}=\frac{n+2m+1}{2m+1}-1.
\]
For odd $t=2m+1$, write $s_N=(-1)^{N(N+1)/2}$.
One has $s_Ns_{N-2}=-1$ and $s_{N-1}^2=1$.  Division by
$B_{m+1,m+1}(n)^2$ reduces the same identity to
\[
 -\frac n{2(n+m+1)}=\frac{n+2m+2}{2(n+m+1)}-1.
\]
Thus the proposed products satisfy the condensation equation.

By the induction hypothesis, the actual denominator
$h_{N-2}^{(t+2)}$ in solving~\eqref{cyc:eq:condensation} for $h_N^{(t)}$
is the proposed signed product, which is nonzero at nonnegative
integer $N-2$.  Solving is therefore legitimate.  It proves the product
at size $N$ for every $t$, completing the induction.  The nonzero
denominator is a conclusion established earlier in this induction,
not an assumption about arbitrary weighted Hankel minors.

\subsection{Minimality and why the one-defect law excludes this point}
The product $B_{a,b}(N)$ has degree $ab$ and nonzero leading
coefficient.  The even product therefore has minimal polynomial
$(X-1)^{m^2+1}$ by Lemma~\ref{cyc:lem:minimal}.  In the odd case,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:signexp}
 s_N=\frac{1+i}{2}\,i^N+\frac{1-i}{2}\,(-i)^N.
\end{equation}
Both coefficients are nonzero.  Multiplication by $B_{m,m+1}(N)$
gives both characteristic roots $i,-i$ with multiplicity
$m(m+1)+1$, proving Theorem~\ref{cyc:thm:central} in full.

For $t=2m\ge2$, the two grouped rates at $z=i$ are $1,-1$.
Their uncorrected exponents would be $m^2+1$ and $m^2$, so
$\Rgrp=2m^2+1$.  The exact product has only the rate $1$: the
entire $-1$ group vanishes, and the actual order is $m^2+1$.
The additional loss is $m^2$, not one.  Formula~\eqref{cyc:eq:nextsurvives}
correctly signals the problem because $z^2+1=0$ here.

For odd $t$, $L=2$ has different parity from $t$, so there is no tied
maximum at all.  The unique-maximum argument already gives the correct
odd central order.  The product evaluation provides much more: all
values and the exact period-four sign.

\section{A root-free minimal-recurrence algorithm}\label{cyc:sec:algorithm}

The classification need not be implemented by factoring the quadratic
for $z$, recognizing a root of unity, or computing Hankel determinants.
There is a direct exact algorithm over the coefficient field of $c$.

\subsection{Detecting a resonance by a short scalar recurrence}
Put $v=c-2$ and define
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:Urec}
 U_0=1,\qquad U_1=v,\qquad U_{a+1}=vU_a-U_{a-1}.
\end{equation}
These are scalar values of the Chebyshev polynomials of the second
kind, $U_a(v/2)$; we suppress the argument to emphasize computation.
From the recurrence,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:Uspectral}
 U_a=\frac{z^{a+1}-z^{-a-1}}{z-z^{-1}}.
\end{equation}
Since $z\ne\pm1$, the first zero $U_{L-1}=0$ occurs exactly at
$L=\ord(z^2)$, if that order is finite.  Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:sigmarootfree}
 \sigma:=z^L=-U_{L-2}\in\{1,-1\}.
\end{equation}
Indeed $U_L=(z^{L+1}-z^{-L-1})/(z-z^{-1})=z^L$, while the
recurrence at the zero gives $U_L=-U_{L-2}$.

Testing $U_1,\ldots,U_{t-1}$ therefore decides whether the recurrence
is resonant at the given multiplicity.  There is no need to decide
whether $z$ has some much larger finite order: all $L>t$ have the same
nonresonant formula.  This requires $O(t)$ exact field operations and
zero tests.

\subsection{Linear and quadratic factors in the original coefficient field}
Define another scalar sequence
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:Srec}
 S_0=2,\qquad S_1=v,\qquad S_{a+1}=vS_a-S_{a-1},
 \qquad S_a=z^a+z^{-a},
\end{equation}
and set
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:Qfactor}
 Q_a(X)=X^2-(-1)^tS_aX+1,\qquad
 E_a=\frac{t^2-a^2}{4}+1\quad(a\equiv t\pmod2).
\end{equation}
These factors have coefficients in $\QQ(c)$, or in any characteristic-zero
coefficient field containing $c$.

\begin{theorem}[Root-free factorization]\label{cyc:thm:rootfree}
For $c\ne0,4$, test~\eqref{cyc:eq:Urec} through $U_{t-1}$.
If no zero occurs, then
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:rootfreegeneric}
 \chi_{t,c}(X)=
 (X-1)^{\ind_{2\mid t}(t^2/4+1)}
 \prod_{\substack{1\le a\le t\\a\equiv t\ (2)}}Q_a(X)^{E_a}.
\end{equation}
The first factor is omitted when $t$ is odd.
If the first zero occurs at $L-1$ with $L=2$, use
\eqref{cyc:eq:centralevenchar}--\eqref{cyc:eq:centraloddchar}.
If $3\le L\le t$, compute $\sigma$ by~\eqref{cyc:eq:sigmarootfree} and
$\delta$ by~\eqref{cyc:eq:defect}, replacing $z^{Lt}$ by $\sigma^t$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:rootfreeresonant}
 \boxed{\quad
 \chi_{t,c}(X)=
 (X-1)^{\ind_{2\mid t}(t^2/4+1)}
 \prod_{\substack{1\le a<L\\a\equiv t\ (2)}}Q_a(X)^{E_a}
 \cdot (X-(-1)^t\sigma)^{\eta(E_L-\delta)}.
 \quad}
\end{equation}
The last factor is omitted when $\eta=0$ or its exponent is zero;
$E_L$ is only used when $\eta=1$.
The nonconstant displayed factors have pairwise disjoint roots, and
each displayed quadratic has distinct roots.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
In the nonresonant case, pair the sectors $k,t-k$ and put
$a=|2k-t|$.  Their rates are $(-1)^tz^a$ and $(-1)^tz^{-a}$,
whose quadratic is~\eqref{cyc:eq:Qfactor}.  Their common exponent is $E_a$.
When $t$ is even, the unpaired sector $k=t/2$ has rate one and
exponent $t^2/4+1$.  Distinctness follows from $L>t$.

In the resonant case, use the centered representatives in
Section~\ref{cyc:sec:orders}.  The pairs $\pm a$ with $0<a<L$ give
exactly the quadratics in~\eqref{cyc:eq:rootfreeresonant}.  The possible
center $a=0$ gives the first linear factor.  The tied endpoints
$a=\pm L$ occur exactly when $\eta=1$ and give the single rate
$(-1)^t\sigma$, with its one-defect exponent.
No two different interior pairs coincide: equality of rates would
force their corresponding $k$ indices to agree modulo $L$, contrary
to the choice of distinct centered representatives.  An interior
quadratic cannot have a double root because that would require
$z^{2a}=1$ with $0<a<L$.
If both linear factors occur, $L$ is even, whence $z^L=-1$; they are
therefore also distinct.  Theorem~\ref{cyc:thm:main} supplies minimality.
\end{proof}

The theorem outputs $O(t)$ linear or quadratic factors with integer
exponents after $O(t)$ scalar field operations.  This is an arithmetic
operation count for the \emph{factored} answer; it is not a bit-complexity
bound and not the cost of expanding a degree-$\Theta(t^3)$ polynomial.
Exact equality tests in the input field are essential.  Approximate
floating-point tests cannot reliably detect an exact resonance.

This improves the task-specific route considerably over building a
long prefix of large Hankel determinants and solving for an unknown
recurrence.  It does not replace the repository's more general
algorithm for a multiplier with several different roots
[Theorem~\ref{rc:thm:finite} with the bound \eqref{rc:eq:rootfreebound}].

\section{Worked examples and a complete rational-parameter corollary}
\label{cyc:sec:examples}

\subsection{Two cubic multipliers with the same cyclotomic order}
For $t=3,c=1$, choose $z=e^{2\pi i/3}$.  Then $L=3$, $z^L=1$,
and $\delta=0$.  The tied constant sectors retain the rate $-1$.
For $t=3,c=3$, choose $z=e^{\pi i/3}$.  Then $L=3$, $z^L=-1$,
and $\delta=1$.  The tied constant sectors at rate $1$ cancel entirely.
The root-free factors are particularly simple:
\begin{align}
 \chi_{3,1}(X)&=(X+1)(X^2-X+1)^3,
                  &R(3,1)&=7,\label{cyc:eq:c1c3a}\\
 \chi_{3,3}(X)&=(X^2+X+1)^3,
                  &R(3,3)&=6.\label{cyc:eq:c1c3b}
\end{align}
For both inputs the generic order would be eight.

There are concise formulas for the determinants themselves.  For
$n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:c3residues}
 \begin{aligned}
 \cH{3n}{3}(3)&=2n+1,\\
 \cH{3n+1}{3}(3)&=-(n+1)(18n+13),\\
 \cH{3n+2}{3}(3)&=(n+1)(18n+23).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For $c=1$, the six formulas are
\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.12}
\begin{tabular}{@{}clrrr@{}}
\toprule
$r$&$\cH{6n+r}{3}(1)$&$n=0$&$n=1$&$n=2$\\
\midrule
0&$(4n+1)^2$&1&25&81\\
1&$(2n+1)(4n+1)$&1&15&45\\
2&$-(2n+1)(4n+3)$&-3&-21&-55\\
3&$-(4n+3)^2$&-9&-49&-121\\
4&$-2(n+1)(4n+3)$&-6&-28&-66\\
5&$2(n+1)(4n+5)$&10&36&78\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
These are exact identities, not extrapolations.  The sector theorem
shows that each displayed progression is a polynomial of degree at
most two: the rates have periods three and six, respectively, and all
sector degrees are at most two.  The first three values in each
progression determine its quadratic.  Evaluating them from
\eqref{cyc:eq:Christoffel} gives the displayed formulas.  The accompanying
code additionally verifies each identity through $N=59$.

For reference, their first twelve values are
\begin{align*}
 \cH N3(1):&\quad 1,1,-3,-9,-6,10,25,15,-21,-49,-28,36,\ldots,\\
 \cH N3(3):&\quad 1,-13,23,3,-62,82,5,-147,177,7,-268,308,\ldots.
\end{align*}
The difference between their minimal orders persists even though
both are low-degree quasipolynomial sequences.

\subsection{A noncentral loss below a positive degree}
Take $c=2+\sqrt3$, $z=e^{\pi i/6}$, $L=6$, and $t=8$.
The tied indices are $1,7$, of degree seven, at rate $-1$.
Since $L\equiv2\pmod4$, their leading terms cancel.  Here
$z^2+1\ne0$, so~\eqref{cyc:eq:nextsurvives} proves that the grouped
polynomial has degree six, not less.  The full answer is
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:largeexample}
 \chi_{8,\,2+\sqrt3}(X)
 =(X-1)^{17}(X+1)^7
   (X^2+X+1)^{13}(X^2-X+1)^{16}.
\end{equation}
Its order is $82$, compared with grouped order $83$ and generic
order $93$.  This example illustrates the genuinely positive-degree
part of the one-defect theorem, rather than just cancellation of
two constants.  The same recurrence holds at $c=2-\sqrt3$.

\subsection{All rational nonendpoint roots}
\begin{corollary}\label{cyc:cor:rational}
If $c\in\QQ\setminus\{0,1,2,3,4\}$, then
$R(t,c)=\Rgen(t)$ for every $t\ge1$.
For $c=1$ and $c=3$, $R(1,c)=2$.  For $t\ge2$ their orders are
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:rationalorders}
 \begin{array}{c|cc}
 &c=1&c=3\\ \hline
 t\text{ even}&3t^2/4+1&3t^2/4+1\\[1mm]
 t\text{ odd}&(3t^2+1)/4-\ind_{t\equiv1\ (4)}
             &(3t^2+1)/4-\ind_{t\equiv3\ (4)}.
 \end{array}
\end{equation}
For $c=2$, the exact orders are
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:centralorders}
 R(t,2)=
 \begin{cases}t^2/4+1,&t\text{ even},\\(t^2+3)/2,&t\text{ odd}.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
If $z$ is a root of unity, $z+z^{-1}$ is an algebraic integer.
A rational algebraic integer is an integer: substitution of a reduced
fraction into its monic integer equation forces its denominator to be
one.  Also $-2<z+z^{-1}<2$ when $c\ne0,4$ is real and cyclotomic.
Thus the only rational possibilities are $c=1,2,3$.  Every other
rational nonendpoint input is nonresonant.  At $c=1,3$ use $L=3$
and $z^L=1,-1$, respectively, in~\eqref{cyc:eq:mainorder}.  The case
$t=1$ precedes the resonant range and is stated separately.
The central formula is Theorem~\ref{cyc:thm:central}.
\end{proof}

\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.08}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrr@{}}
\toprule
$t$&$R(t,1)$&$R(t,2)$&$R(t,3)$&$\Rgen(t)$\\
\midrule
1&2&2&2&2\\
2&4&2&4&4\\
3&7&6&6&8\\
4&13&5&13&15\\
5&18&14&19&26\\
6&28&10&28&42\\
7&37&26&36&64\\
8&49&17&49&93\\
9&60&42&61&130\\
10&76&26&76&176\\
11&91&62&90&232\\
12&109&37&109&299\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
All entries follow from the proved formulas.  The machine-readable
version extends through $t=20$.

\section{Exact verification and reproducibility}\label{cyc:sec:verification}

The universal results above follow from the displayed proofs.  The
verification suite is an independent finite audit of formulas, signs,
normalizations, and algorithmic implementations.  No assertion of
universality is inferred merely from the number of successful tests.

\subsection{Arithmetic models and independent routes}
The program \texttt{code/verify.py} (shipped here as
\nolinkurl{code/03-cyclotomic-verify.py}) uses Python's standard library only.
Cyclotomic arithmetic takes place in
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:ring}
 \ZZ[z]/(\Phi_M(z)),
\end{equation}
with coefficient vectors in the usual basis of degree less than
$\deg\Phi_M$.  The monic polynomials $\Phi_M$ are constructed by exact
successive division of $x^M-1$ by the previously constructed factors
for its proper divisors.  All arithmetic is integral; comparisons do
not use tolerances or approximate complex roots.  Negative powers of
$z$ are reduced using $z^M=1$.

The raw sector formula~\eqref{cyc:eq:rawsector} is evaluated as an exact
polynomial in $N$.  Its degree, leading coefficient, first centered
correction, and reflected polynomial are compared with the theorems.
The sectors are then added according to equal rates, and their actual
polynomial degrees are compared with every predicted characteristic
multiplicity.  The root-free algorithm is tested separately: it does
not access the cyclotomic order or the spectral generator when
constructing its factors from $c$.  Its factors are checked against the
actual grouped rates and multiplicities.

The original small Hankel determinants in the same rings are computed
by the Leibniz permutation formula, not by the sector determinant.
To compare with the rational sector expression, the test clears the
common denominator.  Explicitly it checks
\begin{multline}\label{cyc:eq:divisionfreecheck}
 \cH Nt(c)(z^2-1)^T(z-1)^tA_t\\
 =(-1)^{Nt}z^T\sum_{k=0}^{t}F_{t,k}(N;z)z^{(2k-t)N}.
\end{multline}
Thus no inversion in a number field is needed even in this independent
comparison.

For integer $c$, a second route evaluates the original moment matrix
by pivoted fraction-free Bareiss elimination and compares it with the
$t\times t$ normalized-jet determinant.  The jets are generated from
\eqref{cyc:eq:prec}: if $u_{n,r}=p_n^{(r)}(c)/r!$, then
\begin{equation}\label{cyc:eq:jetcode}
 u_{n+1,r}=(c-2)u_{n,r}+u_{n,r-1}-u_{n-1,r},\qquad u_{n,-1}=0,
\end{equation}
with $u_{0,0}=1$, $u_{1,0}=c-1$, $u_{1,1}=1$, and other initial
jets zero.  This is independent of the subset expansion.

\subsection{Parameter ranges and the recorded run}
The principal cyclotomic grid uses primitive orders $3\le M\le24$
and root multiplicities $1\le t\le12$.  A computation in
$\ZZ[z]/\Phi_M$ is exact at a primitive root and respects all its
Galois conjugates, rather than sampling a floating-point angle.
The original cyclotomic determinant comparisons use $M\le14$,
$t\le8$, and sizes $N=0,\ldots,4$.

The integer determinant comparison uses
$c\in\{-2,0,1,2,3,4,5\}$, $t=1,\ldots,10$, and $N=0,\ldots,10$.
The endpoint values here only audit the classical determinant identity;
they are not applications of the nonendpoint classification.
The central product is checked for $t=0,\ldots,16$ and
$N=0,\ldots,16$.  The scalar sign and square-sum identities are checked
on an additional grid with $M\le60$ and $t\le100$.
