\section{The repository question and the contribution}\label{tec:sec:scope}
\subsection{The endpoint moving-root question}
For $C_n=\binom{2n}{n}/(n+1)$ and $q(x)=\sum_r q_rx^r$, put
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:HN}
 H_N(q)=\det\left(\sum_r q_r C_{i+j+r}\right)_{0\le i,j<N},
 \qquad H_0(q)=1.
\end{equation}
The report \textit{Shifted Catalan Hankel Polynomials} in \textit{ProveIt} contains
three parts: a shifted linear-multiplier analysis, an extension to
arbitrary polynomial multipliers, and a single-root cyclotomic
classification. Its Part II, Section~\ref{tec:x:rcq4}, asks for the locally uniform
limit associated with
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:question}
 c_N=2+2\cosh(\tau/N),\qquad q_N(x)=(x-c_N)^t,
 \qquad \frac{H_N(q_N)}{(-1)^{tN}N^{t(t+1)/2}},
\end{equation}
and for the corresponding left-endpoint limit with exponent
$t(t-1)/2$. It specifically requests confluent determinant limits and
error bounds. The same subsection separately asks about collisions away
from the endpoints; that separate problem is not solved here.

The source was read at repository commit
\texttt{1ee53d57de253d16cdaad79d1f54bbd95d76d682}.
The mathematical source file had blob
\texttt{e5a4d81408c80f0385ae66c26fbd11bba7dfdb09}.
The path, relevant sections, and comparison are recorded in
Section~\ref{tec:sec:source} and the accompanying \texttt{SOURCES.md}.
In particular, the source's fixed-parameter asymptotics
(Section~\ref{rc:sec:asymptotics}) expressly warn
that they cannot simply be substituted into a moving-root limit.

\noindent[Merge note: the manuscript's \texttt{SOURCES.md} is shipped as
\nolinkurl{04-endpoint-confluence-SOURCES.md}; its bibliography item for
the pinned report is replaced by Parts~I--III themselves and the pin
recorded in Section~\ref{tec:sec:source}.  Section~24.4 of Part~II and
Section~22 of Part~II, as the manuscript cites them, are
Sections~\ref{tec:x:rcq4} and~\ref{rc:sec:asymptotics} here: the numbers
did not change.]

The results here address a larger fixed-degree problem. Any number $m$
of roots may approach $0$, any number $\ell$ may approach $4$, and roots
inside either cluster may coincide in any pattern. The approach may be
from outside the interval, inside it, or in complex directions. Uniformity
includes all these collisions on compact sets of the scaled coordinates.

\subsection{What is classical, and what is established here}
The reduction of a polynomially modified moment determinant to a
fixed-size determinant of orthogonal polynomials is classical.
Krattenthaler gives a detailed account, four proofs, a confluent version,
and recurrence applications~\cite{Krattenthaler2023}. We use that identity,
with a self-contained proof for the present normalization. The broader
appearance of characteristic-polynomial determinants, integrable kernels,
and hard-edge limits is likewise established in random matrix theory;
see Strahov--Fyodorov and Akemann--Fyodorov~\cite{StrahovFyodorov2003,AkemannFyodorov2003}. The one-variable
cosine and sine limits are elementary half-integer cases of the
Mehler--Heine phenomenon~\cite{DLMFMehlerHeine}.

The contribution is the following explicit package for the repository's
problem: a simultaneous two-endpoint, collision-uniform theorem; the exact
center $N+d/2$; a genuinely convergent expansion containing no odd powers;
a closed first interaction operator; an all-orders coefficient generator;
an elementary, explicit error bound; and a necessary-and-sufficient
outward stability criterion. These conclusions are proved directly,
without assuming the repository's newer sector-degree or resonance
claims. A bounded literature check does not establish priority for every
identity or corollary in this package.

\subsection{A guide to the proof}
The decisive algebra is not a delicate large determinant estimate.
Centering the columns turns an exponential alternant into a product of
hyperbolic sines within each cluster and hyperbolic cosines between the
clusters. After the forced zero is divided out, that product is even in
the inverse size. A fixed-dimensional Christoffel formula transports this
identity to Catalan determinants. The first correction follows from the
quadratic term of the product; positivity of the limiting profiles then
settles the sharp scale question.

\section{Notation and the two-endpoint model}\label{tec:sec:model}
Fix nonnegative integers $m,\ell$ with $d=m+\ell\ge1$. Write
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:indices}
 b_r=\binom r2,\qquad \rho=b_m+b_\ell,\qquad
 \kappa=b_m+\binom{\ell+1}{2}=\rho+\ell,
 \qquad \nu=N+\frac d2,\quad h=\nu^{-1}.
\end{equation}
For a tuple $\bu$, let $\Del(\bu)=\prod_{i<j}(u_j-u_i)$; empty
products and empty determinants are $1$.

All square-root expressions below denote entire power series in the
squared variable, so no branch choice is part of the definition. Set
\begin{align}
 C(t,y)&=\cos(t\sqrt y)
       =\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{(-1)^k t^{2k}y^k}{(2k)!},\label{tec:eq:CSa}\\
 S(t,y)&=\frac{\sin(t\sqrt y)}{\sqrt y}
       =\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{(-1)^k t^{2k+1}y^k}{(2k+1)!}.
       \label{tec:eq:CSb}
\end{align}
In particular $S(t,0)=t$. For $\by\in\CC^m$ and $\bv\in\CC^\ell$,
define the moving roots and multiplier by
\begin{align}
 c_i^-(h)&=2-2\cos(h\sqrt{y_i}), &1\le i\le m,\\
 c_j^+(h)&=2+2\cos(h\sqrt{v_j}), &1\le j\le\ell,\label{tec:eq:roots}\\
 q_N(x)&=\prod_{i=1}^m(x-c_i^-(h))
          \prod_{j=1}^{\ell}(x-c_j^+(h)).
\end{align}
Thus $c_i^-=h^2y_i+O(h^4)$ and
$c_j^+=4-h^2v_j+O(h^4)$. Negative real coordinates mean outward motion.
The normalized determinant is
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:ZN}
 Z_N(\by,\bv)=(-1)^{N\ell}\nu^{-\kappa}H_N(q_N).
\end{equation}

Define the two entire, symmetric kernel functions by
\begin{align}
 K_m^-(t;\by)
 &=(-1)^{b_m}\frac{\det[\partial_t^r C(t,y_i)]_
             {1\le i\le m,\ 0\le r<m}}{\Del(\by)},\label{tec:eq:Km}\\
 K_\ell^+(s;\bv)
 &=(-1)^{b_\ell}\frac{\det[\partial_s^r S(s,v_j)]_
             {1\le j\le\ell,\ 0\le r<\ell}}{\Del(\bv)}.
             \label{tec:eq:Kp}
\end{align}
The quotients are defined at collisions by analytic continuation. More
concretely, a block of $r$ repeated coordinates $z$ is replaced by rows
of normalized derivatives $(1/a!)\partial_z^a$, $0\le a<r$; the
remaining denominator is the product of cross-block differences to the
products of their multiplicities. Set $K_0^\pm=1$.

\section{Main results}\label{tec:sec:main}
\subsection{An exact even expansion}
\begin{theorem}[Two-endpoint confluence]\label{tec:thm:main}
There are entire functions $A_j(\by,\bv)$, symmetric separately in each
cluster, such that for all sufficiently large $N$,
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:mainseries}
 Z_N(\by,\bv)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{A_j(\by,\bv)}{\nu^{2j}}.
\end{equation}
The series converges locally uniformly in the root coordinates and
uniformly for $|h|$ in a sufficiently small closed disk. It is the Taylor
series of an even holomorphic function $Z(h;\by,\bv)$ with
$Z_N=Z(\nu^{-1};\by,\bv)$. In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:A0}
 A_0(\by,\bv)=2^{\ell-m\ell}
       K_m^-(1;\by)K_\ell^+(1;\bv).
\end{equation}
All statements include repeated roots, zero coordinates, and mixed
collision patterns. For every fixed $J$ the remainder after $A_J$ is
$O(\nu^{-2J-2})$ locally uniformly, including after any fixed number of
derivatives in the coordinates.
\end{theorem}

The theorem is stronger than the existence of a formal asymptotic series.
It constructs an analytic function of the inverse size. The absence of
odd powers is exact, not a cancellation observed through a finite order.
The restriction to fixed $m,\ell$ is important: the constants and the
analytic radius are not claimed to be uniform in growing multiplicities.

\subsection{The complete first correction}
In the next statement every kernel and its derivatives are evaluated at
$t=s=1$. Put $Y=\sum_i y_i$ and $V=\sum_j v_j$.
\begin{theorem}[First interaction law]\label{tec:thm:first}
The coefficient of $\nu^{-2}$ in \eqref{tec:eq:mainseries} is
\begin{align}\label{tec:eq:A1}
 A_1=2^{\ell-m\ell}\Bigg\{&
 \frac{(m+3\ell+1)Y+(\ell+3m-1)V}{24}\,K_m^-K_\ell^+
 \nonumber\\[-1mm]
 &-\frac{(\partial_t^2K_m^-)K_\ell^+
             +K_m^-(\partial_s^2K_\ell^+)}{24}
 -\frac{(\partial_tK_m^-)(\partial_sK_\ell^+)}4\Bigg\}.
\end{align}
This identity is entire in every coordinate; it does not divide by the
leading profile, so it remains valid at zeros of $A_0$.
\end{theorem}

At leading order the clusters decouple, up to the scalar
$2^{\ell-m\ell}$. The first correction has a mixed derivative whose
coefficient is always $-1/4$. It is therefore incorrect, in general, to
multiply two one-endpoint finite-size expansions and expect the
simultaneous two-endpoint correction.

An equivalent form uses the Euler operators
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:euler}
 \mathcal D_-=b_m+2\sum_i y_i\partial_{y_i},\qquad
 \mathcal D_+=\binom{\ell+1}{2}+2\sum_jv_j\partial_{v_j}.
\end{equation}
On the product of the kernels, replace $\partial_t$ by $\mathcal D_-$,
$\partial_t^2$ by $\mathcal D_-(\mathcal D_--1)$, and analogously for
$s$. This eliminates auxiliary variables from the correction formula.

\subsection{The endpoint value}
\begin{corollary}\label{tec:cor:endpoint}
At $\by=\boldsymbol0$, $\bv=\boldsymbol0$,
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:Aendpoint}
 A_0(\boldsymbol0,\boldsymbol0)=
 \mathcal A_{m,\ell}:=
 2^{\ell-m\ell+b_m+b_\ell}
 \prod_{r=0}^{m-1}\frac{r!}{(2r)!}
 \prod_{r=0}^{\ell-1}\frac{r!}{(2r+1)!}>0.
\end{equation}
Writing $a=b_m$ and $b=\binom{\ell+1}{2}$, one also has
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:Aendpoint1}
 \frac{A_1(\boldsymbol0,\boldsymbol0)}{\mathcal A_{m,\ell}}
 =-\frac{a(a-1)+b(b-1)+6ab}{24}.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\subsection{A sharp scale criterion}
For nonnegative real outward displacements define
\begin{align}
 \widetilde q_N(x)&=\prod_{i=1}^m(x+\epsilon_{i,N})
             \prod_{j=1}^{\ell}(x-4-\eta_{j,N}),\\
 R_N&=\frac{H_N(\widetilde q_N)}{H_N(x^m(x-4)^\ell)},\qquad
 M_N=N^2\max\{\epsilon_{i,N},\eta_{j,N}\}.\label{tec:eq:R}
\end{align}
The denominator is nonzero and $R_N$ is positive.
\begin{theorem}[Exact outward stability threshold]\label{tec:thm:scale}
With fixed $m,\ell$ as above,
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:iff}
 R_N\longrightarrow1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad M_N\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
If $N^2\epsilon_{i,N}\to a_i$ and $N^2\eta_{j,N}\to b_j$ with finite
nonnegative limits, then
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:finiteprofile}
 R_N\longrightarrow
 \frac{K_m^-(1;-\ba)}{K_m^-(1;\boldsymbol0)}
 \frac{K_\ell^+(1;-\bb)}{K_\ell^+(1;\boldsymbol0)}.
\end{equation}
This limit exceeds $1$ if at least one limit coordinate is positive.
If $M_N\to\infty$, then $R_N\to\infty$.
\end{theorem}
The equivalence is restricted to outward, nonnegative displacements.
Inside the interval, oscillations and zeros prevent the same monotonicity
argument and may invalidate such an equivalence.

\section{The Catalan moment functional and Christoffel reduction}\label{tec:sec:christoffel}
\subsection{Orthogonal polynomials with unit norms}
The Catalan numbers are moments of the probability measure
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:measure}
 d\mu(x)=\frac1{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{4-x}{x}}\,dx,\qquad 0<x<4.
\end{equation}
Indeed, $x=4u$ gives
$\int x^k\,d\mu=(2/\pi)4^k B(k+1/2,3/2)=C_k$.
Define monic polynomials by
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:pn}
 p_0=1,\qquad p_1=x-1,\qquad
 p_{n+1}=(x-2)p_n-p_{n-1}\quad(n\ge1).
\end{equation}
Their elementary trigonometric expressions are
\begin{align}
 p_n(2+2\cos\theta)
   &=\frac{\sin((n+1/2)\theta)}{\sin(\theta/2)},\label{tec:eq:ptrigplus}\\
 p_n(2-2\cos\theta)
   &=(-1)^n\frac{\cos((n+1/2)\theta)}{\cos(\theta/2)}.
   \label{tec:eq:ptrigminus}
\end{align}
The identities follow by checking $n=0,1$ and the recurrence; the
apparent singularities at special angles are removable where appropriate.

Under $x=2+2\cos\theta$, the measure is
$(2/\pi)\sin^2(\theta/2)\,d\theta$ on $0<\theta<\pi$.
Thus the functions in \eqref{tec:eq:ptrigplus} are orthonormal, since the
half-integer sine functions have squared integral $\pi/2$ and distinct
ones are orthogonal. In particular $\int p_np_k\,d\mu=\delta_{nk}$ and
$H_N(1)=1$. These facts fix all normalizing constants used below.

\subsection{A proof of the fixed-size determinant identity}
\begin{lemma}[Christoffel identity]\label{tec:lem:Christoffel}
For $q(x)=\prod_{a=1}^d(x-c_a)$,
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:Christoffel}
 H_N(q)=(-1)^{Nd}
   \frac{\det[p_{N+j}(c_i)]_{1\le i\le d,\ 0\le j<d}}
        {\Del(c_1,\ldots,c_d)}.
\end{equation}
The quotient is a symmetric polynomial in the $c_a$ and hence extends to
all collisions by normalized derivative rows.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Expanding two determinants and integrating term by term yields the
Gram integral identity
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:Andreief}
 H_N(q)=\frac1{N!}\int_{(0,4)^N}\Del(x_1,\ldots,x_N)^2
                 \prod_{a=1}^Nq(x_a)\,d\mu(x_a).
\end{equation}
The same identity holds for complex $q$, by finite multilinearity.
The enlarged Vandermonde in the ordered tuple
$(x_1,\ldots,x_N,c_1,\ldots,c_d)$ is
\[
 \Del(\boldsymbol x)\Del(\boldsymbol c)
            \prod_{a,i}(c_i-x_a).
\]
Replace its monomial columns by the monic columns $p_0,p_1,\ldots$;
the determinant does not change. Multiply by
$\Del(\boldsymbol x)=\det[p_{j-1}(x_i)]_{1\le i,j\le N}$ and integrate.
Laplace expansion on the first $N$ rows followed by orthogonality kills
every choice of $N$ columns except the first $N$. The surviving integral
is $N!$, and the remaining determinant is
$\det[p_{N+j}(c_i)]$. Comparing with \eqref{tec:eq:Andreief} gives
\eqref{tec:eq:Christoffel}, including the sign $(-1)^{Nd}$.
An alternating polynomial is divisible by the Vandermonde. Taylor
expansion of rows in a colliding block proves the normalized derivative
rule and its factorials.
\end{proof}

This proof is included to make the present article self-contained, not
as a claim of a new Christoffel identity. The general identity and its
confluence are explicitly treated in~\cite{Krattenthaler2023}.

\section{The centered hyperbolic alternant}\label{tec:sec:alternant}
The following finite determinant is the algebraic engine of the article.
Let $\alpha_j=j-(d-1)/2$, $0\le j<d$. Introduce independent auxiliary
variables $u_1,\ldots,u_m,w_1,\ldots,w_\ell$ and the $d\times d$ matrix
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:expMat}
 E(h;\bu,\bw)=
 \begin{bmatrix}
 [(-1)^j e^{\alpha_jhu_i}]_{i,j}\\
 [e^{\alpha_jhw_a}]_{a,j}
 \end{bmatrix}.
\end{equation}
We use $\sinhc z=\sinh(z)/z$, with value $1$ at zero.

\begin{lemma}[Exact centered alternant]\label{tec:lem:alternant}
One has the identity of entire functions
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:master}
 \det E=(-1)^{b_m}2^{m\ell}h^\rho
            \Del(\bu)\Del(\bw)\,\mathcal R(h;\bu,\bw),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}\label{tec:eq:Rgenerator}
 \mathcal R={}&
 \prod_{i<i'}\sinhc\!\left(\frac h2(u_{i'}-u_i)\right)
 \prod_{a<a'}\sinhc\!\left(\frac h2(w_{a'}-w_a)\right)
 \prod_{i,a}\cosh\!\left(\frac h2(w_a-u_i)\right).
\end{align}
Consequently $\mathcal R$ is even in $h$, symmetric within each tuple,
and its constant term is $1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Factor $e^{-h(d-1)u_i/2}$ from each lower row and
$e^{-h(d-1)w_a/2}$ from each upper row. The remaining determinant is
the Vandermonde at nodes $-e^{hu_i}$ and $e^{hw_a}$. Within the first
cluster its factors are
$-2e^{h(u_i+u_{i'})/2}\sinh(h(u_{i'}-u_i)/2)$; within the second
cluster they are the analogous positive factors. Between clusters they
are $2e^{h(u_i+w_a)/2}\cosh(h(w_a-u_i)/2)$.
Every variable occurs in exactly $d-1$ exponential factors, so the
centering cancels all exponentials. Extract $h$ times each within-cluster
difference. There are $\rho$ such differences and $m\ell$ cross pairs.
This gives \eqref{tec:eq:master}, including its sign and power of $2$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The quadratic generator}
The first nonconstant term is
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:R2}
 \mathcal R=1+h^2\mathcal R_2+O(h^4),
\end{equation}
where, writing $U=\sum_i u_i$ and $W=\sum_aw_a$,
\begin{align}\label{tec:eq:R2closed}
 24\mathcal R_2={}&(m+3\ell)\sum_i u_i^2-U^2
       +(\ell+3m)\sum_a w_a^2-W^2-6UW.
\end{align}
Indeed, a within-cluster pair contributes its squared difference divided
by $24$, and a cross pair contributes its squared difference divided by
$8$. Summing the differences gives the displayed formula.

\subsection{The all-orders meaning}
Expand the right side of \eqref{tec:eq:Rgenerator} to any prescribed order.
The coefficient of $h^{2j}$ is a finite symmetric polynomial with rational
coefficients. In the alternant on the left, expanding each row in powers
of $h$ identifies the coefficients with minors of
$[(-1)^j\alpha_j^r/r!]$ and $[\alpha_j^s/s!]$.
Equivalently, expand $\mathcal R$ in the two Schur bases; a Schur
partition records exactly which derivative orders replace the consecutive
orders $0,\ldots,m-1$ or $0,\ldots,\ell-1$.

This supplies an all-orders coefficient algorithm. It can also be read
as an identity of constant-coefficient differential operators: replace
$u_i,w_a$ by derivatives in separate row variables and apply the result
to a product of the functions $C$ and $S$. Each finite Taylor coefficient
uses only finitely many derivatives. Convergence is justified below by
holomorphy, rather than by a purely formal interchange of infinite
operators.

\section{Proof of the even, collision-uniform expansion}\label{tec:sec:proofmain}
\subsection{An exact normalized formula}
Put
\begin{align}
 \phi_h(y)&=\frac{2-2\cos(h\sqrt y)}{h^2},&\phi_0(y)&=y,\\
 c_h(y)&=\cos(h\sqrt y/2),&s_h(y)&=\sinc(h\sqrt y/2).
\end{align}
All three functions are entire in $(h,y)$ and even in $h$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:M}
 M(h;\by,\bv)=
 \begin{bmatrix}
 [(-1)^j C(1+\alpha_jh,y_i)]_{i,j}\\
 [S(1+\alpha_jh,v_a)]_{a,j}
 \end{bmatrix}.
\end{equation}
Using \eqref{tec:eq:ptrigplus}--\eqref{tec:eq:ptrigminus} in
\eqref{tec:eq:Christoffel} yields
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:Zexact}
 Z(h;\by,\bv)=
 \frac{2^\ell(-1)^{b_\ell}h^{-\rho}\det M}
 {\displaystyle
  \prod_i c_h(y_i)\prod_a s_h(v_a)
  \Del(\phi_h(\by))\Del(\phi_h(\bv))
  \prod_{i,a}\bigl[4-h^2(\phi_h(y_i)+\phi_h(v_a))\bigr]}.
\end{equation}
At $h=(N+d/2)^{-1}$ the right side equals $Z_N$.

For completeness, the powers and signs are as follows. The $m$ lower
rows supply $(-1)^{Nm}$, which combines with the Christoffel sign to
leave $(-1)^{N\ell}$. The upper rows each supply $2/h$. The lower
within-cluster Vandermonde is $h^{2b_m}\Del(\phi_h(\by))$; the upper
one is $(-1)^{b_\ell}h^{2b_\ell}\Del(\phi_h(\bv))$.
Multiplication by $h^\kappa$ leaves $h^{-\rho}$, as in
\eqref{tec:eq:Zexact}. No additional factorials belong in this formula.

\subsection{Removal of the apparent singularities}
\begin{lemma}\label{tec:lem:analyticQ}
The function
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:Q}
 Q(h;\by,\bv)=\frac{\det M(h;\by,\bv)}
                        {h^\rho\Del(\by)\Del(\bv)}
\end{equation}
is entire and even in $h$, and entire in every coordinate.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Taylor-expand each row in $h$. In a nonzero determinant term the selected
derivative orders within one cluster must be distinct, since equal
orders would give proportional rows in the corresponding coefficient
matrix. Their smallest possible sum is $b_m$ for the lower cluster and
$b_\ell$ for the upper cluster. Thus the determinant vanishes to order
at least $\rho$. Its quotient by $h^\rho$ is entire, jointly with the
coordinates. Alternation in the coordinates of either cluster then
removes the two Vandermondes. One may justify joint removal by successive
analytic divided differences, which also gives the stated confluent rule.

For parity, reverse the $d$ columns. Since
$\alpha_{d-1-j}=-\alpha_j$, one obtains
\[
 \det M(-h)=(-1)^{b_d+m(d-1)}\det M(h)=(-1)^\rho\det M(h).
\]
The last equality follows because
$b_d+m(d-1)-\rho=m(m-1)+2m\ell$ is even. Dividing by $h^\rho$
proves evenness. Alternatively, every odd coefficient vanishes in
\eqref{tec:eq:master}.
\end{proof}

Write
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:Dphi}
 D_h(\by)=\prod_{i<i'}
 \frac{\phi_h(y_{i'})-\phi_h(y_i)}{y_{i'}-y_i},
\end{equation}
with derivatives at coincidences, and similarly for $\bv$.
Then \eqref{tec:eq:Zexact} is $2^\ell(-1)^{b_\ell}Q$ divided by
$\prod c_h\prod s_hD_h(\by)D_h(\bv)$ and the cross factors.
At $h=0$ these denominator factors are respectively $1,1,1,1$ and
$4^{m\ell}$. They remain nonzero on a sufficiently small disk,
uniformly on any compact coordinate set. This proves holomorphy and
convergence of the even Taylor series.

The least-order term in the row expansion uses precisely the derivative
orders $0,\ldots,m-1$ and $0,\ldots,\ell-1$. By the constant term of
\eqref{tec:eq:master}, its coefficient is
\[
 (-1)^{b_m}2^{m\ell}
 \det[\partial_t^r C(1,y_i)]\det[\partial_s^r S(1,v_a)].
\]
Substitution in \eqref{tec:eq:Zexact} gives \eqref{tec:eq:A0}. All Taylor
coefficients are entire in the coordinates, because every denominator
has a nonzero constant in $h$ and every numerator coefficient is an
entire alternating quotient. Uniform convergence of differentiated
series follows from Cauchy's estimates on a slightly larger compact set.
This proves Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:main}.

\subsection{An explicit remainder bound}
\begin{theorem}[A conservative effective bound]\label{tec:thm:bound}
Suppose $|y_i|,|v_a|\le R$, $R\ge0$. Let
\begin{align}
 r&=\frac1{4\sqrt{R+1}},&T&=1+\frac{d-1}{2}r,\\
 B_{m,\ell,R}&=2^{\ell+d+\rho}d!\,r^{-\rho}T^d
                  \exp\bigl(dT\sqrt{R+1}\bigr).
                  \label{tec:eq:boundconstant}
\end{align}
Then $Z$ is holomorphic on a neighborhood of $|h|\le r$ and has modulus
at most $B_{m,\ell,R}$ on $|h|=r$. For $|h|<r$,
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:bound}
 \left|Z(h)-\sum_{j=0}^J A_jh^{2j}\right|
 \le B_{m,\ell,R}
       \frac{(|h|/r)^{2J+2}}{1-(|h|/r)^2}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Here $|h\sqrt y|\le1/4$. The power-series bounds give
$|c_h(y)-1|<1/2$, $|s_h(y)-1|<1/2$, and
\[
 |\phi_h'(y)-1|=|\sinc(h\sqrt y)-1|
 \le\frac{\sinh(1/4)}{1/4}-1<\frac1{50}.
\]
A divided difference of $\phi_h$ is an integral of its derivative on
the straight segment between its nodes. It consequently has distance
less than $1/50$ from $1$. Also $|\phi_h(y)|\le(51/50)R$, so each cross
factor has modulus greater than $3$. In particular all denominator
factors in the rewritten version of \eqref{tec:eq:Zexact} are nonzero;
for an upper bound their inverse product is at most $2^{d+\rho}$.

To bound the determinant divided by the coordinate Vandermondes, replace
rows by successive divided differences within each cluster. Every such
row entry is bounded by the supremum of its original entire row function
on $|y|\le R+1$, by Cauchy's derivative estimate and the integral formula
for divided differences. On that larger disk, $|1+\alpha_jh|\le T$,
and both $C$ and $S$ have modulus at most
$T\exp(T\sqrt{R+1})$. The determinant is therefore bounded by $d!$
times the $d$th power of this number. On $|h|=r$, division by $h^\rho$
costs $r^{-\rho}$. The factor $2^\ell$ in \eqref{tec:eq:Zexact} gives
\eqref{tec:eq:boundconstant}. Finally apply Cauchy's coefficient bound to
the even Taylor series and sum its geometric tail.
\end{proof}
The constants deliberately favor transparency over sharpness. They are
explicit analytic bounds, whereas the numerical tests later in the
article are not interval certificates.

\section{Derivation of the first interaction coefficient}\label{tec:sec:first}
\subsection{The five relevant minors}
For a cluster of size $n$, denote the determinant with column derivative
orders $0,\ldots,n-1$ by $J_0$. Let $J_1,J_2,J_{11}$ use, respectively,
\[
 (0,\ldots,n-2,n),\quad
 (0,\ldots,n-2,n+1),\quad
 (0,\ldots,n-3,n-1,n).
\]
Set $J_{11}=0$ if $n<2$. The symbols refer to either the $C$ or $S$
cluster. They may be divided by the same Vandermonde and multiplied by
the same sign as the base kernel.

The coefficient of degree two in the centered alternant gives the
following ratios to the base coefficient. The row coefficients use
$\alpha_j^r/r!$, so the factorials in this table are already included.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}\toprule
Derivative selection & Coefficient ratio\\\midrule
Lower $J_2$, upper $J_0$ & $(m+3\ell-1)/24$\\
Lower $J_{11}$, upper $J_0$ & $-(m+3\ell+1)/24$\\
Lower $J_0$, upper $J_2$ & $(\ell+3m-1)/24$\\
Lower $J_0$, upper $J_{11}$ & $-(\ell+3m+1)/24$\\
Lower $J_1$, upper $J_1$ & $-1/4$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
A selection involving a nonexistent cluster is simply absent. To verify
the table directly, use $s_{(2)}-s_{(1,1)}=\sum u_i^2$ and
$s_{(2)}+s_{(1,1)}=(\sum u_i)^2$ in \eqref{tec:eq:R2closed}.
The term $UW=s_{(1)}(\bu)s_{(1)}(\bw)$ gives the mixed coefficient.
There are no degree-one selections.

\subsection{Reduction to ordinary kernel derivatives}
Every row function satisfies $\partial_t^2f(t,y)=-yf(t,y)$. Differentiating
$J_0$ column by column leaves only the last column, hence
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:Jrelations}
 J_0'=J_1,\qquad J_0''=J_2+J_{11},\qquad
 J_2-J_{11}=-\Bigl(\sum y_i\Bigr)J_0.
\end{equation}
For the last identity, sum the determinants obtained by applying two
derivatives to one column. The differential equation says that this sum
is the coefficient of a diagonal row multiplication by $-y_i$, hence
$-(\sum y_i)J_0$. All terms except the last two columns vanish; the
next-to-last term is $-J_{11}$ because its two last columns are reversed.
These identities are polynomial determinant identities and hold even
where $J_0=0$.

It follows from the table that the normalized numerator correction is
\begin{align}\label{tec:eq:numeratorcorr}
 -\frac{(K_m^-)''K_\ell^++K_m^-(K_\ell^+)''}{24}
 -\frac{(m+3\ell)Y+(\ell+3m)V}{24}K_m^-K_\ell^+
 -\frac{(K_m^-)'(K_\ell^+)'}4.
\end{align}

\subsection{The geometric denominator correction}
The remaining factors must not be omitted. Their expansions are
\begin{align*}
 \prod_i c_h(y_i)^{-1}&=1+\frac{h^2Y}{8}+O(h^4),\\
 \prod_a s_h(v_a)^{-1}&=1+\frac{h^2V}{24}+O(h^4),\\
 D_h(\by)^{-1}&=1+\frac{h^2(m-1)Y}{12}+O(h^4),\\
 D_h(\bv)^{-1}&=1+\frac{h^2(\ell-1)V}{12}+O(h^4),\\
 \prod_{i,a}[4-h^2(\phi_h(y_i)+\phi_h(v_a))]^{-1}
 &=4^{-m\ell}\left(1+\frac{h^2(\ell Y+mV)}4+O(h^4)\right).
\end{align*}
For example $\phi_h(y)=y-h^2y^2/12+O(h^4)$ proves the two divided
Vandermonde expansions even at collisions. Adding these contributions
to \eqref{tec:eq:numeratorcorr} gives exactly \eqref{tec:eq:A1}.
This proves Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:first}.

Finally, rescaling the kernel determinants gives
\begin{align}\label{tec:eq:homogeneity}
 K_m^-(t;\by)&=t^{b_m}K_m^-(1;t^2\by),\\
 K_\ell^+(s;\bv)&=s^{\binom{\ell+1}{2}}K_\ell^+(1;s^2\bv).
\end{align}
The identities first follow for nonzero $t,s$ by extracting powers from
rows, columns and Vandermondes, then extend to zero by holomorphy.
Differentiation at $1$ proves the Euler-operator form.

\section{Positive Schur expansions of the outward profiles}\label{tec:sec:schur}
\subsection{A coefficientwise-positive formula}
For a strictly increasing tuple $0\le k_0<\cdots<k_{n-1}$, define the
partition
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:partition}
 \lambda_i=k_{n-i}-(n-i),\qquad 1\le i\le n.
\end{equation}
Let $s_\lambda$ denote the Schur polynomial, equivalently the quotient
$\det[a_i^{k_j}]/\Del(\ba)$ in this ordering.
\begin{theorem}[Positive outward expansion]\label{tec:thm:Schur}
For $\varepsilon=0,1$, write $K_n^\varepsilon$ for $K_n^-$ and $K_n^+$,
respectively. Then
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:Schur}
 K_n^\varepsilon(1;-\ba)=2^{b_n}
 \sum_{0\le k_0<\cdots<k_{n-1}}
 \frac{\Del(k_0,\ldots,k_{n-1})}
      {\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(2k_j+\varepsilon)!}
 s_\lambda(\ba).
\end{equation}
The series converges locally uniformly on $\CC^n$ and all its coefficients
in the variables $a_i$ are nonnegative real numbers. Its constant term is
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:Kzero}
 K_n^\varepsilon(1;\boldsymbol0)
 =2^{b_n}\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{j!}{(2j+\varepsilon)!}.
\end{equation}
For $n\ge1$, the coefficient of $a_1+\cdots+a_n$ is strictly positive.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At negative coordinates, the series for the row functions has positive
coefficients in $a$. Apply Cauchy--Binet to its power-series factorization.
For chosen powers $k_0<\cdots<k_{n-1}$, the second determinant has entries
\[
 \frac{\fall{2k_i+\varepsilon}{r}}{(2k_i+\varepsilon)!},
 \qquad 0\le i,r<n.
\]
The numerator is the Vandermonde in $2k_i+\varepsilon$, because the
falling factorials are monic polynomials of their degrees. It equals
$2^{b_n}\Del(\boldsymbol k)$. The sign in the definition of the kernel
cancels the sign of $\Del(-\ba)$, leaving \eqref{tec:eq:Schur}.

This computation may first be carried out for truncated series. Their
entire row functions converge with all derivatives on compact sets, so
the determinant divided differences converge there as well. Alternatively,
on a positive diagonal $a_i=A$ the series is monotone and bounded by the
confluent determinant limit; its positive coefficients then majorize the
series on $|a_i|\le A$. This gives local uniform convergence.
Schur polynomials have nonnegative coefficients: the branching identity
\[
 s_\lambda(a_1,\ldots,a_n)
 =\sum_{\mu\,\text{interlaces}\,\lambda}
     a_n^{|\lambda|-|\mu|}s_\mu(a_1,\ldots,a_{n-1})
\]
proves this by induction, starting with a monomial in one variable.
The identity follows from the bialternant formula after clearing its
Vandermonde. The smallest tuple is $k_j=j$, which gives the constant
term since $\Del(0,1,\ldots,n-1)=\prod_jj!$.
The tuple $(0,1,\ldots,n-2,n)$ gives $\lambda=(1)$ and a strictly
positive coefficient. For $n=1$ the same assertion uses $k_0=1$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Consequences and a useful relative error bound}
For nonnegative $\ba$,
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:positive}
 K_n^\varepsilon(1;-\ba)\ge K_n^\varepsilon(1;\boldsymbol0)>0.
\end{equation}
The inequality is strict if at least one coordinate is positive, and
every coordinate derivative of the profile is nonnegative. In particular,
$A_0(-\ba,-\bb)\ge\mathcal A_{m,\ell}$. Thus the absolute error in
Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:bound}, divided by $\mathcal A_{m,\ell}$, is a valid
relative-to-leading-term bound on an outward compact set.

Formula \eqref{tec:eq:Kzero} and the homogeneity laws immediately give
\eqref{tec:eq:Aendpoint} and \eqref{tec:eq:Aendpoint1}. This proves
Corollary~\ref{tec:cor:endpoint}. The two different triangular exponents
are now visible directly: the cosine determinant contributes $b_m$,
whereas the sine-divided-by-root determinant contributes $b_\ell+\ell$.

\section{Proof of the sharp outward critical-scale theorem}\label{tec:sec:scaleproof}
\subsection{Monotonicity at every finite size}
Multiply \eqref{tec:eq:Andreief} by $(-1)^{N\ell}$ for the outward
multiplier. Its integrand contains
\[
 \prod_{a=1}^N\left\{
 \prod_i(x_a+\epsilon_{i,N})
 \prod_j(4+\eta_{j,N}-x_a)\right\}.
\]
Every factor is positive for $0<x_a<4$. The integral is positive, and
increasing any displacement increases the integrand pointwise.
Consequently $R_N\ge1$ and it is nondecreasing in every displacement.
No assumption of distinct roots is required.

\subsection{Finite scaled coordinates}
For an outward displacement $\epsilon\ge0$, define its angular coordinate
by
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:inversecoord}
 a(h,\epsilon)=h^{-2}\operatorname{arcosh}(1+\epsilon/2)^2.
\end{equation}
Then $2-2\cos(h\sqrt{-a})=-\epsilon$. Since
$\operatorname{arcosh}(1+\epsilon/2)^2=\epsilon+O(\epsilon^2)$ as
$\epsilon\to0$, bounded $N^2\epsilon$ implies
$a(h,\epsilon)-N^2\epsilon\to0$. The same coordinate works for
$4+\eta$. Apply Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:main} to these bounded moving
coordinates and divide by its value at zero. This proves
\eqref{tec:eq:finiteprofile}, including $R_N\to1$ when $M_N\to0$.

\subsection{Necessity and escape from the critical window}
Suppose $M_N$ does not tend to zero. Along a subsequence, one fixed
coordinate has $N^2$ times its displacement at least some $a>0$;
there is a fixed coordinate after passing to a further subsequence
because $d$ is fixed. By finite-size monotonicity, $R_N$ is bounded
below by the ratio with that displacement replaced by $a/N^2$ and
all other displacements set to zero. Its limit is strictly greater
than $1$ by Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:Schur}. Hence $R_N$ cannot tend to $1$.

Now suppose $M_N\to\infty$. Fix $A>0$. For all sufficiently large $N$,
some displacement is at least $A/N^2$. The same comparison bounds
$R_N$ below by the minimum of the finitely many one-coordinate comparison
ratios. For each location, its limiting Schur series has a positive
linear coefficient in $A$, so the minimum of those finitely many limits
tends to infinity as $A\to\infty$. Given any desired lower bound,
choose $A$ first and then $N$ sufficiently large. This proves
$R_N\to\infty$ and completes Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:scale}.

The divergence conclusion is not a large-coordinate use of the compact
asymptotic theorem. It follows by comparison with arbitrarily large
\emph{fixed} critical coordinates, which is why no unjustified uniformity
at infinity is needed.

\section{The original scaling and other coordinate choices}\label{tec:sec:original}
\subsection{An explicit answer to the repository's question}
Let $\boldsymbol\tau_t=(-\tau^2,\ldots,-\tau^2)\in\CC^t$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:Ft}
 F_t^-(\tau)=K_t^-(1;\boldsymbol\tau_t),\qquad
 F_t^+(\tau)=2^t K_t^+(1;\boldsymbol\tau_t).
\end{equation}
Both functions are entire in $\tau$ and even. At repeated coordinates
they are the explicit $t\times t$ determinants
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:repeatedkernel}
 K_t^\varepsilon(1;(z,\ldots,z))
 =(-1)^{b_t}\det\left[
 \frac1{i!}\partial_z^i\partial_s^j f_\varepsilon(s,z)
 \bigg|_{s=1}\right]_{0\le i,j<t},
\end{equation}
where $f_0=C$ and $f_1=S$.

\begin{corollary}[Original uncentered formulation]\label{tec:cor:original}
Locally uniformly for $\tau\in\CC$,
\begin{align}
 \frac{H_N((x-(2-2\cosh(\tau/N)))^t)}{N^{t(t-1)/2}}
   &=F_t^-(\tau)+O(N^{-1}),\label{tec:eq:origleft}\\
 \frac{H_N((x-(2+2\cosh(\tau/N)))^t)}{(-1)^{tN}N^{t(t+1)/2}}
   &=F_t^+(\tau)+O(N^{-1}).\label{tec:eq:origright}
\end{align}
The endpoint values are
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:origzero}
 F_t^-(0)=2^{b_t}\prod_{j=0}^{t-1}\frac{j!}{(2j)!},\qquad
 F_t^+(0)=2^{t+b_t}\prod_{j=0}^{t-1}\frac{j!}{(2j+1)!}.
\end{equation}
If $\kappa_t$ is the corresponding triangular exponent, the first
uncentered correction is explicitly
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:uncentered1}
 F_t^\pm(\tau)+\frac{t}{2N}
       \left(\kappa_t+\tau\frac d{d\tau}\right)F_t^\pm(\tau)
       +O(N^{-2}).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Put $\nu=N+t/2$. In the centered theorem the coordinates are
$-(\nu/N)^2\tau^2$, and the normalization changes by
$(\nu/N)^{\kappa_t}$. Expand this analytic rescaling. The centered
finite-size correction itself starts at $\nu^{-2}$, so the entire
$N^{-1}$ term is exactly the coordinate and normalization change.
All expansions are uniform on compact sets. Formula \eqref{tec:eq:origzero}
follows from \eqref{tec:eq:Kzero}.
\end{proof}

Thus the $O(N^{-1})$ term in the originally posed coordinates is real,
but it is a centering effect. Replacing $N$ by $N+t/2$ in both the angular
scale and the normalization removes it and every later odd inverse power.

\subsection{Exact spatial displacements also have an even expansion}
Suppose the desired roots are $h^2y_i$ and $4-h^2v_a$, rather than
\eqref{tec:eq:roots}. Inverting $\phi_h$ near zero gives
\[
 \widehat y=y+\frac{h^2y^2}{12}+O(h^4),\qquad
 \phi_h(\widehat y)=y.
\]
The inverse is analytic in $h^2$ and locally uniform in $y$. Therefore
the exact spatial-coordinate model also has an even convergent expansion.
Its first correction is
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:spatialcorrection}
 A_1^{\mathrm{spatial}}=A_1+
 \frac1{12}\left(\sum_i y_i^2\partial_{y_i}
                 +\sum_a v_a^2\partial_{v_a}\right)A_0.
\end{equation}
This demonstrates that evenness is not confined to a trigonometric
notation for the roots. The center of the determinant size, however,
is essential to the stated form.

\section{Worked examples and checks on the constants}\label{tec:sec:examples}
\subsection{One root at the left endpoint}
For $m=1$, $\ell=0$, $\nu=N+1/2$ and $\kappa=0$, the exact formula is
\[
 Z_N(y)=\frac{\cos\sqrt y}{\cos(h\sqrt y/2)}.
\]
Hence $A_0=\cos\sqrt y$ and $A_1=(y/8)\cos\sqrt y$.
At $y=-\tau^2$ this is $\cosh\tau$ divided by
$\cosh(h\tau/2)$, and the limiting profile is $F_1^-(\tau)=\cosh\tau$.
This also makes the sharp $N^{-2}$ threshold visible without determinants.

\subsection{One root at the right endpoint}
For $m=0$, $\ell=1$,
\[
 Z_N(v)=\frac{2\sin\sqrt v/\sqrt v}{\sinc(h\sqrt v/2)}.
\]
Thus $A_0=2\sin\sqrt v/\sqrt v$ and $A_1=vA_0/24$.
The outward profile is $F_1^+(\tau)=2\sinh\tau/\tau$, with value $2$
at zero. These two one-root examples verify both boundary normalizations.

\subsection{One root at each endpoint}
For $m=\ell=1$, write $C=C(1,y)$ and $S=S(1,v)$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:11}
 A_0=CS,\qquad
 A_1=\left(\frac y4+\frac v6\right)CS
                  -\frac14 C_t(1,y)S_t(1,v).
\end{equation}
The mixed term generally does not vanish. At $y=v=0$ the exact
unsigned endpoint determinant is $N+1$, and the centered normalization
with $\nu=N+1$ is identically $1$; accordingly $A_1(0,0)=0$.

\subsection{Two coincident roots at an endpoint}
The two repeated-root profiles from \eqref{tec:eq:repeatedkernel} simplify to
\begin{align}
 F_2^-(\tau)&=\frac12+\frac{\sinh(2\tau)}{4\tau},\label{tec:eq:F2minus}\\
 F_2^+(\tau)&=\frac{\sinh(2\tau)-2\tau}{\tau^3}.\label{tec:eq:F2plus}
\end{align}
The singularities at zero are removable; the values there are $1$ and
$4/3$, respectively. To derive them, evaluate the $2\times2$ determinant
in \eqref{tec:eq:repeatedkernel}, or differentiate the two elementary
hyperbolic row functions before taking the coincident limit.

For example, with two roots at each endpoint, all exactly at the
endpoints, one has
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:22exact}
 H_N(x^2(x-4)^2)=\frac{(N+1)(N+2)^2(N+3)}{12}.
\end{equation}
This follows by inserting the endpoint jets in the four-row
Christoffel determinant and factoring the resulting polynomial.
Writing $\nu=N+2$ gives
$Z_N=(1-h^2)/12$, agreeing with both
\eqref{tec:eq:Aendpoint} and \eqref{tec:eq:Aendpoint1}.

\subsection{A genuinely confluent two-cluster numerical example}
Take $m=\ell=2$, $\by=(-1,-1)$ and $\bv=(-2,-2)$.
The first two coefficients, computed from the confluent kernels, are
\[
 A_0=0.1741166983662596749147659\ldots,\qquad
 A_1=-0.7183984032902893460064576\ldots.
\]
The following are high-precision evaluations, not interval enclosures.
Here $h=1/(N+2)$.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrr@{}}\toprule
$N$ & $|Z_N-A_0|$ & $|Z_N-A_0-h^2A_1|$ &
 $(Z_N-A_0-h^2A_1)/h^4$\\\midrule
16 & $2.2027646\cdot10^{-3}$ & $1.4514422\cdot10^{-5}$ & 1.52366601\\
32 & $6.2030784\cdot10^{-4}$ & $1.1440675\cdot10^{-6}$ & 1.52885861\\
64 & $1.6484093\cdot10^{-4}$ & $8.0651949\cdot10^{-8}$ & 1.53034944\\
128 & $4.2503422\cdot10^{-5}$ & $5.3595798\cdot10^{-9}$ & 1.53074959\\
\bottomrule\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The corrected residual displays the predicted fourth-order behavior.
The proof of the error order is Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:main}, not the table.

\section{Computation, reproducibility, and the verification boundary}\label{tec:sec:verification}
\subsection{Two independent determinant paths}
The accompanying \texttt{code/verify.py} (shipped as
\nolinkurl{code/04-endpoint-confluence-verify.py}) uses exact rational arithmetic for
its algebraic tests. One path forms the original Catalan moment matrix
from \eqref{tec:eq:HN}. A second path uses the fixed-size confluent
Christoffel determinant. The latter obtains Taylor jets from
\eqref{tec:eq:pn}: if $p_n(c+u)=\sum_r a_{n,r}u^r$, then
\[
 a_{n+1,r}=(c-2)a_{n,r}+a_{n,r-1}-a_{n-1,r},
 \qquad a_{n,-1}=0.
\]
No trigonometric root extraction is needed for these exact tests.
The endpoint jet identities checked separately are
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:endpointjets}
 \frac{p_n^{(r)}(0)}{r!}=(-1)^{n+r}\binom{n+r}{2r},\qquad
 \frac{p_n^{(r)}(4)}{r!}
   =\frac{2n+1}{2r+1}\binom{n+r}{2r},
\end{equation}
with zero values when $r>n$. Both follow from the recurrence, or the
trigonometric formulas expanded at the endpoints.

The code also evaluates the normalized kernel jets by the convergent
entire series
\begin{equation}\label{tec:eq:jetseries}
 \frac1{q!}\partial_y^q\partial_t^r f_\varepsilon(t,y)
 =\sum_{\substack{k\ge q\\2k+\varepsilon\ge r}}
 \frac{(-1)^k\binom{k}{q}y^{k-q}t^{2k+\varepsilon-r}}
 {(2k+\varepsilon-r)!}.
\end{equation}
This handles zero coordinates and repeated roots without dividing by
small root differences. High-precision tests use 100 decimal digits.
They include inward roots, complex roots, exact endpoints, and mixed
repeated-root blocks of total degree up to eight in the endpoint
coefficient checks.

\subsection{Executed checks}
The run recorded in \texttt{data/verification.json} and
\texttt{data/verification.txt} completed the following checks.

\noindent[Merge note: those two records are shipped as
\nolinkurl{data/04-endpoint-confluence-verification.json} and
\nolinkurl{data/04-endpoint-confluence-verification.txt}.  In this
report the unprefixed \nolinkurl{data/verification.json} and
\nolinkurl{data/verification.txt} are Part~I's recorded outputs; see
Section~\ref{tec:app:archive} before rerunning anything.]
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{.77\textwidth}r@{}}\toprule
Check & Count\\\midrule
Exact original determinants versus confluent Christoffel determinants &104\\
Exact endpoint Taylor-jet identities &378\\
Exact leading constants of centered coefficient minors &35\\
Exact vanishing of degree-one centered minors &60\\
Exact second-order one-cluster minor ratios &108\\
Exact mixed minor ratios $-1/4$ &25\\
High-precision first-correction residual checks &40\\
High-precision analytic evenness checks &6\\
Analytic continuation formula versus Christoffel evaluation &6\\
High-precision endpoint leading and correction constants &48\\\midrule
\textbf{Total; all passed} &\textbf{810}\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The first six rows comprise 710 exact-arithmetic checks. The last four
rows comprise 100 high-precision diagnostic checks. A check count measures
executed identities, not distinct universal results. In particular,
finite tests of the table of minors do not replace the all-parameter
proof from \eqref{tec:eq:master}.

\subsection{Computing higher coefficients}
For a specified order $J$, expand the finite product
\eqref{tec:eq:Rgenerator} through degree $2J$. Convert its separately
symmetric coefficients to Schur polynomials, or directly to the
coefficient minors of the centered exponential matrix. Replace the
corresponding alternants by kernel determinants with the indicated
increased derivative orders. Finally multiply by the truncated reciprocal
series of $c_h,s_h,D_h$ and the cross factors in
\eqref{tec:eq:Zexact}. Only finite operations are needed for each $J$.

This is a constructive mathematical algorithm, but the shipped script
is a verification implementation for the leading and first correction,
not a fully optimized arbitrary-order engine. The bound in
Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:bound} can be applied once the desired exact coefficients
or certified enclosures for them have been computed. The present
high-precision implementation does not perform outward-rounded interval
arithmetic.

\subsection{A formalization route}
No Lean or Rocq file is claimed for this article. A feasible formalization
would begin with the polynomial Vandermonde identity underlying
\eqref{tec:eq:master}, the first coefficient table over $\mathbb Q$, and
\eqref{tec:eq:Jrelations}. Formal power series can express the exact
parity and coefficient algorithm independently of analysis. A second
layer would prove the Catalan moment functional and the Christoffel
identity. Only the final layer needs holomorphic divided differences,
uniform convergence, and the Cauchy remainder estimate. Keeping those
layers separate would make the new algebra reusable for other moment
families.

\section{Further research questions and proposed next steps}\label{tec:sec:future}
The following are research directions, not assertions that every item
is globally open in the published literature.

\subsection{Nonendpoint collisions on the reciprocal-size scale}\label{tec:x:q1}
The unsolved second portion of the repository's question lets two
nonendpoint roots approach at order $N^{-1}$. Construct a uniform
counterpart of the present theorem with oscillatory sector amplitudes
retained. A useful first target is two equal-multiplicity roots with
spectral coordinates $z e^{\pm\tau/N}$ and $z\ne\pm1$, allowing $z$
on the unit circle. Here different exponential sectors can compete;
the endpoint factorization alone does not settle their cancellation.

\noindent[Added 29 September 2026, batch 55: answered by Part~V
(Theorems~\ref{ic:thm:main} and~\ref{ic:thm:pair}), in the centred
coordinates $2+2\cos(\theta\pm\tau/(N+t))$, with $z=e^{i\theta}$ on the unit
circle and the oscillatory sectors retained; off the circle one sector
dominates exponentially.  In the coordinates $ze^{\pm\tau/N}$ proposed here
the $N^{-1}$ term gains a non-oscillatory summand
(Remark~\ref{ic:rem:coordinates}, an observation of this merge).]

\subsection{Endpoint clusters in the presence of fixed interior roots}\label{ic:x:tecq2}
Add a fixed polynomial multiplier whose roots avoid $0$ and $4$.
Determine the correct effective size and the influence of its boundary
amplitudes on evenness. A finite perturbation of the Jacobi recurrence
may alter the amplitude without changing the local oscillatory scale.
An exact criterion for disappearance of odd powers would separate
structural symmetry from a choice of normalization.

\subsection{Higher interaction operators}
The quadratic generator produces the universal mixed term $-1/4$.
Compute the quartic and sextic operators in a minimal basis of Euler
operators and symmetric coordinate polynomials. Determine whether the
logarithm of the coefficient generator yields a particularly simple
connected-interaction expansion. Its elementary factors already show
that only even orders can occur, but do not automatically give the most
compact differential representation.

\subsection{Multiplicities growing with determinant size}\label{gs:x:tecq4}
The theorem keeps $m,\ell$ fixed. Find a regime $m=m_N$, $\ell=\ell_N$
in which the factorized leading profile or the first correction remains
uniform. The explicit bound here is deliberately too coarse to locate a
sharp growth threshold. Such a threshold should account simultaneously
for the number of pair interactions, growth of the confluent kernels,
and the shrinking domain on which an effective relative error is small.

\noindent[Added 29 September 2026, batch 57: partly answered by Part~VI,
for $\ell=0$ and the cluster $x^m(x+\varepsilon)$ with one outward
displacement $\varepsilon\ge0$: the ratio to $H_N(x^{m+1})$ tends to $1$
exactly when $\varepsilon N(N+m+1)/(4m+2)\to0$, for every growth of $m$
(Corollary~\ref{gs:cor:invisibility}).  For fixed $m$ this is the
threshold $N^2\varepsilon\to0$ of Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:scale}, and when
$N^2\varepsilon\to y$ the limit is ${}_0F_1(;m+\tfrac12;y/4)$
\eqref{gs:eq:fixed-m}; for $N,m\to\infty$ the fixed-$m$ profile is
replaced by $e^\tau$ with the corrections of
Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:hierarchy}, on the critical scales of
Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:overview}.  Several displaced roots, $\ell>0$, the
first correction in the sense of Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:main} and an
approximation uniform in $1\le m\le N$ (Section~\ref{gs:x:bridge})
remain open (Section~\ref{gs:sec:answers}).]

\subsection{Other Jacobi endpoint exponents}
Replace the Catalan weight by $x^\alpha(4-x)^\beta$ with
$\alpha,\beta>-1$. Determine which effective size optimizes convergence,
and whether an exact even analytic interpolation survives beyond the
half-integer trigonometric cases. The appropriate limiting kernels are
Bessel-type, so the classical hard-edge theory supplies context; the
specific objective is an explicit collision-uniform coefficient and
remainder calculus analogous to the one proved here.

\subsection{Zeros of inward confluent profiles}
For positive scaled coordinates, the kernel determinants can vanish.
Classify real zeros of the one-parameter repeated-root profiles and
establish simplicity or interlacing where true. Holomorphic convergence
and a nonzero derivative at a limiting zero would then give quantitative
locations of zeros of the finite Catalan determinant. The absolute
first-correction formula remains meaningful there even when a relative
formula would be singular.

\subsection{Degeneration of finite recurrences}
For each fixed multiplier the repository gives a finite exponential-sector
recurrence description. Explain how those recurrences degenerate under
the present moving-root limit and recover differential equations for the
profile determinants. A rigorous bridge between coalescing characteristic
roots and the centered even series would connect the repository's
algebraic and asymptotic descriptions rather than treating them separately.

\subsection{Sharper analytic bounds and stable numerical evaluation}
Locate the nearest genuine singularities of the analytic interpolation
in $h$, distinguishing removable singularities from denominator zeros.
Use that geometry to improve \eqref{tec:eq:bound} and select a practical
truncation order. Develop interval implementations using divided
differences or jets so that collisions are handled without loss of
significance. This would convert the available analytic error estimates
into end-to-end numerical certificates.

\subsection{Rational modifications and Cauchy-transform blocks}
Allow poles of a rational multiplier away from the integration interval,
with selected poles approaching the endpoints on the same scale.
Characteristic-polynomial ratio identities suggest determinant blocks
involving Cauchy transforms~\cite{StrahovFyodorov2003,AkemannFyodorov2003}. Determine which sign, centering,
and evenness statements survive, and what replaces the monotonicity
argument for the sharp outward stability criterion.

\subsection{A machine-checked coefficient and confluence library}
Formalize the centered alternant and the second-order interaction formula
first, then implement a verified finite coefficient generator. A reusable
library should state its divided-difference confluence conventions
explicitly and track every factorial. The final target is a checked
interface connecting exact rational algebra, analytic remainder bounds,
and certified evaluations of the confluent kernels.

\section{Conclusion}\label{tec:sec:conclusion}
The repository's endpoint moving-root question has an explicit,
collision-uniform answer for every fixed multiplicity, with a simultaneous
two-endpoint extension. The principal structural fact is exact evenness
in $(N+d/2)^{-1}$ after the natural sign and power are removed.
The two endpoints decouple in the leading term but interact at the next
order through a computable differential operator. Positive Schur
coefficients turn the asymptotic profile into a sharp necessary-and-sufficient
criterion for stability under outward perturbations.

The scope remains precise: the work proves the endpoint results stated
here, not a general solution of all root collisions, all recurrence
resonances, or hard-edge universality for arbitrary weights. The result
is a conventional mathematical manuscript with explicit tests; neither
formal verification nor worldwide priority is asserted.

\section{A sign and normalization audit}\label{tec:app:audit}

This section is Appendix~A of the manuscript.

The following elementary checks are useful when porting the formulas.
For one multiplier factor, \eqref{tec:eq:Christoffel} says
$H_N(x-c)=(-1)^Np_N(c)$. Thus
\[
 H_N(x)=1,\qquad H_N(x-4)=(-1)^N(2N+1).
\]
For two endpoint factors the same formula and
$p_n(0)=(-1)^n$, $p_n(4)=2n+1$ give
$H_N(x(x-4))=(-1)^N(N+1)$.
For two repeated lower factors the derivative determinant gives
$H_N(x^2)=N+1$; for two upper factors it gives
\[
 H_N((x-4)^2)=\frac{(N+1)(2N+1)(2N+3)}3.
\]
These identities include $N=0$. They fix the asymmetric triangular
powers and the factor $2^\ell$ at the right endpoint. They also verify
that derivatives in a repeated-root block must be divided by their
factorials, whereas the derivative columns in \eqref{tec:eq:Km} and
\eqref{tec:eq:Kp} are \emph{not} factorial-normalized.

At a tuple of all zero coordinates, the kernel coefficient matrix has
entries $(-1)^i\fall{2i+\varepsilon}{j}/(2i+\varepsilon)!$.
Its determinant has sign $(-1)^{b_n}$ and nonzero magnitude
$2^{b_n}\prod i!/(2i+\varepsilon)!$, exactly as used in
\eqref{tec:eq:Kzero}. This is a direct independent derivation of the
endpoint constant without evaluating a Selberg integral.

file. After installing \texttt{requirements.txt}, run
\texttt{python code/verify.py}; the script writes to \texttt{data/}
regardless of the working directory. Exact tests use the standard library;
\texttt{mpmath} is used only for numerical diagnostics. Rebuild with
\texttt{latexmk -pdf article.tex}, or repeated \texttt{pdflatex} runs.
No external images or custom font files are required.

the arrival commit (the checksum list was verified at placement and
retired).

The manuscript contributes, in the order printed here: the repository
question, what is classical, and why phases must be retained
(Section~\ref{ic:sec:context}); the Catalan normalization and a
self-contained Christoffel reduction (Section~\ref{ic:sec:normalization});
the one-cluster theorem, an exact sector expansion with convergent
collision-uniform amplitudes, and the values and symmetries of its universal
profile (Section~\ref{ic:sec:main}); its proof by centred exponential
alternants (Section~\ref{ic:sec:proof}); the first-correction operator and an
all-orders coefficient construction (Section~\ref{ic:sec:first}); an explicit
Cauchy remainder (Section~\ref{ic:sec:bound}); several separated clusters
(Section~\ref{ic:sec:multi}); the dominant sectors inside the moment interval
(Section~\ref{ic:sec:bulk}); sine-kernel duality and Gram positivity
(Section~\ref{ic:sec:kernel}); the complete first-order law of two
equal-multiplicity roots and its identification with the oscillatory Jacobi
(kissing-polynomial) determinant (Section~\ref{ic:sec:pair}); the profile at
large real frequency (Section~\ref{ic:sec:frequency}); the transport of
finite-size zeros and its convergent Laurent-phase expansion
(Section~\ref{ic:sec:zeros}); explicit examples, including periodic and
quasiperiodic scaled zero motion (Section~\ref{ic:sec:examples}); its checks
and their evidentiary status (Section~\ref{ic:sec:checks}); what is resolved
and what is not (Section~\ref{ic:sec:scope}); nine research directions
(Section~\ref{ic:sec:research}); a conclusion; a proof-dependency map
(Section~\ref{ic:app:dependency}); and a normalization checklist
(Section~\ref{ic:app:checklist}).  Every result, proof, example, remark,
limitation and question of the manuscript is printed.  Where it re-derives
something Parts~I--IV already prove, the overlap is marked
(Section~\ref{ic:sec:merge}).

The manuscript's own abstract reads:
\begin{quote}\small
The Catalan-Hankel reports in \textsc{ProveIt} ask for uniform limits when
roots of a polynomial multiplier collide away from the endpoints of the
moment interval. We resolve this fixed-degree, reciprocal-size collision
problem, including arbitrary internal coincidences and several separated
clusters. With effective size $\nu=N+d/2$, the determinant has an exact
finite sum of exponential sectors whose amplitudes are holomorphic in
$\nu^{-1}$. We give every leading amplitude, a closed first-correction
operator, an all-orders coefficient construction, and an explicit
compact-uniform Cauchy remainder bound. For two equal-multiplicity roots
colliding inside $(0,4)$, the leading limit is a classical oscillatory
Jacobi Hankel determinant, but its first correction retains the phase
$\sin(2\nu\theta)$. At each simple limiting zero we obtain a convergent
phase-dependent expansion of the finite-size zero, not merely a formal
asymptotic series. A completely explicit two-simple-root case exhibits
periodic or quasiperiodic scaled zero motion. Classical Christoffel,
sine-kernel, and kissing-polynomial results are credited and rederived
where needed. The written proofs are accompanied by 392 exact-arithmetic
and 126 high-precision checks.
\end{quote}
Its title page carried this scope box:
\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Scope and status.} The resolved question is the nonendpoint
moving-root problem explicitly proposed in the repository and its
endpoint continuation. This is an unrefereed manuscript, not a Lean or
Rocq formalization. The contribution is the proved uniform finite-size
and zero-transport package; no claim of a first discovery of classical
bulk limits, kissing-polynomial parity, or worldwide priority for every
formula is made.
\end{quote}

\begin{center}
\fcolorbox{muted}{lightblue}{\parbox{\dimexpr\textwidth-2\fboxsep-2\fboxrule\relax}{%
\textbf{Status (manuscript~03).}
This is an unrefereed, AI-assisted manuscript with conventional written
proofs, not a Lean or Rocq formalization and not an interval-certified
computation.  It resolves the nonendpoint moving-root question of
Sections~\ref{tec:x:rcq4} and~\ref{tec:x:q1} for fixed multiplier degree and
fixed nonendpoint bases.  Classical ingredients are credited, not presented
as new: the Catalan moment measure and its orthogonal polynomials, the
Christoffel polynomial-modification identity, its bilinear kernel variant
and divided-difference confluence (Krattenthaler~\cite{Krattenthaler2023};
re-proved in the present normalization), determinants of orthogonal-polynomial
kernels and bulk (sine-kernel) universality for characteristic-polynomial
correlations (Strahov--Fyodorov~\cite{StrahovFyodorov2003}, context only),
and the oscillatory Jacobi Hankel determinant of the kissing-polynomial
literature with its even-degree nondegeneracy, odd-degree degeneracy and
large-frequency analysis (Celsus--Dea\~no--Huybrechs--Iserles~%
\cite{CelsusDeanoHuybrechsIserles2022}), whose versions are independently
proved here in the needed normalization; the centred exponential Vandermonde
is an elementary repackaging of the ordinary one.  The claimed contribution
relative to the inspected reports is the collision-uniform convergent
amplitude construction, its explicit first coefficient and compact Cauchy
bound, the separated-cluster constants, and the convergent phase-dependent
finite-size zero expansion.  The literature audit was bounded: the full text
of the kissing-polynomial paper could not be retrieved, so no
theorem-by-theorem comparison with it was made, and worldwide priority for
every identity, coefficient or corollary is not established.  Not claimed:
uniformity as a base approaches an endpoint or another base; growing degree,
unbounded scaled coordinates varying with~$N$, or zero indices growing
with~$N$; a classification of nonsimple zeros or of all recurrence
resonances; constant-coefficient recurrences for a moving multiplier $q_N$;
validation of every theorem or file of the repository.  No theorem of the
unrefereed source reports is used as an unproved premise.  The 518 recorded
assertions (392 exact, 126 at 100 digits) are finite audits; the CSV
experiments are not certificates, and numerical root decimals are not
certified isolating intervals.  No proof assistant was run, nothing in
Part~V is formalized, and its placement beside a Lean project confers no
formal status.  (Sources: the manuscript, its delivery README, and its
source audit and proof status,
\nolinkurl{05-interior-collision-SOURCES.md} and
\nolinkurl{05-interior-collision-PROOF_STATUS.md}.)}}
\end{center}

\subsection{Reading conventions}\label{ic:sec:reading}
Inside Sections~\ref{ic:sec:context}--\ref{ic:app:checklist}, ``this
paper'', ``the present manuscript'' and ``here'' mean manuscript~03 as
printed in Part~V; ``the repository'', ``the inspected repository reports''
and ``the report'' mean Parts~I--III at the pin \texttt{e1afd75e3} (its
``root-collision report'' is Part~II), and ``the endpoint continuation''
means Part~IV.  The manuscript cites Part~II's Section~24.4 by this report's
number, which did not change (Section~\ref{tec:x:rcq4}); its citation of the
standalone Part~IV manuscript's Section~13.1 is printed as a
cross-reference to Section~\ref{tec:x:q1}.  ``The package'' means the
delivered archive, whose files are shipped under the prefixed names listed in
Section~\ref{ic:sec:source}.  The manuscript's two appendices are printed as
Sections~\ref{ic:app:dependency} and~\ref{ic:app:checklist}, so that Part~I's
appendices keep their letters.  Its Sections~1--17 are
Sections~\ref{ic:sec:context}--\ref{ic:sec:conclusion}, so a manuscript
number $n.j$ of a section, theorem, lemma, proposition, corollary, example,
remark or equation is printed as $(n+61).j$ (for example, its Theorem~3.1 is
Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:main} and its Theorem~10.1 is Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:pair}),
and its Appendices~A and~B are Sections~\ref{ic:app:dependency}
and~\ref{ic:app:checklist}; its two data tables are
Tables~\ref{ic:tab:correction} and~\ref{ic:tab:zeros}.  The shipped
\nolinkurl{05-interior-collision-PROOF_STATUS.md} and
\nolinkurl{05-interior-collision-SOURCES.md} use the manuscript's numbers.
The manuscript cites Krattenthaler's Ramanujan Journal paper (with
arXiv:2101.04225v5) and the Strahov--Fyodorov paper; they are Part~I's
reference~\cite{Krattenthaler2023} and Part~IV's
reference~\cite{StrahovFyodorov2003}.  Bracketed paragraphs beginning
``Merge note'' are this merge's, not the manuscript's.

\subsection{Notation of Part~V}\label{ic:sec:notation}
Part~V keeps the manuscript's symbols.  Within
Sections~\ref{ic:sec:context}--\ref{ic:app:checklist} every symbol has its
Part~V meaning, except in merge notes and where a sentence names Part~I, II,
III or~IV or cites one of their equations.  The multiplier $q_N$ has only
moving roots; with all of them at one fixed base it is Part~II's
$q=\gamma\prod_i(x-c_i)^{t_i}$ with $\gamma=1$, $m=\ell=0$, and $H_N(q)$ is
Part~II's \eqref{rc:eq:HN}.  Table~\ref{ic:tab:notation} lists the symbols
that a reader of Parts~I--IV is likely to misread.  One symbol was renamed:
the manuscript's $\operatorname{shc}z=\sinh z/z$ is printed $\sinhc z$,
Part~IV's name for the same function; and the manuscript's upright
$\operatorname{Re}$ is printed $\Re$.  No normalization changed.  Four
readings need particular care: $D=\binom d2$ is \emph{not} Part~II's
$D=\deg q$, which is Part~V's $d$; the spectral base $w$ is not Part~III's
centred size $w=N+t/2$; the letter $t$ is a formal branch-counting variable
in \eqref{ic:eq:R} and in the proof of Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:multi} (there
$t_a$), but the multiplicity of each root of a symmetric pair from
Section~\ref{ic:sec:pair} on; and $Z_N$ in \eqref{ic:eq:ZN} is normalized by
$A_t(\theta)\nu^{t^2}$, not like Part~IV's $Z_N$ \eqref{tec:eq:ZN}.

\subsection{What Part~V answers, and what stays open}\label{ic:sec:answers}
Part~II's Section~\ref{tec:x:rcq4} asked, besides the endpoint limits that
Part~IV supplied, for a second version in which two distinct nonendpoint
roots approach each other at rate $N^{-1}$, with a uniform confluent limit
and error bounds; Part~IV's Section~\ref{tec:x:q1} repeated it, asking for
oscillatory sector amplitudes to be retained and proposing as a first target
two equal-multiplicity roots with spectral coordinates $ze^{\pm\tau/N}$,
$z\ne\pm1$, $z$ on the unit circle allowed.  Part~V answers both, for fixed
total degree and fixed nonendpoint bases, inside a larger statement: any
number of roots in a cluster, any collision pattern
(Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:main}), with the explicit first coefficient
(Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:first}), an explicit remainder bound
(Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:bound}) and several separated clusters
(Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:multi}).  For two simple roots the determinant is
explicit (Example~\ref{ic:ex:t1}).  For two roots of multiplicity $t$ on the
unit circle the limit is $\Phi_t$, an oscillatory Jacobi Hankel determinant,
and the $\nu^{-1}$ term keeps the phase $\sin(2\nu\theta)$
(Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:pair}); the value at $\tau=0$ is
\eqref{ic:eq:centerlaw}; off the unit circle one sector dominates
exponentially (the paragraph after Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:main}).  Unlike
Part~IV's, the expansion is not even (the remark closing
Section~\ref{ic:sec:first}).  The same theorems settle, for collisions away
from the endpoints, the caveat that closes Part~II's
Section~\ref{rc:sec:asymptotics}: the double-scaling limit is proved
directly, without \eqref{rc:eq:asymptotic}.

The coordinates differ from those proposed in Section~\ref{tec:x:q1}.  The
manuscript states its pair law only in centred coordinates; the following
translation is this merge's.

\begin{remark}[The coordinates of Section~\ref{tec:x:q1}; an observation of
this merge]\label{ic:rem:coordinates}
Fix $t\ge1$ and $0<\theta<\pi$, and put $\nu=N+t$.  For the uncentred pair
$q_N=(x-2-2\cos(\theta+\tau/N))^t(x-2-2\cos(\theta-\tau/N))^t$, uniformly on
compact sets of complex $\tau$,
\[
 \frac{H_N(q_N)}{A_t(\theta)\nu^{t^2}}
 =\Phi_t(\tau)+\frac1\nu\Bigl[t\tau\Phi_t'(\tau)
   +(-1)^t\frac{\sin(2\nu\theta)}{\sin\theta}\Psi_t(\tau)\Bigr]
 +O(\nu^{-2}).
\]
Indeed $\theta\pm\tau/N=\theta\pm\tau'/\nu$ with $\tau'=\tau\nu/N
=\tau+t\tau/\nu+O(\nu^{-2})$, and Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:pair} holds uniformly
with derivatives on a slightly larger compact set, so Taylor expansion of
$\Phi_t(\tau')$ at $\tau$ gives the extra summand, while $\Psi_t(\tau')/\nu$
changes only at order $\nu^{-2}$.  With $z=e^{i\theta}$, the spectral
coordinates $ze^{\pm\sigma/N}$ of Section~\ref{tec:x:q1} give the roots
$2+2\cosh(i\theta\pm\sigma/N)=2+2\cos(\theta\mp i\sigma/N)$, that is,
$\tau=i\sigma$ (the profiles are even, so the sign is immaterial).  So in
the coordinates proposed there the $\nu^{-1}$ term has the non-oscillatory
summand $t\tau\Phi_t'(\tau)$ besides the oscillatory one.  As in Part~IV's
Corollary~\ref{tec:cor:original}, where the $N^{-1}$ term is only the
centring, the extra summand is produced by the centring alone, here of the
coordinate.  Normalizing by $N^{t^2}$ instead of $\nu^{t^2}$ would add a
further $t^3\Phi_t(\tau)/\nu$.  (Checked numerically for this merge against
direct determinants for $t=1$, $\theta=1.1$, $\tau=0.8$ and $N=20,40,80$:
$\nu$ times the residual of Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:pair}'s terms stays near
$t\tau\Phi_t'(\tau)\approx-0.65$, and $\nu^2$ times the residual after the
extra summand stays bounded; the computation is not shipped.)
\end{remark}

\emph{Not answered.}  Nothing in Part~V is uniform as a base approaches $0$
or $4$; the bridge to Part~IV is Part~V's own first research question
(Section~\ref{ic:x:bridge}), so Section~\ref{tec:x:rcq4} as a whole, with
limits uniform across the whole spectrum, is still open there.  Part~IV's
second research question, endpoint clusters with fixed interior roots
(Section~\ref{ic:x:tecq2}), is generalized to moving interior clusters in
Part~V's Section~\ref{ic:x:mixed}, not answered.  Part~III's research
directions on endpoint factors coupled to a cyclotomic interior root
(Section~\ref{tec:x:cycendpoint}) and on uniform limits near a disappearing
characteristic factor (Section~\ref{ic:x:cycuniform}) are not discussed by
the manuscript; its theorems hold at every nonendpoint base, cyclotomic ones
included, but whether they describe the transition asked for there is not
examined.  Part~V's Section~\ref{ic:x:jacobi} restates, with a sharper
target, Part~II's research question on other eventually constant Jacobi
recurrences (Section~\ref{ic:x:rcq5}), as the manuscript says.

\emph{Consistency with Parts~II--IV} (observations of this merge).
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
\item At $\bu=\boldsymbol0$, one root of multiplicity $d$ at $c=2+z+z^{-1}$,
 $z=e^w$, the sector $k$ of Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:main} is
 $((-1)^dz^{2k-d})^N$ times $z^{(2k-d)d/2}\nu^{k(d-k)}F_k(\nu^{-1};\boldsymbol0,w)$,
 whose leading amplitude $f_{k,0}=B_{d,k}(w)S_{d,k}(\boldsymbol0)$ is nonzero
 by \eqref{ic:eq:Szero}: the rate $(-1)^dz^{2k-d}$ and the growth
 $N^{k(d-k)}$ are the rate and the exact sector degree of Part~II's
 Corollary~\ref{rc:cor:single}.  (The leading constants were not compared
 symbolically with Part~II's Theorem~\ref{rc:thm:leading} by this merge.)
\item At the central base $\theta=\pi/2$ ($c=2$), \eqref{ic:eq:centerlaw}
 has no $\nu^{-1}$ term, since $\sin(\nu\pi)=0$ for the integer $\nu=N+t$.
 This agrees with Part~III's central product,
 $H_N((x-2)^{2t})=B_{t,t}(N)=\prod_{i,j=1}^t(N+i+j-1)/(i+j-1)$
 (Theorem~\ref{cyc:thm:central}): its factors are $\nu+(i+j-1-t)$, symmetric
 about $\nu$, so it is a polynomial in $\nu$ of the parity of $t^2$, and its
 leading coefficient $\prod_{j<t}j!^2/\prod_{j<2t}j!$ equals $A_t(\pi/2)$
 by \eqref{ic:eq:st}--\eqref{ic:eq:At}.  For $t=1,2$ it is $\nu$ and
 $\nu^2(\nu^2-1)/12$.
\item Lemma~\ref{ic:lem:christoffel} is Part~II's
 Lemma~\ref{rc:lem:Christoffel} for $\gamma=1$ and Part~IV's
 Lemma~\ref{tec:lem:Christoffel}, of which Part~III's
 Lemma~\ref{cyc:lem:Christoffel} and Part~I's Lemma~\ref{lem:Christoffel}
 are special cases; with the Catalan measure, the monic orthonormal
 polynomials and their trigonometric form \eqref{ic:eq:ptrig} (Part~II's
 \eqref{rc:eq:ptrig}, Part~IV's \eqref{tec:eq:ptrigplus}) it is a fifth
 self-contained route.  Lemma~\ref{ic:lem:center} and the $\sinhc$ quotients
 of \eqref{ic:eq:L} use the centring $\alpha_j=j-(d-1)/2$ of Part~IV's exact
 centred alternant (Lemma~\ref{tec:lem:alternant}), which the manuscript does
 not cite.
\item Tables~\ref{ic:tab:correction} and~\ref{ic:tab:zeros} are rounded
 subsets (rows $N=40,160,320$) of the shipped CSV files
 \nolinkurl{finite_size_corrections.csv} and \nolinkurl{zero_transport.csv}
 (prefixed \texttt{05-interior-collision-} under \nolinkurl{data/}), which
 also have rows $N=80$; a rerun reproduces both files byte for byte
 (Section~\ref{ic:sec:checks}).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Where the merge had to choose}\label{ic:sec:merge}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
\item \emph{Placement.}  Part~V is printed after Part~IV and before Part~I's
 appendices, so that no section, equation, theorem or table number of
 Parts~I--IV moves (checked against the \texttt{.aux} file of a build of the
 committed text).  Its sections continue the numbering
 (Sections~\ref{ic:sec:intro} to~\ref{ic:app:checklist}), with the
 concordance of Section~\ref{ic:sec:reading}.
\item \emph{Labels.}  Every label of Part~V carries the prefix \texttt{ic:};
 without it, six of the manuscript's labels, \texttt{thm:main},
 \texttt{sec:main}, \texttt{sec:examples}, \texttt{eq:measure},
 \texttt{eq:R} and \texttt{eq:Jacobi}, would have collided with Part~I's bare
 labels.  Labels were added to the manuscript's four unlabelled sections
 (its Section~2, its conclusion and its two appendices) and to three of its
 research subsections, and to Part~II's Section~\ref{ic:x:rcq5}, Part~III's
 Section~\ref{ic:x:cycuniform} and Part~IV's Section~\ref{ic:x:tecq2} so that
 they can be cited; no existing label was changed.
\item \emph{Overlaps with Parts~I--IV.}  The manuscript re-derives, without
 assuming the report correct, the Catalan measure, the orthogonal
 polynomials and the Christoffel identity (item~3 above).  Printed with a
 pointer and kept.  Everything else (the collision-uniform sector expansion

 extension, the pair law and the zero transport) has no counterpart in
 Parts~I--III, which are fixed-parameter, nor in Part~IV, which is at the
 endpoints.  No statement of Parts~I--IV was changed.

 Strahov--Fyodorov references are \cite{Krattenthaler2023}
 and~\cite{StrahovFyodorov2003}; its kissing-polynomial reference is added to

 three references to the pinned repository report, the standalone Part~II
