\section{The repository question and the contribution}\label{ic:sec:context}
\subsection{A specific unresolved continuation}
Let $C_n=\binom{2n}{n}/(n+1)$ be the Catalan numbers. For a polynomial
$q(x)=\sum_r q_rx^r$, define
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Hdef}
 H_N(q)=\det\left[\sum_r q_r C_{i+j+r}\right]_{0\le i,j<N},
 \qquad H_0(q)=1.
\end{equation}
The report \textit{Shifted Catalan Hankel Polynomials} in \textsc{ProveIt}\ includes
an analysis of fixed polynomial multipliers, their exponential sectors,
and their sharp constant-coefficient recurrences (Parts~I--II above).
Part~II, Section~\ref{tec:x:rcq4}, proposes uniform limits when roots move with $N$.
Its nonendpoint version asks what happens when two roots approach one
another at order $N^{-1}$. Fixed-parameter asymptotics alone do not answer
this question: their separate coefficients can become singular as the
roots collide, while the determinant itself remains regular.

The later manuscript \textit{Two-Endpoint Confluence for Catalan Hankel
Determinants} (Part~IV above) addresses the endpoint part of that program, but explicitly
leaves the nonendpoint problem open in its Section~\ref{tec:x:q1}.
It suggests spectral coordinates $ze^{\pm\tau/N}$, with $z\ne\pm1$, and
asks for uniformity with the oscillatory sectors retained. This is the
problem solved here. We treat all fixed multiplicities, arbitrary
collision patterns within a cluster, and multiple separated nonendpoint
clusters, rather than just two simple roots.

The repository README used for scope comparison was inspected at commit
\begin{center}\small\ttfamily
 e1afd75e35a4de734d5aa47aec5cdb917b82ed3e.
\end{center}
The exact question and predecessor scope were also checked in the
standalone manuscripts available in the user's file library. The source
audit is recorded in \texttt{SOURCES.md}. This is a targeted continuation,
not an assertion that every file or every incoming report in a changing
repository has been exhaustively compared.

\noindent[Merge note: the commit above is the pin recorded in
Section~\ref{ic:sec:source}; at that commit this report's
\nolinkurl{article.tex} held Parts~I--III only.  The ``standalone
manuscripts'' are those of Parts~II and~IV, and \nolinkurl{SOURCES.md} is
shipped as \nolinkurl{05-interior-collision-SOURCES.md}.]

\subsection{What is established here, and what is classical}
Our first principal result is Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:main}. It replaces singular
fixed-root formulas by an exact finite sum of sectors with convergent
analytic amplitudes. The centering $\nu=N+d/2$ is forced by the
Christoffel columns. Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:first} evaluates the complete first
coefficient using an Euler differential operator, while
Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:bound} supplies an explicit absolute remainder.
Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:multi} extends the construction to several separated
clusters. These statements include collisions and coordinate derivatives.

The second principal result concerns zeros. For a symmetric pair of
multiplicity-$t$ roots, Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:pair} identifies the first
phase-dependent correction. Theorems~\ref{ic:thm:transport}
and~\ref{ic:thm:convergentzeros} then give the zero displacement and a
convergent expansion whose coefficients are Laurent polynomials in the
oscillatory phase. Example~\ref{ic:ex:t1} shows explicitly why an ordinary
$N$-independent first coefficient can fail to exist.

Several ingredients are classical and are not presented as new.
Krattenthaler gives the polynomial-modification determinant identity,
its confluent interpretation, and recurrence applications~\cite{Krattenthaler2023}.
Determinants of orthogonal-polynomial kernels and bulk universality for
characteristic-polynomial correlations are established topics; see
Strahov--Fyodorov~\cite{StrahovFyodorov2003}. The oscillatory moment determinant with weight
$e^{i\omega x}$ on $[-1,1]$ is the subject of the kissing-polynomial
literature, including even-degree nondegeneracy, odd-degree degeneracy,
and large-frequency asymptotics~\cite{CelsusDeanoHuybrechsIserles2022}. We explicitly identify our
limiting profile with that family. Its parity and sine-kernel
interpretations are context and tools, not claims of a newly discovered
special function or a newly solved classical parity problem.

The bounded literature audit did not establish worldwide novelty for
all finite-size coefficients. The precise claim is that the following
proofs solve the stated repository continuation and give an explicit,
self-contained package that is not supplied by the inspected
fixed-parameter and endpoint reports. No unverified theorem from those
reports is needed in the proofs below.

\subsection{Why the answer has to retain phases}
Two effects coexist. Within a collision cluster the Vandermonde
in the root locations has small factors, producing powers of $N$ after
cancellation. Independently, every orthogonal polynomial has two spectral
branches. If the base root is inside $(0,4)$, both branches have the same
absolute size and their phases cannot be discarded. For an even total
multiplicity there is one largest power of $N$, so the leading limit can
be phase-free; adjacent sectors are only one power smaller and reappear
in the first correction. For odd total multiplicity two sectors already
tie at leading order. The exact analytic decomposition keeps all of this
information without dividing by a potentially zero limiting profile.

\section{Catalan normalization and the classical determinant reduction}\label{ic:sec:normalization}
\subsection{The moment measure and its polynomials}
Put
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:measure}
 \,\dd\mu(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{4-x}{x}}
 \mathbf1_{(0,4)}(x)\,\dd x.
\end{equation}
A beta integral, or the substitution $x=4s$, gives
$\int x^n\,\dd\mu(x)=C_n$. Let
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:prec}
 p_0(x)=1,\quad p_1(x)=x-1,\quad
 p_{n+1}(x)=(x-2)p_n(x)-p_{n-1}(x)\quad(n\ge1).
\end{equation}
These polynomials are monic and have squared norm $1$. For completeness,
write $x=2+2\cos\theta$, $0<\theta<\pi$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:ptrig}
 \,\dd\mu=\frac2\pi\sin^2(\theta/2)\,\dd\theta,
 \qquad
 p_n(2+2\cos\theta)=
 \frac{\sin((n+\tfrac12)\theta)}{\sin(\theta/2)}.
\end{equation}
Orthogonality and the norms follow immediately from the orthogonality of
the half-integer sine functions on $(0,\pi)$. Analytic continuation gives
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:phyper}
 p_n(2+2\cosh w)=
 \frac{e^{(n+1/2)w}-e^{-(n+1/2)w}}{2\sinh(w/2)}.
\end{equation}
At singular spectral coordinates the right side is interpreted by
continuity, but the main theorems keep the base away from them.

\subsection{Vandermonde convention and collisions}
Throughout,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:delta}
 \Del(v_1,\ldots,v_d)=\prod_{i<j}(v_j-v_i).
\end{equation}
An empty product or determinant is $1$. The quotient of an alternating
holomorphic function by $\Del$ extends holomorphically across all
coincidences. For a determinant of row evaluations, the extension at a
block of $m$ repeated nodes $v$ is obtained by replacing its rows with
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:jets}
 \frac1{a!}\partial_v^a\bigl(\text{row at }v\bigr),
 \qquad 0\le a<m,
\end{equation}
and retaining the cross-block Vandermonde factors. We use these
\emph{normalized} derivatives everywhere. Omitting the factorials would
change the constants in several theorems.

\begin{lemma}[Christoffel reduction, in the present normalization]
\label{ic:lem:christoffel}
For $q(x)=\prod_{i=1}^d(x-c_i)$,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:christoffel}
 H_N(q)=(-1)^{Nd}
 \frac{\det[p_{N+j}(c_i)]_{1\le i\le d,\,0\le j<d}}
 {\Del(c_1,\ldots,c_d)}.
\end{equation}
The formula includes arbitrary repeated roots by \eqref{ic:eq:jets}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Andr\'eief's determinant integration identity, obtained by expanding two
determinants and integrating each term, gives
\[
 H_N(q)=\frac1{N!}\int_{(0,4)^N}
 \Del(\bx)^2\prod_{a=1}^Nq(x_a)\prod_{a=1}^N\,\dd\mu(x_a).
\]
For distinct $c_i$,
\[
 \Del(x_1,\ldots,x_N,c_1,\ldots,c_d)
 =\Del(\bx)\Del(\boldsymbol c)
   \prod_{a,i}(c_i-x_a).
\]
Replace the monomial columns in this augmented Vandermonde by the monic
polynomials $p_0,\ldots,p_{N+d-1}$; its determinant is unchanged. Multiply
by $\Del(\bx)$, likewise written using $p_0,\ldots,p_{N-1}$, and integrate.
Orthogonality forces the first $N$ columns to pair with the integration
rows and contributes $N!$, since all squared norms are $1$. The surviving
minor is $\det[p_{N+j}(c_i)]$. The product
$\prod_{a,i}(c_i-x_a)=(-1)^{Nd}\prod_aq(x_a)$ supplies the sign. This proves
\eqref{ic:eq:christoffel}. Polynomial continuity, equivalently the
holomorphic divided-difference construction, proves the confluent case.
\end{proof}

\section{The one-cluster theorem}\label{ic:sec:main}
\subsection{Coordinates and universal profiles}
Fix an integer $d\ge1$ and a spectral coordinate
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:nonendpoint}
 w\in\CC\setminus\pi i\ZZ.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, the base point $c=2+2\cosh w$ is neither $0$ nor $4$.
Set
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:moving}
 \nu=N+\frac d2,\quad h=\nu^{-1},\quad
 c_i(h)=2+2\cosh(w+hu_i),\quad
 q_N(x)=\prod_{i=1}^d(x-c_i(h)).
\end{equation}
Thus $c_i(h)=c+2\sinh(w)hu_i+O(h^2)$ uniformly on compact coordinate sets.
The nonzero derivative $2\sinh w$ shows that this describes the ordinary
reciprocal-size collision scale in the root variable as well.

For $0\le k\le d$, write
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:ke}
 r=d-k,\qquad D=\binom d2,\qquad
 e_k=\binom k2+\binom r2=D-kr.
\end{equation}
The universal profile is
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Ssubset}
 S_{d,k}(\bu)=
 \sum_{\substack{A\subseteq[d]\\|A|=k}}
 \frac{\exp(\sum_{a\in A}u_a-\sum_{b\notin A}u_b)}
 {\displaystyle\prod_{a\in A,\,b\notin A}(u_a-u_b)}.
\end{equation}
The individual summands need distinct coordinates, but their sum does
not. Indeed, Laplace expansion yields
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Sdet}
 S_{d,k}(\bu)=(-1)^{kr}
 \frac{\det\left[
       u_i^j e^{u_i}\ (0\le j<k)\ \middle|\
       u_i^j e^{-u_i}\ (0\le j<r)
      \right]_{1\le i\le d}}
 {\Del(\bu)}.
\end{equation}
The vertical bar separates blocks of columns. Consequently $S_{d,k}$ is
an entire symmetric function of all coordinates, including when $k=0$
or $r=0$. In particular $S_{d,0}=e^{-\sum u_i}$ and
$S_{d,d}=e^{\sum u_i}$.

Define the nonzero base constant
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:B}
 B_{d,k}(w)=
 \frac{(-1)^{r(r+1)/2}}
 {(2\sinh(w/2))^d(2\sinh w)^{e_k}}.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Convergent collision-sector expansion]\label{ic:thm:main}
There are functions $F_k(h;\bu,w)$, $0\le k\le d$, holomorphic near
$h=0$ locally uniformly in $\bu\in\CC^d$ and in nonendpoint $w$, for
which the following identity is exact for all sufficiently large $N$:
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:main}
 H_N(q_N)=(-1)^{Nd}\sum_{k=0}^d
 e^{(2k-d)\nu w}\nu^{k(d-k)}F_k(\nu^{-1};\bu,w).
\end{equation}
Each amplitude is symmetric in $\bu$ and has a genuinely convergent
Taylor series
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:tay}
 F_k(h;\bu,w)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}f_{k,j}(\bu,w)h^j,
 \qquad f_{k,0}=B_{d,k}(w)S_{d,k}(\bu).
\end{equation}
The coefficient functions are entire in $\bu$. On every compact
coordinate set the expansion and any fixed number of coordinate
derivatives converge uniformly for sufficiently small $|h|$.
After retaining $j\le J$, the absolute remainder in
\eqref{ic:eq:main} is bounded by a constant times
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:absolute}
 \sum_{k=0}^d
 \bigl|e^{(2k-d)\nu w}\bigr|\nu^{k(d-k)-J-1}.
\end{equation}
All these assertions include arbitrary internal collisions.
\end{theorem}

The theorem is deliberately stated with an absolute remainder. A
relative estimate obtained by dividing by a leading profile would be
false or meaningless at its zeros. Outside the moment interval a single
spectral direction may dominate exponentially; on the unit circle the
retained sectors can interfere. Formula~\eqref{ic:eq:main} handles both cases.

\begin{proposition}[Values and elementary symmetries of the profile]
\label{ic:prop:Sfacts}
With $r=d-k$,
\begin{align}
 S_{d,k}(\boldsymbol0)
 &=2^{kr}\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}j!\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}j!}
                    {\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}j!}>0,\label{ic:eq:Szero}\\
 S_{d,k}(\bu+a\boldsymbol1)&=e^{(2k-d)a}S_{d,k}(\bu),\label{ic:eq:Sshift}\\
 S_{d,d-k}(\bu)&=S_{d,k}(-\bu).\label{ic:eq:Sreflection}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The last two identities follow directly from \eqref{ic:eq:Ssubset} on the
distinct-coordinate locus, and then everywhere by analyticity. For
\eqref{ic:eq:Szero}, use \eqref{ic:eq:Sdet} with the normalized derivatives of
orders $0,\ldots,d-1$ at zero. Before dividing the rows by their
factorials, the determinant is the confluent Vandermonde at the two
spectral values $+1$ and $-1$: it equals
$(-2)^{kr}\prod_{j<k}j!\prod_{j<r}j!$. The factor $(-1)^{kr}$ in
\eqref{ic:eq:Sdet} cancels its sign. Division by $\prod_{j<d}j!$ proves the
formula. The confluent Vandermonde identity itself follows by letting
$k$ distinct spectral parameters tend to $+1$ and $r$ tend to $-1$ in the
ordinary exponential Wronskian.
\end{proof}

\section{Proof by centered exponential alternants}\label{ic:sec:proof}
\subsection{The centered Vandermonde identity}
Let
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:alpha}
 \alpha_j=j-\frac{d-1}{2},\qquad 0\le j<d.
\end{equation}
Then $N+j+\tfrac12=\nu+\alpha_j$. This equality explains the effective
size in \eqref{ic:eq:moving}; it is not an empirically fitted shift.

\begin{lemma}[Centered exponential Vandermonde]\label{ic:lem:center}
For all $v_1,\ldots,v_d\in\CC$,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:center}
 \det[e^{\alpha_jv_i}]_{i,j}
 =\prod_{i<j}2\sinh\left(\frac{v_j-v_i}{2}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Factor $e^{-(d-1)v_i/2}$ from each row and use the ordinary Vandermonde
in $e^{v_i}$. In each factor
$e^{v_j}-e^{v_i}=e^{(v_i+v_j)/2}2\sinh((v_j-v_i)/2)$, the exponential
prefactors multiply to the inverse of the row factors.
\end{proof}

Put $\sinhc z=\sinh z/z$, with $\sinhc0=1$. Define
\begin{align}
 R_k(h;\bu,w)
 &=[t^k]\det\left[
 t e^{u_i+\alpha_j(w+hu_i)}
       -e^{-u_i-\alpha_j(w+hu_i)}\right]_{i,j},\label{ic:eq:R}\\
 L(h;\bu,w)
 &=\prod_i2\sinh\left(\frac{w+hu_i}{2}\right)\notag\\[-2pt]
 &\quad\times\prod_{i<j}
 \left[2\sinh\left(w+\frac{h(u_i+u_j)}2\right)
       \sinhc\left(\frac{h(u_j-u_i)}2\right)\right].\label{ic:eq:L}
\end{align}
Here $[t^k]$ denotes the coefficient of $t^k$ in a polynomial of degree at
most $d$. The promised exact analytic amplitude is
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Fexact}
 F_k(h;\bu,w)=
 \frac{R_k(h;\bu,w)}{h^{e_k}\Del(\bu)L(h;\bu,w)},
\end{equation}
with removable singularities filled in as proved next.

\subsection{Divisibility in the inverse size and in the coordinates}
\begin{lemma}\label{ic:lem:divisibility}
$R_k$ is divisible by $h^{e_k}\Del(\bu)$ as a holomorphic function of
$(h,\bu,w)$. The quotient is entire in $h,\bu,w$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Expand the determinant by choosing the positive branch in a set $A$ of
$k$ rows and the negative branch in its complement $B$. Apart from the
row factors $e^{\sum_Au-\sum_Bu}$ and a sign, the determinant is
\eqref{ic:eq:center} with
\[
 v_i=\begin{cases}w+hu_i,&i\in A,\\-w-hu_i,&i\in B.\end{cases}
\]
Every pair within $A$ or within $B$ contributes a factor divisible by
$h$. There are exactly $e_k$ such pairs. Thus every summand, and hence
$R_k$, is divisible by $h^{e_k}$ in the ring of entire functions.

After dividing by this common power, the sum is alternating in all
$u_i$, because the original determinant has that property. It vanishes
on every hyperplane $u_i=u_j$. Holomorphic division by a linear
coordinate difference is elementary: in coordinates $a=u_j-u_i$, a
holomorphic function vanishing at $a=0$ is $a$ times a holomorphic
function, as follows from its Taylor series. Divide successively by all
the differences in $\Del$. Each subsequent vanishing continues to hold
away from the already divided hyperplanes and therefore on their
intersections by continuity. This proves simultaneous divisibility,
including at $h=0$ and arbitrary multiple collisions.
\end{proof}

At $h=0$ the denominator that remains is
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Lzero}
 L(0;\bu,w)=(2\sinh(w/2))^d(2\sinh w)^D\ne0.
\end{equation}
Therefore \eqref{ic:eq:Fexact} is holomorphic near $h=0$ locally uniformly in
all coordinates. The location of this neighborhood can depend on a
compact coordinate set and on the distance of $w$ from the excluded
spectral points.

\subsection{Recovery of the determinant and the leading term}
Factor the row denominators in \eqref{ic:eq:phyper} from the Christoffel
matrix and use $\nu h=1$. A row-sign choice with $k$ positive branches
has the factor $e^{(2k-d)\nu w}$ and the remaining coefficient is exactly
$R_k$. Also,
\begin{align}\label{ic:eq:crootdiff}
 c_j(h)-c_i(h)
 &=2\sinh\left(w+\frac{h(u_i+u_j)}2\right)
   h(u_j-u_i)\sinhc\left(\frac{h(u_j-u_i)}2\right).
\end{align}
Consequently the row denominators times $\Del(\boldsymbol c(h))$ equal
$h^D\Del(\bu)L(h;\bu,w)$. Since $D-e_k=kr$, substitution in
\eqref{ic:eq:christoffel} gives \eqref{ic:eq:main} exactly.

For distinct coordinates the centered identity also gives the useful
formula
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Fsubset}
\begin{split}
 F_k(h;\bu,w)
 ={}&(-1)^{r(r+1)/2}
 \sum_{|A|=k}\frac{e^{\sum_Au-\sum_Bu}}{
 \displaystyle\prod_i2\sinh\left(\frac{w+hu_i}{2}\right)}\\
 &\times\frac{1}{
 \displaystyle\prod_{\{i,j\}\subset A\text{ or }B}
  2\sinh\left(w+\frac{h(u_i+u_j)}2\right)}\\
 &\times\frac{1}{
 \displaystyle\prod_{a\in A,b\in B}(u_a-u_b)
       \sinhc\left(\frac{h(u_a-u_b)}2\right)}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Here $B=[d]\setminus A$, and unordered within-group pairs occur once.
The sign includes the $r$ negative row choices and the
$\binom r2$ reversed within-negative pairs; conversion of cross factors
to the convention in \eqref{ic:eq:Ssubset} gives the displayed common sign.
At $h=0$, \eqref{ic:eq:Fsubset} becomes
$B_{d,k}(w)S_{d,k}(\bu)$. The identity extends across collisions by
Lemma~\ref{ic:lem:divisibility}. Taylor's theorem for the analytic amplitudes
now proves convergence, derivative-uniformity, and the remainder order
asserted in Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:main}.

\section{The first correction and all-order construction}\label{ic:sec:first}
Set $U=\sum_{i=1}^d u_i$ and let
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Euler}
 \EE=\sum_{i=1}^d u_i\partial_{u_i}.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[First-correction operator]\label{ic:thm:first}
The first Taylor coefficient in \eqref{ic:eq:tay} is
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:first}
\begin{split}
 f_{k,1}(\bu,w)=B_{d,k}(w)\bigg[&
 -\left(\frac12\coth\frac w2+\frac{d-2}{4}\coth w\right)
     U S_{d,k}(\bu)\\
 &-\frac{2k-d}{4}\coth w\,
          (\EE+k(d-k))S_{d,k}(\bu)\bigg].
\end{split}
\end{equation}
In particular, for $d=2m$, $k=m$, and $U=0$, the first coefficient of the
central amplitude vanishes identically.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Work first with distinct coordinates. The factors $\sinhc$ in
\eqref{ic:eq:Fsubset} are even in $h$ and have no linear contribution. The
logarithmic derivative of a summand's other reciprocal factors at zero
is
\[
 -\frac12\coth(w/2)U
 -\frac12\coth w
   \sum_{\{i,j\}\subset A\text{ or }B}(u_i+u_j).
\]
Write $V_A=\sum_Au-\sum_Bu$. A direct count gives
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:samepair}
 \sum_{\{i,j\}\subset A\text{ or }B}(u_i+u_j)
 =\frac{d-2}{2}U+\frac{2k-d}{2}V_A.
\end{equation}
The cross denominator in \eqref{ic:eq:Ssubset} is homogeneous of degree
$kr$. Hence, applying $\EE$ to each summand,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:ES}
 \sum_{|A|=k}\frac{V_Ae^{V_A}}
 {\prod_{a\in A,b\in B}(u_a-u_b)}
 =(\EE+kr)S_{d,k}(\bu).
\end{equation}
Substituting \eqref{ic:eq:samepair} and \eqref{ic:eq:ES} proves
\eqref{ic:eq:first} on the distinct-coordinate set. Both sides are entire
in $\bu$, so the formula holds everywhere.
\end{proof}

\subsection{An exact finite coefficient algorithm}
Formula~\eqref{ic:eq:Fexact} gives every coefficient without estimating a
large $N\times N$ determinant. For fixed $d,k,J$, expand each entry in
\eqref{ic:eq:R} through order $e_k+J$ in $h$, extract $[t^k]$, divide by
$h^{e_k}\Del(\bu)$, and multiply by the reciprocal Taylor series of
$L$ through order $J$. All operations are finite. At repeated coordinates
use normalized row jets before taking the determinant. The divisibility
lemma guarantees that the apparent negative powers disappear.

Equivalently, away from coordinate collisions one may expand the
finite subset formula~\eqref{ic:eq:Fsubset}; analyticity then uniquely
specifies the result at collisions. This is often a convenient symbolic
route for low orders, but the divided-difference or confluent-determinant
route is preferable numerically when coordinates are close. The theorem
is an all-orders construction, not a claim that the supplied verification
program is an optimized arbitrary-order symbolic engine.

\begin{remark}[A caution about evenness]
For endpoint clusters, a suitable normalization may have an exactly even
inverse-size expansion (Part~IV, Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:main}). There is no analogous blanket
statement here. Even the central sector has a linear term when $U\ne0$,
and when $U=0$ the neighboring sectors usually contribute at order
$\nu^{-1}$. Centering removes an artificial shift but does not remove
actual phase-dependent corrections.
\end{remark}

\section{An explicit collision-uniform remainder}\label{ic:sec:bound}
The compact-uniform statement in Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:main} is already enough
for many applications. The following deliberately conservative bound
makes the analytic radius and Taylor remainder explicit.

Fix $R\ge0$ and a nonendpoint $w$, and set
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:epsilon}
 \epsilon=\min\left\{\frac14,
  |\sinh(w/2)|e^{-(|\Re w|+1)/2},
  \frac12|\sinh w|e^{-(|\Re w|+1)}\right\},
 \qquad \rho=\frac{\epsilon}{R+1}.
\end{equation}
For $e=e_k$, define
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Mbound}
 M_k=\frac{\rho^{-e}d^{d/2}
 \left[2(R+1)\exp\left(R+1+\frac{d-1}{2}(|\Re w|+\epsilon)\right)\right]^d}
 {|\sinh(w/2)|^d\bigl(|\sinh w|/2\bigr)^D}.
\end{equation}
The constants are intended as rigorous bounds, not as sharp estimates.

\begin{theorem}[Explicit Cauchy remainder]\label{ic:thm:bound}
For $|u_i|\le R$, $|h|<\rho$, and $J\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:remainder}
 \left|F_k(h;\bu,w)-\sum_{j=0}^Jf_{k,j}(\bu,w)h^j\right|
 \le M_k\frac{(|h|/\rho)^{J+1}}{1-|h|/\rho}.
\end{equation}
The estimate includes all coordinate collisions. Multiplication by the
sector weights in \eqref{ic:eq:main} and summation yields an explicit
absolute error for $H_N(q_N)$ whenever $\nu^{-1}<\rho$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $|h|\le\rho$ and $|u_i|\le R$, the elementary bound
$|\cosh z|\le e^{|\Re z|}$ and integration along a line segment show
\[
 \left|2\sinh\frac{w+hu_i}{2}\right|\ge|\sinh(w/2)|,
 \qquad
 \left|2\sinh\left(w+\frac{h(u_i+u_j)}2\right)\right|
 \ge|\sinh w|.
\]
Indeed, the change in the first expression from $2\sinh(w/2)$ is at most
$\epsilon e^{(|\Re w|+1)/2}$, and the change in the second from
$2\sinh w$ is at most $2\epsilon e^{|\Re w|+1}$. The definition of
$\epsilon$ gives the inequalities. Also $|z|\le1/4$ implies
$|\sinhc z-1|<1/2$ by its power series. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Llower}
 |L(h;\bu,w)|\ge
 |\sinh(w/2)|^d\bigl(|\sinh w|/2\bigr)^D.
\end{equation}

It remains to bound $R_k/\Del$ without dividing by small coordinate
differences. For each column in \eqref{ic:eq:R}, replace the evaluation rows
successively by Newton divided differences at
$u_1$, then $u_1,u_2$, and so on. The resulting determinant is the original
determinant divided by $\Del(\bu)$. A divided difference of an entire
function $g$ has the contour representation
\[
 g[u_1,\ldots,u_a]=\frac{1}{2\pi i}
 \int_{|z|=R+1}\frac{g(z)}{\prod_{i=1}^a(z-u_i)}\,\dd z.
\]
Every denominator on this contour has modulus at least $1$. For
$|t|=1$, $|h|=\rho$, and $|z|=R+1$, each original entry of
\eqref{ic:eq:R} has modulus at most
\[
 2\exp\left(R+1+\frac{d-1}{2}(|\Re w|+\epsilon)\right).
\]
The contour bound multiplies this by at most $R+1$. Hadamard's inequality
therefore bounds the divided-difference determinant by the numerator
in \eqref{ic:eq:Mbound} without its factor $\rho^{-e}$. Cauchy's
coefficient formula in $t$ gives the same bound for its $k$th
coefficient. Division by $h^e$ on $|h|=\rho$, followed by
\eqref{ic:eq:Llower}, proves $|F_k|\le M_k$ on that circle. The removable
singularities were already established in Lemma~\ref{ic:lem:divisibility}.
Cauchy's coefficient bound $|f_{k,j}|\le M_k\rho^{-j}$ and summation of a
geometric series prove \eqref{ic:eq:remainder}.
\end{proof}

For coordinate derivatives, apply the same theorem on a slightly larger
polydisk and then Cauchy's derivative formula on the smaller one. This
provides the derivative-uniform estimates used below, including near
zeros. The constants necessarily deteriorate as $w$ approaches an
endpoint spectral value, and no endpoint-uniform conclusion is hidden
in this theorem.

\section{Several separated nonendpoint clusters}\label{ic:sec:multi}
Let there be $s$ clusters with fixed sizes $d_a\ge1$, total size
$d=\sum_a d_a$, and base spectral values $w_a\notin\pi i\ZZ$. Assume
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:distinctbases}
 c_a=2+2\cosh w_a\quad\text{are pairwise distinct}.
\end{equation}
With the \emph{global} effective size $\nu=N+d/2$, define
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:multiroots}
 c_{a,i,N}=2+2\cosh\left(w_a+\frac{u_{a,i}}\nu\right),
 \quad 1\le i\le d_a,\qquad
 q_N(x)=\prod_{a,i}(x-c_{a,i,N}).
\end{equation}
For a sector vector $\bk=(k_1,\ldots,k_s)$, put
$r_a=d_a-k_a$, $n_{a,+}=k_a$, and $n_{a,-}=r_a$. Define the nonzero
cross-cluster factor
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:crossfactor}
 T_{ab}(k_a,k_b)=
 \frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\sigma,\tau\in\{+1,-1\}}
 \left[2\sinh\frac{\tau w_b-\sigma w_a}{2}\right]
 ^{n_{a,\sigma}n_{b,\tau}}}
 {(c_b-c_a)^{d_ad_b}}.
\end{equation}
There is no ambiguity from an ordering of roots inside a cluster; the
cluster order in the numerator and denominator is the same.

\begin{theorem}[Multiple-cluster confluence]\label{ic:thm:multi}
There are analytic amplitudes $F_{\bk}(h;\bu,\bw)$ such that, exactly for
all sufficiently large $N$,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:multi}
 H_N(q_N)=(-1)^{Nd}\sum_{0\le k_a\le d_a}
 e^{\nu\sum_a(2k_a-d_a)w_a}
 \nu^{\sum_a k_ar_a}F_{\bk}(\nu^{-1};\bu,\bw).
\end{equation}
They are symmetric in each cluster's coordinates, have convergent Taylor
series locally uniformly including every internal collision, and satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:multilead}
 F_{\bk}(0;\bu,\bw)=
 \prod_a\left[B_{d_a,k_a}(w_a)S_{d_a,k_a}(\bu_a)\right]
 \prod_{a<b}T_{ab}(k_a,k_b).
\end{equation}
The same uniformity holds after any fixed number of coordinate
derivatives. On compact sets of pairwise separated nonendpoint bases,
Taylor truncation gives the analogue of \eqref{ic:eq:absolute}, with
$k(d-k)$ replaced by $\sum_a k_ar_a$ and the corresponding exponential
weight in \eqref{ic:eq:multi}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Use the same centered columns $\alpha_j=j-(d-1)/2$ in the full
$d\times d$ Christoffel determinant. Introduce one branch-counting
variable $t_a$ for each cluster and extract $\prod_a t_a^{k_a}$.
For a row-sign choice, apply Lemma~\ref{ic:lem:center} to all $d$ signed
spectral coordinates at once.

Within cluster $a$, equal-sign pairs force
$h^{\binom{k_a}2+\binom{r_a}2}$. The root Vandermonde within that cluster
has $h^{\binom{d_a}2}$, leaving $h^{-k_ar_a}$. The coefficient is
alternating in each cluster's coordinates and hence divisible by
$\prod_a\Del(\bu_a)$ even when the coordinates collide. Every cross-cluster
factor is nonzero at $h=0$, by \eqref{ic:eq:distinctbases}: a zero of
$\sinh((\tau w_b-\sigma w_a)/2)$ would imply
$\cosh w_a=\cosh w_b$. The same is true of $c_b-c_a$.

At $h=0$, the cross factors depend only on the numbers of positive and
negative rows in each cluster, not on which coordinate carries which
sign. The sum over row-sign choices therefore factors into one
$S_{d_a,k_a}$ for each cluster. The remaining within-cluster constants
are exactly $B_{d_a,k_a}$, and the cross constants are
\eqref{ic:eq:crossfactor}. This proves \eqref{ic:eq:multilead}. Analyticity of
the quotients and nonvanishing of their residual denominators prove the
convergent expansion and the uniformity assertions as in the one-cluster
case.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Separation is a genuine hypothesis]
The theorem permits arbitrary collisions \emph{inside} each prescribed
cluster. It does not assert uniformity while two different base values
$c_a,c_b$ approach one another. Such a regime changes the cluster
structure and must be recentered as a larger cluster or analyzed with an
additional scaling parameter. Nor may one replace the global
$N+d/2$ by a different local size for each cluster without correspondingly
changing the amplitudes.
\end{remark}

\section{Inside the moment interval: dominant sectors and parity}
\label{ic:sec:bulk}
Take $w=i\theta$ with $0<\theta<\pi$. The base root is in $(0,4)$, and
all the factors $e^{(2k-d)\nu w}$ have modulus $1$. The dominant powers
are determined by the elementary concave quadratic $k(d-k)$.

\begin{corollary}[Even and odd total multiplicity]\label{ic:cor:bulk}
For $d=2m$,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:evenbulk}
 \frac{H_N(q_N)}{\nu^{m^2}}=
 B_{2m,m}S_{2m,m}+\frac1\nu\left[
 f_{m,1}+e^{2i\nu\theta}B_{2m,m+1}S_{2m,m+1}
 +e^{-2i\nu\theta}B_{2m,m-1}S_{2m,m-1}\right]
 +O(\nu^{-2}).
\end{equation}
All $B$ and $f$ are evaluated at $i\theta$, and all profiles at $\bu$.
For $d=2m+1$, the two leading sectors give
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:oddbulk}
 \frac{(-1)^N H_N(q_N)}{\nu^{m(m+1)}}=
 e^{-i\nu\theta}B_{d,m}S_{d,m}
 +e^{i\nu\theta}B_{d,m+1}S_{d,m+1}+O(\nu^{-1}).
\end{equation}
The errors are compact-uniform with coordinate derivatives. In
\eqref{ic:eq:oddbulk} the first corrections come only from these two leading
amplitudes; the next pair of sectors starts two powers lower.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For $d=2m$, put $k=m+j$; then $k(d-k)=m^2-j^2$. For $d=2m+1$, the maximum
$m(m+1)$ occurs exactly at $k=m,m+1$, and the next value is smaller by
$2$. Substitute these identities in Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:main}.
\end{proof}

The odd case need not have an $N$-independent limit even at leading
order. The even case usually does have one, but it need not have an
$N$-independent first correction. This distinction is essential in using
bulk limits to predict finite determinant zeros.

\section{Sine-kernel duality and a positive Gram representation}
\label{ic:sec:kernel}
This section identifies classical structure inside the universal
profile. The proofs also provide an independent route to its positivity
properties and an independent numerical check of its normalization.

\subsection{A bilinear Christoffel identity}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:kernel}
 K_M(x,y)=\sum_{\ell=0}^M p_\ell(x)p_\ell(y).
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Bilinear kernel formula]\label{ic:lem:bilinear}
For two tuples $\boldsymbol a,\boldsymbol b$ of length $t$,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:bilinear}
 H_N\left(\prod_{i=1}^t(x-a_i)(x-b_i)\right)=
 \frac{\det[K_{N+t-1}(a_i,b_j)]_{i,j=1}^t}
 {\Del(\boldsymbol a)\Del(\boldsymbol b)}.
\end{equation}
The quotient extends to repeated nodes by normalized derivatives.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Multiply the two augmented Vandermondes
\[
 \Del(x_1,\ldots,x_N,a_1,\ldots,a_t)
 \quad\text{and}\quad
 \Del(x_1,\ldots,x_N,b_1,\ldots,b_t),
\]
divide by $\Del(\boldsymbol a)\Del(\boldsymbol b)$, and integrate
against $\prod_{a=1}^N\,\dd\mu(x_a)/N!$.
The product of the two sign factors is $1$, so this is the left side of
\eqref{ic:eq:bilinear}. Rewrite both determinants in the orthonormal basis
$p_0,\ldots,p_{N+t-1}$. Expanding the last $t$ rows and integrating the
first $N$ rows forces identical complementary column sets, with unit
norm factors. The sum over the remaining $t$-element column sets is,
by Cauchy--Binet, $\det K_{N+t-1}(a_i,b_j)$. The identical expansion signs
occur twice and cancel. Continuity proves confluence.
\end{proof}

The finite kernel has a particularly transparent bulk expression. With
$\nu=N+t$, summing the products of sines in \eqref{ic:eq:ptrig} gives
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Kexact}
\begin{split}
 &K_{\nu-1}(2+2\cos\theta_a,2+2\cos\theta_b)\\
 &\quad=\frac1{4\sin(\theta_a/2)\sin(\theta_b/2)}
 \left[
 \frac{\sin(\nu(\theta_a-\theta_b))}{\sin((\theta_a-\theta_b)/2)}
 -\frac{\sin(\nu(\theta_a+\theta_b))}{\sin((\theta_a+\theta_b)/2)}
 \right].
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Every coincident argument is interpreted by continuity. In particular,
for $\theta_a=\theta+x/\nu$ and $\theta_b=\theta+y/\nu$,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Klimit}
 \nu^{-1}K_{\nu-1}\longrightarrow
 \frac{\sinc(x-y)}{2\sin^2(\theta/2)},
 \qquad \sinc z=\frac{\sin z}{z},\quad\sinc0=1,
\end{equation}
locally uniformly in complex $x,y$, with all fixed derivatives. The
second term of \eqref{ic:eq:Kexact} is bounded on compact coordinate sets
and is precisely one source of the oscillatory finite-size correction.

\begin{theorem}[Bilinear universal profile]\label{ic:thm:profilekernel}
For two complex tuples $\bx,\by$ of length $t$,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:profilekernel}
 S_{2t,t}(ix_1,\ldots,ix_t,iy_1,\ldots,iy_t)
 =2^t\frac{\det[\sinc(x_i-y_j)]_{i,j=1}^t}
 {\Del(\bx)\Del(\by)}.
\end{equation}
Both sides are entire with their confluent interpretations. If
$\bx=\by$ is real, the quotient on the right is strictly positive,
including after any collisions within the tuple.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply Lemma~\ref{ic:lem:bilinear} with
$a_i=2+2\cos(\theta+x_i/\nu)$ and
$b_j=2+2\cos(\theta+y_j/\nu)$. Use \eqref{ic:eq:Klimit} and
\[
 \Del(\boldsymbol a)\Del(\boldsymbol b)
 =\left(\frac{-2\sin\theta}{\nu}\right)^{t(t-1)}
   \Del(\bx)\Del(\by)(1+O(\nu^{-1})).
\]
Compare the resulting leading term with the unique central sector in
Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:main}, noting that
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Bcentral}
 B_{2t,t}(i\theta)=
 \frac1{(2\sin(\theta/2))^{2t}(2\sin\theta)^{t(t-1)}}.
\end{equation}
The constants differ by the factor $2^t$ in
\eqref{ic:eq:profilekernel}. This proves the formula first for distinct
coordinates; derivative-uniformity or analytic continuation proves it
at collisions.

For real distinct $x_i$, use
\[
 \sinc(x_i-x_j)=\frac12\int_{-1}^1e^{ix_is}\overline{e^{ix_js}}\,\dd s.
\]
The matrix is the Gram matrix of linearly independent exponentials in
$L^2([-1,1],\,\dd s/2)$ and is positive definite. Division by
$\Del(\bx)^2>0$ preserves positivity. At repeated nodes, its normalized
confluent matrix is the Gram matrix of functions
$(is)^a e^{ixs}/a!$ attached to the distinct node blocks. These
exponential-polynomials are still linearly independent: an identity on
an interval extends analytically, and repeated application of the
operators $\partial_s-ix$ separates the distinct frequencies and their
polynomial degrees. The confluent Gram determinant is therefore
strictly positive. The remaining cross-block Vandermonde factors are
squares of nonzero real numbers. This proves strict positivity at every
collision pattern.
\end{proof}

\section{Two equal-multiplicity roots: the complete first-order law}
\label{ic:sec:pair}
Fix an integer $t\ge1$ and $0<\theta<\pi$. For $\nu=N+t$ put
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:pairroots}
 c_{\pm,N}=2+2\cos\left(\theta\pm\frac\tau\nu\right),
 \qquad q_{N,t,\tau}(x)=(x-c_{+,N})^t(x-c_{-,N})^t.
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{align}
 s_t&=2^{t^2}\frac{\left(\prod_{j=0}^{t-1}j!\right)^2}
                         {\prod_{j=0}^{2t-1}j!},\label{ic:eq:st}\\
 A_t(\theta)&=\frac{s_t}{(2\sin(\theta/2))^{2t}
                              (2\sin\theta)^{t(t-1)}}>0,\label{ic:eq:At}\\
 \Phi_t(\tau)&=\frac{S_{2t,t}((i\tau)^t,(-i\tau)^t)}{s_t},\label{ic:eq:Phi}\\
 \Psi_t(\tau)&=\frac{S_{2t,t+1}((i\tau)^t,(-i\tau)^t)}{s_t}.
 \label{ic:eq:Psi}
\end{align}
The notation $(i\tau)^t$ in a tuple means $t$ repeated coordinates, not
a power. Both profiles are entire even functions of $\tau$, real-valued
on the real axis. Their values at zero are
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Phizero}
 \Phi_t(0)=1,\qquad \Psi_t(0)=\frac t2.
\end{equation}
These follow from Proposition~\ref{ic:prop:Sfacts}. Reality follows by
complex conjugation and permutation of the two repeated blocks.

\begin{theorem}[Symmetric collision and its phase correction]
\label{ic:thm:pair}
Uniformly on compact subsets of complex $\tau$, with any fixed number of
derivatives,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:pairlaw}
 \frac{H_N(q_{N,t,\tau})}{A_t(\theta)\nu^{t^2}}
 =\Phi_t(\tau)+
 \frac{(-1)^t}{\nu}\frac{\sin(2\nu\theta)}{\sin\theta}\Psi_t(\tau)
 +O(\nu^{-2}).
\end{equation}
In particular the leading limit exists without selecting a phase
subsequence, although its first correction generally oscillates with $N$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Use the one-cluster theorem with $d=2t$, $w=i\theta$, and coordinate
multiset $\bu=((i\tau)^t,(-i\tau)^t)$. Its sum $U$ is zero, so
Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:first} makes the first central amplitude correction
vanish. The adjacent sectors $k=t\pm1$ are exactly one power below the
central sector. By reflection and symmetry of the coordinate multiset,
\[
 S_{2t,t-1}(\bu)=S_{2t,t+1}(\bu).
\]
A direct calculation from \eqref{ic:eq:B} gives
\[
 \frac{B_{2t,t+1}(w)}{B_{2t,t}(w)}
   =\frac{(-1)^t}{2\sinh w},\qquad
 \frac{B_{2t,t-1}(w)}{B_{2t,t}(w)}
   =\frac{(-1)^{t+1}}{2\sinh w}.
\]
Their phase-weighted sum is
$(-1)^t\sin(2\nu\theta)/\sin\theta$. Division by
$A_t=B_{2t,t}(i\theta)s_t$ proves \eqref{ic:eq:pairlaw}. The residual
central Taylor series begins at order $\nu^{-2}$, the adjacent amplitude
corrections also start at that order after their sector weight, and all
other sectors are at least four powers below the center. The uniform
remainder follows from Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:main}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Identification with the classical oscillatory Jacobi determinant}
For $j\ge0$ define
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:oscillmom}
 m_j(\tau)=\frac12\int_{-1}^1y^j e^{2i\tau y}\,\dd y.
\end{equation}
At $\tau=0$, $m_j(0)=0$ for odd $j$ and $1/(j+1)$ for even $j$.

\begin{proposition}[Jacobi Hankel duality]\label{ic:prop:Jacobi}
The collision profile has the identities
\begin{align}
 \Phi_t(\tau)&=
 \frac{\det[m_{i+j}(\tau)]_{0\le i,j<t}}
      {\det[m_{i+j}(0)]_{0\le i,j<t}},\label{ic:eq:Jacobi}\\
 &=\frac1{Z_t}\int_{[-1,1]^t}
       e^{2i\tau\sum_jy_j}\Del(\by)^2\,\dd\by,\label{ic:eq:JacobiInt}\\
 Z_t&=2^{t^2}\prod_{j=0}^{t-1}
       \frac{(j!)^2(j+1)!}{(t+j)!}.\label{ic:eq:Z}
\end{align}
Thus $\Phi_t$ is a normalized version of the Hankel determinant studied
in the kissing-polynomial literature, with frequency $\omega=2\tau$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
In Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:profilekernel}, let all $x_i$ tend to $\tau$ and all
$y_j$ to $-\tau$. A confluent entry is
\[
 \frac{1}{a!b!}\partial_x^a\partial_y^b\sinc(x-y)
 \bigg|_{x=\tau,y=-\tau}
 =\frac{i^a(-i)^b}{a!b!}m_{a+b}(\tau).
\]
The row and column phase factors multiply to $1$, and the factorial
factors cancel upon division by the same expression at zero. This
proves \eqref{ic:eq:Jacobi}. Andr\'eief gives \eqref{ic:eq:JacobiInt} and
$Z_t=2^t t!\det[m_{i+j}(0)]$.

For the product evaluation, the monic Legendre polynomial of degree $j$
has squared norm
\[
 \frac{2}{2j+1}\frac{2^{2j}}{\binom{2j}{j}^2}
\]
with respect to $\,\dd y$ on $[-1,1]$. This follows from the Rodrigues
formula by $j$ integrations by parts and the leading coefficient
$2^{-j}\binom{2j}{j}$ of the usual Legendre polynomial. Hence
$Z_t=t!$ times the product of these monic squared norms. Simplifying the
factorials gives \eqref{ic:eq:Z}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Even multiplicities have no real profile zeros]
\label{ic:cor:positive}
For every $m\ge1$ and every real $\tau$,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:positive}
 \Phi_{2m}(\tau)>0.
\end{equation}
For real roots in \eqref{ic:eq:pairroots}, the corresponding finite
$H_N(q_{N,2m,\tau})$ is also strictly positive for every $N$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Take $t=2m$ and let the real tuple $\bx$ of length $t$ consist of $m$
copies of $\tau$ and $m$ copies of $-\tau$. The coordinate multiset
$(i\bx,i\bx)$ is the one defining $\Phi_t$. Strict positivity follows
from the confluent Gram statement in Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:profilekernel} and
$s_t>0$. For the finite determinant, $q_{N,2m,\tau}$ is the square of a
nonzero real polynomial. The modified measure $q\,\dd\mu$ is positive
and has support containing an interval apart from finitely many points.
Its moment Gram matrix is positive definite.
\end{proof}

This elementary proof does not make the positivity statement new. The
same nondegeneracy phenomenon is part of the established analysis of
oscillatory orthogonal polynomials~\cite{CelsusDeanoHuybrechsIserles2022}. Its role here is to
separate genuine moving zeros from a spurious prediction obtained by
truncating a finite-size expansion outside its uniform regime.

\section{The classical profile at large real frequency}\label{ic:sec:frequency}
We give a self-contained asymptotic argument for the odd profile zeros
needed in the next section. The subject and its parity phenomenon are
classical~\cite{CelsusDeanoHuybrechsIserles2022}; the finite-$N$ transport theorem, rather than a
new claim about the existence of kissing points, is the application.
The proof is elementary here because the integrand in
\eqref{ic:eq:JacobiInt}, apart from its exponential, is a polynomial.

Put
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Lagconst}
 L_j=\prod_{\ell=1}^j\ell!(\ell-1)!,\qquad L_0=1.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Vertex contribution]\label{ic:lem:vertex}
For $\tau\ne0$, the integral in \eqref{ic:eq:JacobiInt} is an exact finite
sum of exponentials in $\tau$ with Laurent-polynomial coefficients.
For a class of vertices with $j$ coordinates at $+1$ and $r=t-j$ at
$-1$, its leading contribution to the normalized integral is
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:vertex}
 \binom tj\frac{2^{2jr}L_jL_r}{Z_t}
 e^{2i\tau(j-r)}(2i\tau)^{-j^2}(-2i\tau)^{-r^2}
 \left[1+\frac{jr(r-j)}{2i\tau}+O(\tau^{-2})\right].
\end{equation}
The remainder has the corresponding differentiated asymptotics along
the positive real axis.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Repeated integration by parts in each variable of a polynomial times
$e^{2i\tau\sum y}$ terminates. The answer is a finite sum of boundary
jets at the vertices of the cube, with powers of $(2i\tau)^{-1}$. Thus no
unproved steepest-descent interchange is required.

To compute the first nonzero jets at one vertex, write the $j$ upper
coordinates as $1-a_i$ and the $r$ lower coordinates as $-1+b_i$. The
within-group Vandermonde squares are $\Del(\boldsymbol a)^2$ and
$\Del(\boldsymbol b)^2$, of degrees $j(j-1)$ and $r(r-1)$. The cross
part is
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:crossvertex}
 \prod_{i=1}^j\prod_{\ell=1}^r(2-a_i-b_\ell)^2
 =2^{2jr}\left[1-r\sum_i a_i-j\sum_\ell b_\ell
                    +\text{terms of degree at least }2\right].
\end{equation}
The boundary-jet functional on a monomial of degree $a$ at an upper
endpoint is $a!/(2i\tau)^{a+1}$, and at a lower endpoint is
$a!/(-2i\tau)^{a+1}$. Their sums can be computed using the convergent
Laplace integral
\[
 \int_{[0,\infty)^j}e^{-\sum v_i}\Del(\boldsymbol v)^2\,\dd\boldsymbol v
 =j!\det[(a+b)!]_{0\le a,b<j}=L_j.
\]
For the determinant evaluation, factor $a!$ and $b!$ from its rows and
columns; the remaining Pascal-type determinant
$\det[\binom{a+b}{a}]$ is $1$, by the factorization into a lower Pascal
matrix and its transpose. Scaling the Laplace parameter shows that the
same integral with an extra $\sum v_i$ is $j^2L_j$.

These identities give the leading powers $j^2$ and $r^2$ in
\eqref{ic:eq:vertex}. The linear part of \eqref{ic:eq:crossvertex} contributes
\[
 -\frac{rj^2}{2i\tau}-\frac{jr^2}{-2i\tau}
 =\frac{jr(r-j)}{2i\tau}.
\]
There are $\binom tj$ vertices of this type. All remaining terms are at
least two powers smaller within that vertex contribution. Because the
integration-by-parts expansion is finite, its differentiated remainder
bounds follow directly.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Parity asymptotics and large odd zeros]
\label{ic:prop:largezeros}
As $\tau\to+\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:evenasymp}
 \Phi_{2m}(\tau)=
 \frac{\binom{2m}{m}L_m^2}{Z_{2m}}
 \tau^{-2m^2}\left[1+O(\tau^{-2})\right].
\end{equation}
For $t=2m+1$, set $E=(t^2+1)/2$ and
$Q_t=\binom tm L_mL_{m+1}/Z_t$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:oddasymp}
 \Phi_t(\tau)=(-1)^m Q_t\tau^{-E}
 \left[\sin(2\tau)+\frac{m(m+1)}{2\tau}\cos(2\tau)
                  +O(\tau^{-2})\right].
\end{equation}
The normalized bracket has the corresponding derivative remainder.
In particular, for each sufficiently large integer $j$ there is exactly
one simple positive real zero near $j\pi/2$, and
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:largezeros}
 \tau_j=\frac{j\pi}{2}-\frac{m(m+1)}{2\pi j}+O(j^{-2}).
\end{equation}
These account for all sufficiently large positive real zeros.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The smallest exponent $j^2+(t-j)^2$ in Lemma~\ref{ic:lem:vertex} occurs
at $j=m$ for $t=2m$, and at $j=m,m+1$ for $t=2m+1$. In the even case,
the linear correction vanishes, the next vertex class is two powers
smaller, and the powers of $2$ and $i$ in the central contribution
cancel. This proves \eqref{ic:eq:evenasymp}.

In the odd case the two leading vertices are complex conjugates. Each
has the factor $Q_t/2$, and their phases combine into
$(-1)^m\sin(2\tau)$. Their opposite linear corrections combine into
$(-1)^m m(m+1)\cos(2\tau)/(2\tau)$. The next vertex class is four powers
smaller, proving \eqref{ic:eq:oddasymp} and its derivative form.

Let the normalized bracket be $g(\tau)$. Its estimate implies that any
large zero lies within $O(1/\tau)$ of an integer multiple of $\pi/2$.
On each such neighborhood, $g'(\tau)=2\cos(2\tau)+O(1/\tau)$ is bounded
away from zero for sufficiently large $\tau$. The values on the two
sides of a sufficiently large fixed multiple of $1/\tau$ have opposite
signs. Hence there is precisely one real zero in the neighborhood, and
it is simple. Writing $\tau=j\pi/2+\delta$ in \eqref{ic:eq:oddasymp} gives
$2\delta+m(m+1)/(2(j\pi/2))=O(j^{-2})$, which is
\eqref{ic:eq:largezeros}. The localization argument rules out other large
real zeros.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Order of limits]
The assertion above has fixed $t$ and $\tau\to\infty$. The collision
expansion has fixed $t$ and bounded $\tau$ as $N\to\infty$. They may be
used successively to choose any particular sufficiently large simple
profile zero and then transport that fixed zero as $N$ grows. They do
not, without further estimates, give a theorem in which the zero index
$j$ grows with $N$.
\end{remark}

\section{Finite-size zeros and convergent phase-dependent transport}
\label{ic:sec:zeros}
Write
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:ZN}
 Z_N(\tau)=\frac{H_N(q_{N,t,\tau})}{A_t(\theta)\nu^{t^2}},
 \qquad \nu=N+t.
\end{equation}
The preceding sections provide simple profile zeros for every odd $t$.
The next two results apply at \emph{any} simple zero, without requiring
its large-frequency approximation.

\begin{theorem}[First displacement of a real zero]\label{ic:thm:transport}
Suppose $\tau_*\in\RR$ satisfies
$\Phi_t(\tau_*)=0$ and $\Phi_t'(\tau_*)\ne0$. For all sufficiently large
$N$, there is exactly one zero $\tau_N$ of $Z_N$ in a fixed sufficiently
small complex disk about $\tau_*$. That zero is real and simple, and
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:transport}
 \tau_N=\tau_*+
 \frac{(-1)^{t+1}}\nu\frac{\sin(2\nu\theta)}{\sin\theta}
 \frac{\Psi_t(\tau_*)}{\Phi_t'(\tau_*)}
 +O(\nu^{-2}).
\end{equation}
The error is uniform in the phase $e^{2i\nu\theta}$ for fixed
$t,\theta,\tau_*$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Choose a small closed disk centered at $\tau_*$ containing no other
zero of $\Phi_t$. On its boundary $|\Phi_t|$ has a positive minimum.
The derivative-uniform expansion in Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:pair} gives
$Z_N=\Phi_t+O(\nu^{-1})$. Rouch\'e's theorem therefore gives one zero in
the disk, counted with multiplicity. It is simple. Since $Z_N$ is real
on the real axis, nonreal zeros come in conjugate pairs; uniqueness in
a conjugation-invariant disk makes this zero real.

The nonvanishing derivative and the $O(\nu^{-1})$ perturbation first give
$\delta_N=\tau_N-\tau_*=O(\nu^{-1})$. Taylor expansion of
\eqref{ic:eq:pairlaw} at $\tau_*$ now yields
\[
 0=\Phi_t'(\tau_*)\delta_N+
 \frac{(-1)^t}{\nu}\frac{\sin(2\nu\theta)}{\sin\theta}
 \Psi_t(\tau_*)+O(\nu^{-2}).
\]
Solving this equation proves \eqref{ic:eq:transport}. Every bound used was
uniform for the phase on the unit circle.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A genuinely convergent expansion, not only an asymptotic one}
The fast phase can be treated as an independent variable. Write
$\bu(\tau)=((i\tau)^t,(-i\tau)^t)$ and define
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Zinterpol}
 \mathcal Z(h,\zeta,\tau)=\frac1{A_t(\theta)}
 \sum_{j=-t}^t h^{j^2}\zeta^j
 F_{t+j}(h;\bu(\tau),i\theta),
\end{equation}
where the amplitudes are those for $d=2t$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Zrestriction}
 Z_N(\tau)=\mathcal Z\left(\nu^{-1},e^{2i\nu\theta},\tau\right),
 \qquad \mathcal Z(0,\zeta,\tau)=\Phi_t(\tau).
\end{equation}
The function is holomorphic for $h$ small, $\tau$ in any fixed compact
neighborhood, and $\zeta$ in an annulus about the unit circle. It is not
asserted to extend to $\zeta=0$.

\begin{theorem}[Convergent Laurent-phase zero expansion]
\label{ic:thm:convergentzeros}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:transport}, there are Laurent
polynomials $b_\ell(\zeta)$, $\ell\ge1$, whose exponents lie between
$-\ell$ and $\ell$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:zerotaylor}
 \tau_N=\tau_*+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}
 \nu^{-\ell}b_\ell(e^{2i\nu\theta}).
\end{equation}
The series is convergent for all sufficiently large $N$, uniformly in
the phase on the unit circle. More generally it is the restriction of a
holomorphic root function
$\tau(h,\zeta)=\tau_*+\sum_{\ell\ge1}b_\ell(\zeta)h^\ell$
on a product of a disk in $h$ and an annulus in $\zeta$. Its first
coefficient is
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:b1}
 b_1(\zeta)=
 \frac{(-1)^{t+1}}{2i\sin\theta}
 (\zeta-\zeta^{-1})
 \frac{\Psi_t(\tau_*)}{\Phi_t'(\tau_*)}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At every point $(0,\zeta,\tau_*)$,
$\partial_\tau\mathcal Z=\Phi_t'(\tau_*)\ne0$.
Fix a closed annulus contained in $\CC\setminus\{0\}$ with the unit
circle in its interior. Uniform Rouch\'e estimates on its compact set of
phases, or the holomorphic implicit-function theorem followed by
compactness, give a single root in a common small disk about $\tau_*$
for all sufficiently small $|h|$. The roots obtained locally agree by
uniqueness. They form a jointly holomorphic function $\tau(h,\zeta)$,
whose Taylor expansion in $h$ converges uniformly on a smaller product
domain.

It remains to prove the finite Laurent support rather than merely
holomorphy in $\zeta$. The coefficient of $h^a$ in
\eqref{ic:eq:Zinterpol} is a Laurent polynomial with $|j|\le a$, because a
sector of frequency $j$ first appears at $h^{j^2}$ and $j^2\ge|j|$.
At order $h^\ell$ in the identity
$\mathcal Z(h,\zeta,\tau(h,\zeta))=0$, the only term containing the new
unknown $b_\ell$ is $\Phi_t'(\tau_*)b_\ell$. All other terms are finite
products of earlier coefficients and derivatives of the coefficient
functions of $\mathcal Z$. By induction, their Laurent degrees are
bounded by the sum of their $h$-orders, namely $\ell$. Division by the
nonzero scalar $\Phi_t'(\tau_*)$ proves the claim. The coefficient at
order one is exactly \eqref{ic:eq:b1}, by Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:pair}.
\end{proof}

The recursive proof is also an all-orders zero-coefficient algorithm.
It uses differentiation and finite Laurent-polynomial arithmetic after
the amplitude coefficients have been computed. The statement is
stronger than saying that each phase subsequence has a formal series:
it gives one analytic root function valid on a whole annulus of phases.
It is also weaker, and more accurate, than asserting a phase-independent
power series in $1/N$.

\section{Explicit examples and a sharp failure of a phase-free correction}
\label{ic:sec:examples}
\subsection{Two simple roots: an exact formula}
\begin{example}\label{ic:ex:t1}
Take $t=1$ and $\nu=N+1$. Lemma~\ref{ic:lem:bilinear}, now a $1\times1$
identity, and \eqref{ic:eq:Kexact} give
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:t1exact}
 H_N(q_{N,1,\tau})=
 \frac{\displaystyle
     \frac{\sin(2\tau)}{\sin(\tau/\nu)}
       -\frac{\sin(2\nu\theta)}{\sin\theta}}
 {4\sin((\theta+\tau/\nu)/2)
       \sin((\theta-\tau/\nu)/2)}.
\end{equation}
At $\tau=0$ the first quotient is $2\nu$. On every fixed compact
$\tau$-set the denominator is nonzero for all sufficiently large $N$.
The profiles are
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:t1profiles}
 \Phi_1(\tau)=\frac{\sin(2\tau)}{2\tau},\qquad
 \Psi_1(\tau)=\frac12,\qquad
 A_1(\theta)=\frac1{2\sin^2(\theta/2)}.
\end{equation}
For a fixed integer $j\ge1$, the limiting zero
$\tau_*=j\pi/2$ is simple, and
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:t1zero}
 \tau_{N,j}=\tau_*+
 \frac{(-1)^j\tau_*}{2\nu\sin\theta}\sin(2\nu\theta)
 +O(\nu^{-2}).
\end{equation}
Indeed, away from removable singularities, the exact zero equation is
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:t1zeroeq}
 \sin(2\tau)=\frac{\sin(2\nu\theta)}{\sin\theta}
                  \sin(\tau/\nu).
\end{equation}
Expanding it at $j\pi/2$ reproduces \eqref{ic:eq:t1zero} independently of
the general theorem.
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}[Periodic and quasiperiodic scaled zero motion]
\label{ic:cor:quasi}
For the fixed zero in Example~\ref{ic:ex:t1}, if $\theta/\pi$ is
irrational, the set of subsequential limits of
$\nu(\tau_{N,j}-\tau_*)$ is the full interval
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:limitinterval}
 \left[-\frac{\tau_*}{2\sin\theta},
        \frac{\tau_*}{2\sin\theta}\right].
\end{equation}
If $\theta/\pi$ is rational, there are finitely many phase limits,
obtained from its periodic phase sequence. At $\theta=\pi/2$, the
fixed zeros $j\pi/2$ are exact finite-size zeros for all sufficiently
large $N$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
When $\theta/\pi$ is irrational, the sequence $e^{2i(N+1)\theta}$ is
dense on the unit circle, so its sine has precisely $[-1,1]$ as its set
of subsequential limits. This standard rotation fact also follows from
the pigeonhole approximation argument: integer multiples of an
irrational angle have arbitrarily small nonzero steps modulo $2\pi$,
whose successive multiples form arbitrarily fine nets. Formula
\eqref{ic:eq:t1zero} then gives \eqref{ic:eq:limitinterval}; its scaled error
tends to zero. For rational $\theta/\pi$, the phase is periodic.
At $\theta=\pi/2$, $\sin(2\nu\theta)=\sin(\nu\pi)=0$ since
$\nu=N+1$ is an integer. Equation~\eqref{ic:eq:t1zeroeq} then has the
claimed exact roots, with nonzero denominator for large $N$.
\end{proof}

Thus the first zero displacement need not approach any one constant
after multiplication by $N$. Replacing the phase-dependent correction
by an ordinary coefficient would lose an entire interval of possible
scaled behavior. This is already visible in degree two, and the general
Laurent-phase theorem explains its higher-multiplicity analogue.

\subsection{Two roots of multiplicity two}
For $t=2$, the $2\times2$ determinant in \eqref{ic:eq:Jacobi} simplifies to
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:Phi2}
 \Phi_2(\tau)=\frac{3(4\tau^2-\sin^2(2\tau))}{16\tau^4},
 \qquad \Phi_2(0)=1.
\end{equation}
For real nonzero $\tau$, $|\sin(2\tau)|<2|\tau|$, so the expression is
strictly positive, agreeing with Corollary~\ref{ic:cor:positive}. Its
first finite-size correction is nevertheless oscillatory:
\[
 Z_N(\tau)=\Phi_2(\tau)+
 \frac{\sin(2(N+2)\theta)}{(N+2)\sin\theta}\Psi_2(\tau)
 +O((N+2)^{-2}).
\]
Positivity of the leading profile is not a justification for suppressing
that correction when accurate values, rather than only existence of a
limit, are needed.

\subsection{A repeated root at the collision center}
At $\tau=0$, all $2t$ roots coincide, and
Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:pair} gives the particularly simple law
\begin{equation}\label{ic:eq:centerlaw}
 \frac{H_N((x-(2+2\cos\theta))^{2t})}
      {A_t(\theta)(N+t)^{t^2}}
 =1+\frac{(-1)^t t}{2(N+t)}
       \frac{\sin(2(N+t)\theta)}{\sin\theta}
 +O((N+t)^{-2}).
\end{equation}
This overlaps the fixed-root setting, but here it appears as the regular
center of an entire collision family, with a uniform neighborhood and
all coordinate derivatives. The limit has not been obtained by
substituting coincident roots into separately singular coefficients.

\section{Reproducible checks and their evidentiary status}\label{ic:sec:checks}
\subsection{What the verification program does}
The accompanying \texttt{code/verify.py} implements the Catalan moment
determinant, confluent Christoffel determinants, the universal profile,
the exact finite-sector amplitude, the first correction, and the
oscillatory Jacobi moment determinant. Its exact tests use Python's
\texttt{fractions.Fraction}; numerical tests use \texttt{mpmath} at 100
decimal digits. All 518 assertions passed in the recorded run:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.64\linewidth}r}
\toprule
Exact-arithmetic assertions & Count\\
\midrule
Moment determinant versus confluent Christoffel, $N=0,\ldots,6$ &63\\
Centered Vandermonde identities, dimensions $1,\ldots,7$ &7\\
Leading sector coefficients at full confluence, $d\le7$ &35\\
First sector coefficients at full confluence, $d\le7$ &35\\
Forced lower-order zeros in the confluent sector numerator &252\\
\midrule
Exact subtotal &392\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The 126 numerical assertions comprise 35 profile values at zero, 25
profile/Jacobi duality checks through $t=5$, 30 exact finite-sector
identity comparisons, 14 first-correction comparisons, four bilinear
sine-kernel comparisons, twelve evaluations of the exact two-root
formula, and six positive samples of even profiles.

{\raggedright\noindent[Merge note: the program is shipped as
\nolinkurl{code/05-interior-collision-verify.py}; in this report the
unprefixed \nolinkurl{code/verify.py} is Part~I's verifier.  The recorded
run is \nolinkurl{data/05-interior-collision-verification.json}, and the four
CSV files named below are shipped as
\nolinkurl{data/05-interior-collision-finite_size_corrections.csv},
\nolinkurl{data/05-interior-collision-zero_transport.csv},
\nolinkurl{data/05-interior-collision-multiple_clusters.csv} and
\nolinkurl{data/05-interior-collision-jacobi_parity_audit.csv}.]\par}

The exact coefficient audit uses rational spectral value $z=e^w=4$ and
all-collided coordinates, dimensions $d\le7$. The numerical sector audit
also includes distinct coordinates, real and complex off-circle bases,
and an interior base on the unit circle. None of these finite parameter
sets is a substitute for the all-parameter divisibility and analyticity
proofs.

The largest normalized discrepancy in the numerical finite-sector
identity comparisons was approximately $2.16\times10^{-90}$; for the
Jacobi duality it was $2.68\times10^{-92}$. The numerical derivative
check of the first coefficient used a centered difference with
$h=10^{-6}$, and its largest normalized discrepancy was
$6.62\times10^{-13}$, consistent with the deliberately finite
$O(h^2)$ differentiation error. These are high-precision consistency
checks, \emph{not} outward-rounded interval enclosures or
proof-assistant certificates.

\subsection{Reproduction and computational limitations}
From the package directory, run
\begin{verbatim}
python -m pip install -r requirements.txt
python code/verify.py --out data
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
\end{verbatim}

\noindent[Merge note: these commands assume the delivered package, which is
not the layout here, and must \emph{not} be run in this directory.  The
manuscript \nolinkurl{article.tex} and the package \nolinkurl{requirements.txt}
are not at the report root (the manuscript is printed as Part~V, and the
dependency file is shipped as
\nolinkurl{data/05-interior-collision-requirements.txt}); here
\nolinkurl{code/verify.py} is Part~I's verifier, and the shipped Part~V
program, run from this directory without \texttt{-{}-out}, would write
\nolinkurl{verification.json} into \nolinkurl{data/}, overwriting Part~I's
recorded \nolinkurl{data/verification.json}, and would add four unprefixed
CSV files.  The delivered \nolinkurl{Makefile}, shipped as
\nolinkurl{code/05-interior-collision-Makefile}, runs
\texttt{python code/verify.py -{}-out data} (Part~I's verifier, here) and
pdf\LaTeX{} on \nolinkurl{article.tex}, and its \texttt{clean} target deletes
\nolinkurl{article.aux}, \nolinkurl{.log}, \nolinkurl{.out}, \nolinkurl{.toc},
\nolinkurl{.fls} and \nolinkurl{.fdb_latexmk}; do not run it from this
directory either.  Restore the
delivered layout in a scratch copy instead (a POSIX shell, from the report
directory):]
\begin{lstlisting}
W=/path/to/scratch/interior-collision
mkdir -p "$W/code" "$W/recorded"
cp code/05-interior-collision-verify.py "$W/code/verify.py"
for f in data/05-interior-collision-*; do
  cp "$f" "$W/recorded/${f#data/05-interior-collision-}"; done
cd "$W" && uv run --no-project --with mpmath==1.3.0 \
  python code/verify.py --out data   # compare $W/data with $W/recorded
\end{lstlisting}
\noindent[Run this way on 29 September 2026 with Python~3.13.5 and
\texttt{mpmath}~1.3.0, it passed all 518 assertions in a few seconds; the
four CSV files were byte-identical to the recorded ones (CRLF, as recorded,
because the \texttt{csv} module ends rows with CRLF on every system), and
\nolinkurl{verification.json} equalled the recorded file apart from
\texttt{elapsed\_seconds} and Windows line endings.  The checks raise
exceptions explicitly, so \texttt{-O} does not disable them.]
The supplied determinant routines are small reference implementations.
A degree-$d$ Christoffel calculation replaces the original $N\times N$
determinant by a $d\times d$ determinant, with polynomial jets generated
by recurrence. At close but nonidentical nodes, cancellation can still
require extra precision; exact coincidence should use confluent jets
rather than tiny numerical separations. The Fourier coefficient
extraction used in the numerical amplitude routine is a finite
root-of-unity evaluation at high precision, not an interval algorithm.
A production implementation should adapt its working precision and,
when certification is required, use exact or outward-rounded arithmetic.

\section{What is resolved and what is not}\label{ic:sec:scope}
The reciprocal-size collision problem in the inspected repository
reports is resolved for fixed multiplier degree and fixed nonendpoint
bases. The answer is a convergent analytic sector decomposition, not
only the existence of a leading limit. Arbitrary multiplicities and
coordinate collisions are included, and several separated clusters are
covered with explicit cross constants. For symmetric equal-multiplicity
interior collisions, the phase-dependent first correction and the
convergent motion of simple zeros are obtained in closed form.

This does not amount to a solution of every nearby problem. The estimates
are not uniform as a base approaches $0$ or $4$, as the degree grows
with $N$, or as the scaled coordinate grows without bound with $N$.
Multiple or nonsimple profile zeros require another local analysis.
The results do not classify every resonance of the repository's
fixed-parameter recurrence problem. They do not assert that a moving
sequence $H_N(q_N)$ itself satisfies the same constant-coefficient
recurrence as a sequence with a fixed multiplier $q$.

The leading sine-kernel and oscillatory Jacobi structures, together with
the classical kissing-polynomial parity mechanism, are credited prior
mathematics. The new work asserted relative to the inspected repository
is the explicit collision-uniform analytic package and its finite-size
phase consequences. The manuscript's proof status and its historical
priority status are separate: detailed proofs are provided, while an
exhaustive worldwide priority determination has not been made.

\section{Further research questions and proposed topics}\label{ic:sec:research}
These are proposed continuations of the present and repository results,
not a claim that every formulation below is an open problem throughout
the published literature.

\subsection{A uniform bridge from interior collisions to endpoint confluence}\label{ic:x:bridge}
Let $\theta=\theta_N\to0$ or $\pi$ while an interior cluster collides.
The factors $\sin\theta$ in the bulk constants diverge, and the natural
root scale changes from $N^{-1}$ to $N^{-2}$. Construct one matched
analytic description that recovers this paper when $N\theta_N$ is large
and the endpoint continuation when $N\theta_N$ is bounded. A precise
first target is $\theta_N=\lambda/\nu$ with a fixed-degree symmetric
pair, retaining the collision coordinate $\tau$ and controlling the
joint $(\lambda,\tau)$ confluence.

\subsection{Mixed endpoint and interior clusters}\label{ic:x:mixed}
Allow a fixed number of roots to approach $0$ and $4$, while the
remaining roots form one or more interior clusters. The centered
Vandermonde proof already identifies the types of within- and
cross-cluster factors, but endpoint branch cancellations alter their
valuations. Derive the correct common normalization and determine
exactly which amplitudes retain even inverse-size expansions. An
explicit first mixed interaction operator would connect the two
existing continuation theories.

\subsection{Moving zero indices and mesoscopic collision coordinates}
Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:transport} fixes a simple zero before letting $N$ grow.
Determine the largest regime $j=j_N\to\infty$ for which a uniform
version of \eqref{ic:eq:transport} holds at the $j$th odd profile zero.
This requires balancing the large-frequency decay of $\Phi_t'$ with
the growth of the collision remainder, not simply substituting
\eqref{ic:eq:largezeros} into a compact-uniform estimate. Even the exact
$t=1$ equation offers a tractable test case for sharp thresholds.

\subsection{Zeros of the transport coefficient and suppressed drift}
At a simple zero of $\Phi_t$, the first phase correction disappears
when $\Psi_t$ also vanishes, or when the sampled phase has zero sine.
Classify the former condition and its relation to the determinant
identities of oscillatory orthogonal polynomials. Determine whether the
leading nonzero displacement then has a universal second-order form.
This is a question about the pair $(\Phi_t,\Psi_t)$ and finite-size
transport; basic kissing-polynomial nondegeneracy should be compared
with the existing literature before assigning novelty to a subproblem.

\subsection{Higher correction operators in a compact basis}
Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:first} uses only multiplication by $U$ and an Euler
operator. At quadratic and higher order, express the coefficients in a
minimal basis of symmetric differential operators acting on $S_{d,k}$.
The logarithm of the finite product in \eqref{ic:eq:Fsubset} gives a
systematic generator, but the most economical collision-regular form is
not supplied here. Such a form could make higher zero coefficients
practical without large determinant expansions.

\subsection{Growing degree and conditioning}
Let $d=d_N$ grow. The explicit Cauchy constant in
\eqref{ic:eq:Mbound} is too coarse to reveal a sharp threshold for uniform
convergence of a fixed number of leading sectors. Separate analytic
radius loss from numerical conditioning and from factorial growth of
the profile. A useful target is a regime in which relative errors are
controlled on compact sets avoiding profile zeros, together with an
absolute result on the full compact set.

\subsection{Eventually constant Jacobi recurrences}\label{ic:x:jacobi}
Replace the Catalan polynomials by a family whose three-term recurrence
is eventually constant. Its spectral expression generally has two
nonconstant branch amplitudes. A zero of one branch amplitude at the
base can change the sector valuation. Classify those changes and derive
an analogue of \eqref{ic:eq:first} that separates the universal centered
Vandermonde part from the finite initial perturbation. This continues a
separate question already stated in the repository's root-collision
report (Part~II, Section~\ref{ic:x:rcq5}).

\subsection{Multiple phase tori and resonant zero geometry}
In Theorem~\ref{ic:thm:multi}, interior base angles produce several phase
variables. Develop a convergent zero or critical-point expansion over
their phase torus, including resonant rational relations among the
angles. For one scalar simple zero, the implicit-function argument
extends readily, but collisions of several zeros or several constraints
may produce nontrivial singular phase loci. Determining those loci is a
more substantial target than merely repeating the one-phase proof.

\subsection{Exact certificates, interval numerics, and formalization}
The shortest formalization path is algebraic: the centered Vandermonde
identity, row-sign sector extraction, forced valuations, and the
first-coefficient identity. Formal power series can encode these steps
before complex analysis is introduced. A second layer would formalize
the Catalan moment functional and the Christoffel reduction. The final
layer needs holomorphic divided differences, Cauchy estimates, and the
implicit-function argument. In parallel, an interval implementation
could certify specific profile-zero isolations and quantitative
finite-$N$ displacement bounds. The present package does neither of
those certification tasks and makes no machine-checked proof claim.

\section{Conclusion}\label{ic:sec:conclusion}
Interior root collisions have a regular description once their spectral
branches are retained and the Christoffel columns are centered. Same-sign
pairs account for the forced inverse-size zeros, alternating division
removes every coordinate collision, and the residual amplitudes are
holomorphic. This yields an exact convergent sector expansion with
explicit leading terms and first correction, and it extends to several
separated clusters.

For a symmetric pair inside the Catalan interval, the leading profile is
classical, but its finite-size behavior contains information that the
leading bulk limit suppresses. Adjacent sectors produce a
$\sin(2\nu\theta)/\nu$ correction. At a simple profile zero, that
correction becomes a calculable zero displacement and, more strongly, a
convergent Laurent-phase expansion. The exact two-simple-root example
shows both periodic cancellation and quasiperiodic scaled motion. These
results answer the nonendpoint continuation explicitly posed in the
repository, with the endpoint, growing-degree, and moving-zero-index
regimes left as clearly separated research directions.

\section{A proof-dependency map and implementation audit}\label{ic:app:dependency}

This section is Appendix~A of the manuscript.

The principal dependency chain is
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.27\linewidth}p{0.65\linewidth}}
\toprule
Layer&Ingredients and output\\
\midrule
Catalan normalization&The moment measure, orthonormal recurrence, and
classical Christoffel identity reduce the problem to fixed dimension.\\[3pt]
Centered algebra&The exponential Vandermonde and row-sign expansion
prove forced powers and analytic amplitude formulas.\\[3pt]
Uniform analysis&Alternating division and nonvanishing residual factors
prove collision-uniform convergence and Cauchy remainders.\\[3pt]
Coefficient calculus&Logarithmic differentiation gives the first
operator and the finite construction of higher coefficients.\\[3pt]
Profile structure&The bilinear kernel identity supplies sine-kernel and
oscillatory Jacobi representations; Gram positivity and finite
integration by parts give the needed profile information.\\[3pt]
Zero transport&Rouch\'e and the holomorphic implicit-function theorem
turn derivative-uniform convergence into convergent phase-dependent
zero expansions.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The universal proofs do not depend on any computed decimal value or any
assertion in \nolinkurl{verification.json}. The program checks independent
routes wherever possible: moment matrices against confluent polynomial
jets, profile alternants against oscillatory moment matrices, and sector
amplitudes against direct Christoffel evaluations. The first-coefficient
identity is checked both by exact full-confluence polynomials and by
numerical derivatives at distinct coordinates. The multi-cluster and
zero tables are experiments supporting interpretation, not additional
universal proof certificates.

\noindent[Merge note: \nolinkurl{verification.json} is shipped as
\nolinkurl{data/05-interior-collision-verification.json}.]

\section{Normalization checklist}\label{ic:app:checklist}

This section is Appendix~B of the manuscript.

For reuse, the following conventions should be preserved together.
The determinant size is $N$, not $N+1$; $H_0=1$. The Catalan polynomials
are monic with $p_1=x-1$, not $x-2$, and have squared norm $1$.
The Vandermonde is $\prod_{i<j}(u_j-u_i)$, whereas the subset profile has
cross denominator $\prod_{a\in A,b\notin A}(u_a-u_b)$. The sign
$(-1)^{k(d-k)}$ in its alternant representation reconciles those
conventions. Confluent rows use $1/a!$ times the $a$th derivative. The
effective size is the global $N+d/2$. For the paired kernel identity,
the final polynomial index is $N+t-1$, while the effective size is
$N+t$. The oscillatory Jacobi frequency is $2\tau$, not $\tau$.
These points explain the signs, the factors of $2$, and the first-order
phase in the main formulas.

limitation and question of the manuscript is printed.  Where it re-derives
something Parts~I--V already prove, the overlap is marked
(Section~\ref{gs:sec:merge}).

The manuscript's own abstract reads:
\begin{quote}\small
Consider the shifted Catalan Hankel determinant
$D_N^{(m)}(a,1)=\det(aC_{i+j+m}+C_{i+j+m+1})_{0\le i,j<N}$.
The repository's fixed-shift formulas do not give uniform asymptotics when
$m$ grows with $N$. We develop such a theory for a single moving linear
factor, allowing \emph{arbitrary relative growth} of $N$ and $m$.
An exact normalization reduces the determinant to a polynomial $\Rgs(\tau)$
whose negative zeros define positive weights of total mass one. An explicit
quadratic index $\beta$ controls these weights, and an elementary Riccati
argument proves that their maximum is at most $8\beta$.

A size-biased inverse-zero measure converges to a shifted and scaled
Marchenko--Pastur law; its moments are binomial transforms of the Catalan
numbers. This single law determines an infinite hierarchy of renormalized
limits at $\tau\asymp\beta^{-(r-1)/r}$. In particular, the approximation
$\Rgs(\tau)\sim e^\tau$ breaks down at $\beta^{-1/2}$, whereas the
determinant's coefficient distribution separates from $\Pois(\tau)$ at
$\beta^{-2/3}$.  At $\tau\asymp\beta^{-1}$, the hierarchy resums to an
explicit algebraic free-energy law, valid also when $m/N$ tends to zero or
infinity.  A separate saddle calculation supplies a uniform leading
prefactor when $m/N$ remains in a compact subset of $(0,\infty)$.

All principal claims have written proofs. Exact-arithmetic audits and
floating-point diagnostics accompany the article. The underlying Jacobi,
Christoffel, and Poisson-approximation machinery is classical; the claimed
advance is relative to the inspected repository questions. Worldwide
priority is not established, and no Lean formalization is claimed.
\end{quote}
Its title page carried this scope line:
\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Scope.} One moving linear factor; nonnegative real perturbations for
probability and sharp-order statements; complex compact limits where stated.
No claim is made about arbitrary growing-degree multipliers.
\end{quote}

\begin{center}
\fcolorbox{muted}{lightblue}{\parbox{\dimexpr\textwidth-2\fboxsep-2\fboxrule\relax}{%
\textbf{Status (manuscript~02).}
This is an unrefereed, AI-assisted manuscript with conventional written
proofs, not a Lean or Rocq formalization and not an interval-certified
computation.  It treats one additional linear factor $x+a$ beside a
growing endpoint power $x^m$, with $a/b\ge0$ for its probabilistic and
sharp-order statements, and complex limits only where stated.  Classical
ingredients are credited, not presented as new: the Catalan moment measure,
the one-factor Christoffel identity (Krattenthaler~\cite{Krattenthaler2023};
re-derived in the present normalization), the finite Jacobi hypergeometric
representation (DLMF~\cite{DLMF}), large-parameter Jacobi-zero asymptotics
(Dette--Studden~\cite{DetteStudden1994}, context only), the Barbour--Hall
Bernoulli-sum Poisson bound~\cite{BarbourHall1984}, the only Poisson
approximation estimate used, and mod-Poisson and higher-order Chen--Stein
approximation (M\'eliot--Nikeghbali--Visentin~%
\cite{MeliotNikeghbaliVisentin2022}, context only).  The fixed-$m$
${}_0F_1$ limit is a standard special-function phenomenon, not claimed as
new, and the coefficient formula \eqref{gs:eq:repository-coeff} is Part~I's.
The claimed contribution relative to the inspected reports is the
integrated calculation: the exact normalization and concentration index,
the uniform inverse-zero bound, the size-biased Catalan law in every
relative-growth regime, the all-orders hierarchy, the distinct determinant
and nominal-Poisson thresholds, and the algebraic resummation with a
proportional-growth prefactor.  Equivalent formulations may exist in the
orthogonal-polynomial literature; the bounded literature check does not
settle first-discovery priority, and requested PDF fetches of the
Krattenthaler and Dette--Studden papers did not succeed (only their
abstracts were read).  Not claimed: several moving factors or arbitrary
growing-degree multipliers; both endpoint exponents growing; negative
perturbations, across which the positive-axis statements need not hold; an
extreme-zero theorem (the constant~$8$ is not claimed sharp); uniformity in
the hierarchy order~$q$; a saddle prefactor uniform as $m/N$ or $t$
approaches $0$ or~$\infty$; exhaustive inspection of the repository.  The
recorded assertions (270 direct determinant comparisons, 420 moment
parameter pairs with 5,040 moment equalities and 5,040 majorant checks,
60 limiting-recurrence checks, 16 floating-point Jacobi-root parameter
pairs) are finite audits, and the CSV tables are floating-point
diagnostics, not outward-rounded certificates.  No proof assistant was run,
nothing in Part~VI is formalized, and its placement beside a Lean project
confers no formal status.  (Sources: the manuscript, its delivery README,
and its source and proof audits, \nolinkurl{06-growing-shifts-SOURCES.md}
and \nolinkurl{06-growing-shifts-PROOF_AUDIT.md}.)}}
\end{center}
