article'' and ``here'' mean manuscript~02 as printed in Part~VI; ``the
repository'', ``the inspected reports'' and ``the combined source
manuscript'' mean Parts~I--IV at the pin \texttt{1085b506d}.  ``The
package'' and ``the delivered archive'' mean the delivered archive, whose
files are shipped under the prefixed names listed in
Section~\ref{gs:sec:source}.  The manuscript's two appendices are printed
as Sections~\ref{gs:app:formulas} and~\ref{gs:app:reproduction}, so that
Part~I's appendices keep their letters.  Its Sections~1--16 are
Sections~\ref{gs:sec:context}--\ref{gs:sec:conclusion}, so a manuscript
number $n.j$ of a section, theorem, proposition, lemma, corollary,
definition, remark or equation is printed as $(n+81).j$ (for example, its
Theorem~2.1 is Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:overview} and its Theorem~7.2 is
Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:spectral}), and its Appendices~A and~B are
Sections~\ref{gs:app:formulas} and~\ref{gs:app:reproduction}; its two
figures are Figures~\ref{gs:fig:crossover} and~\ref{gs:fig:freeenergy}.
The shipped \nolinkurl{06-growing-shifts-PROOF_AUDIT.md} and
\nolinkurl{06-growing-shifts-SOURCES.md} use the manuscript's numbers.  The
manuscript cites Krattenthaler's Ramanujan Journal paper (with
arXiv:2101.04225) and DLMF equation~18.5.7; they are Part~I's
references~\cite{Krattenthaler2023} and~\cite{DLMF}.  Bracketed paragraphs
beginning ``Merge note'' are this merge's, not the manuscript's.

\subsection{Notation of Part~VI}\label{gs:sec:notation}
Part~VI keeps the manuscript's symbols.  Within
Sections~\ref{gs:sec:context}--\ref{gs:app:reproduction} every symbol has its
Part~VI meaning, except in merge notes and where a sentence names Part~I,
II, III, IV or~V or cites one of their equations.  The determinants are
Part~I's: $D_N^{(m)}(a,b)$ and $H_N^{(m)}$ are Part~I's \eqref{eq:D} and
\eqref{eq:Hproduct}, $a$ and $b$ are Part~I's parameters, and the
manuscript works at $b=1$ except in Section~\ref{gs:sec:physical}, where it
restores $b>0$ (there $a$ is replaced by $a/b$).  In Part~IV's terms,
$D_N^{(m)}(a,b)=b^NH_N(x^m(x+\varepsilon))$ with $\varepsilon=a/b$: a cluster
of $m+1$ roots at the endpoint~$0$, $m$ of them fixed there and one
displaced outward when $\varepsilon>0$.  Table~\ref{gs:tab:notation} lists
the symbols that a reader of Parts~I--V is likely to misread.  No printed
symbol was renamed, but two macros were: the manuscript's calligraphic
$\mathcal R$ for the normalized determinant \eqref{gs:eq:Rdef} is typed
\verb|\Rgs| in the source (it prints the same $\Rgs$), so that no bare
\verb|\R| stands beside Part~II's $\Rzero$; and the manuscript's expectation
$\mathbb E$ is typed \verb|\Egs|,
because the report's \verb|\E| is Part~II's sans-serif shift $\E$.  No
normalization changed.  Five readings need particular care: $\beta$ is the
concentration index \eqref{gs:eq:beta-theta}, not a Jacobi endpoint
exponent (which is $\frac12$ here) nor Part~I's $\beta_j^\pm$; $c=v/(NB)$ is
not Part~I's coefficient $c_{N,m,k}$ nor the base $c$ of Parts~II, III
and~V; $\nu_{N,m}$ and $\nu_\theta$ are probability measures, not Part~IV's
and Part~V's centred size $\nu$; $b$ is Part~I's parameter in
Section~\ref{gs:sec:physical} but $(N+m+1)/N$ in
Section~\ref{gs:sec:saddle} (a clash inside the manuscript); and $t$ is
$\beta\tau$ in Sections~\ref{gs:sec:normalization}
and~\ref{gs:sec:freeenergy} but $\tau/N$ in Section~\ref{gs:sec:saddle}
(also inside the manuscript).

\subsection{What Part~VI answers, and what stays open}\label{gs:sec:answers}
Part~I's status paragraph (Section~\ref{sec:asymptotics}) restricts its
estimates to fixed $m,a,b$, ``not claimed to be uniform \dots\ when $m$
grows with $N$'', and its Section~\ref{sec:status} names joint limits with
growing $m$ as requiring uniform asymptotics; its dated notes of batches~39
and~53 there left them open.  Part~IV treats $a\to0$ at fixed~$m$
(Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:scale} and Section~\ref{tec:sec:answers}) and
repeats that ``joint limits with $m$ growing with $N$ remain open'', and its
research question ``Multiplicities growing with determinant size''
(Section~\ref{gs:x:tecq4}) asks for a regime $m=m_N$, $\ell=\ell_N$ in which
the factorized leading profile or the first correction remains uniform.
Part~VI answers the growing-$m$ question for the one-factor family with real
$a/b\ge0$, at every relative growth of $N$ and $m$: the ratio
$D_N^{(m)}(a,b)/D_N^{(m)}(0,b)$ tends to $1$ exactly when
$\tau=(a/b)NB/(4v)\to0$ (Corollary~\ref{gs:cor:invisibility}); when
$N,m\to\infty$ its logarithm is governed by $\beta$, with the critical
scales $\beta^{-1/2}$, $\beta^{-2/3}$ and $\beta^{-1}$
(Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:overview}), the size-biased inverse-zero law
(Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:spectral}), every fixed level of the hierarchy
(Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:hierarchy}), the algebraic free energy
(Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:freeenergy}) and, in proportional growth, a uniform
prefactor (Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:prefactor}).  In Part~IV's terms this is
$\ell=0$ and the cluster $x^m(x+\varepsilon)$, $\varepsilon=a/b\ge0$, with
one displaced root.  The manuscript does not cite Part~IV's
Section~\ref{gs:x:tecq4}; the identification is this merge's.

\emph{Not answered.}  Part~IV's Section~\ref{gs:x:tecq4} stays open for
several displaced roots, for $\ell>0$, and for the first correction in the
sense of Part~IV's Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:main}; a single approximation
uniform for $1\le m\le N$ bridging the fixed-$m$ and growing-$m$ regimes is
Part~VI's own question (Section~\ref{gs:x:bridge}).  Part~VI's
Section~\ref{gs:x:several} poses several moving factors, and its
Section~\ref{gs:x:both}, both endpoint exponents growing, is related to
Part~IV's question ``Other Jacobi endpoint exponents'' but not the same.
Coalescing roots and $a/b<0$ (Part~I's other caveats) are not treated.  A
combinatorial interpretation of Part~I's $c_{N,m,k}$ and a product formula
for its minors $M_{m,\ell}(N)$ remain open; Part~VI's
Section~\ref{gs:x:combinatorial} asks for a combinatorial coefficient model
in the same spirit.  Part~V's ``Not claimed'' (growing degree, or zero
indices growing with $N$) concerns interior roots and is neither answered
nor contradicted.

\emph{Consistency with Parts~I and~IV} (observations of this merge).
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
\item \eqref{gs:eq:repository-coeff} is Part~I's \eqref{eq:coeff} at
 $b=1$, as the manuscript says: \eqref{gs:eq:coefficients} is the
 statement $A_{N,m}(k)\mu^k/k!=\binom Nk(B)_k/(4^k(v)_k)
 =c_{N,m,k}/H_N^{(m+1)}$.
\item For fixed $m$, Corollary~\ref{gs:cor:invisibility} reduces to
 Part~IV's threshold: $\tau=\varepsilon N(N+m+1)/(4m+2)\to0$ exactly when
 $N^2\varepsilon\to0$ (Theorem~\ref{tec:thm:scale} with $\ell=0$ and $m+1$
 roots at~$0$).  If $N^2\varepsilon\to y$, then $v\tau\to y/4$ and
 \eqref{gs:eq:fixed-m} gives the limit ${}_0F_1(;m+\frac12;y/4)$, which
 must therefore be Part~IV's profile ratio \eqref{tec:eq:finiteprofile} at
 the coordinates $(0,\ldots,0,y)$.  By hand for $m=0$ and $m=1$, Part~IV's
 kernel quotient \eqref{tec:eq:Km} gives $\cosh\sqrt y$ and
 $\sinh\sqrt y/\sqrt y$, which are ${}_0F_1(;\frac12;y/4)$ and
 ${}_0F_1(;\frac32;y/4)$.  For $m\to\infty$ with $\tau$ bounded, the limit
 is $e^\tau$ instead (Section~\ref{gs:sec:onlyone}), with the corrections
 of Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:hierarchy}.
\item The table of Section~\ref{gs:sec:twothresholds} is a rounded copy of
 the shipped \nolinkurl{data/06-growing-shifts-critical_scales.csv}
 (checked for this merge).
\end{enumerate}

\section{The repository question and the contribution}
\label{gs:sec:context}

The relevant ProveIt project is \emph{Shifted Catalan Hankel Polynomials},
inside the Catalan-and-ballot Hankel-determinant reports. Its Part I gives
an exact coefficient formula and sharp recurrences for
$x^m(a+bx)$, but explicitly restricts its asymptotic estimates to fixed
$m,a,b$. Its research agenda asks for joint limits with growing $m$.
The later endpoint-confluence part fixes its endpoint multiplicities and
explicitly does not make its constants uniform in them
(Parts~I and~IV above, and this report's README at the pin). The snapshot
inspected here is
\begin{center}\small\ttfamily
1085b506d65e207a05b7e9c861bb1fe88432fe38.
\end{center}
The combined source manuscript has blob
\texttt{b23831b8f20efab08342b11bb4939809d0a8047e}.

We address the following precise version of the growing-shift question:
\begin{quote}
How does $D_N^{(m)}(a,1)/D_N^{(m)}(0,1)$ behave when $N,m\to\infty$
with no restriction on their ratio, and how far can its simplest
exponential approximation be trusted?
\end{quote}
The determinant also defines a natural probability distribution by
normalizing its nonnegative coefficients at $a>0$. We ask the corresponding
question for that distribution, rather than assuming that accuracy of the
normalizing constant and accuracy of the probability law have the same
threshold.

The answer is organized by an exact parameter $\beta$, not by $N$ alone.
The three most visible scales are
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:three-scales}
 \beta^{-1/2},\qquad \beta^{-2/3},\qquad \beta^{-1}.
\end{equation}
The first is a relative-error threshold for the determinant. The second is
a \emph{mean-displacement threshold} for comparison with the nominal law
$\Pois(\tau)$; a Poisson law with the corrected mean remains accurate much
longer. The third is a genuinely nonlinear regime for the logarithm of the
determinant. Between the first and third lies a full hierarchy of exponents
$(r-1)/r$.

\subsection{What is classical and what is being added}\label{gs:sec:classical}

The Christoffel determinant reduction is classical; a modern detailed
account is Krattenthaler \cite{Krattenthaler2023}. Jacobi hypergeometric formulas
are recorded in DLMF \cite{DLMF}. Large-parameter zero asymptotics for Jacobi
polynomials already have an extensive literature; Dette--Studden
\cite{DetteStudden1994} is particularly relevant to regimes in which parameters
outgrow the degree. Bernoulli sums and higher-order Poisson corrections
belong to the established Chen--Stein and mod-Poisson frameworks
\cite{BarbourHall1984,MeliotNikeghbaliVisentin2022}. None of these broad mechanisms is claimed as new.

The addition relative to the inspected reports is the following integrated
calculation: the exact normalization and concentration index; a uniform
inverse-zero bound; the explicit size-biased Catalan law in every relative
growth regime; the associated all-orders critical hierarchy; the distinct
sharp determinant and nominal-Poisson thresholds; and an algebraic resummation
with a proportional-growth prefactor. Equivalent formulations may exist in
the orthogonal-polynomial literature. The bounded literature check conducted
for this article does not settle first-discovery priority.

\section{Normalization and a map of the main results}
\label{gs:sec:normalization}

Let
\begin{equation}
 C_k=\frac{1}{k+1}\binom{2k}{k},\qquad
 H_N^{(m)}=\det(C_{i+j+m})_{0\le i,j<N},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 D_N^{(m)}(a,b)=\det(aC_{i+j+m}+bC_{i+j+m+1})_{0\le i,j<N}.
\end{equation}
Throughout the article, $N\ge1$ and $m\ge0$ are integers. Set
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:params}
 v=m+\frac12,\qquad B=N+m+1,\qquad
 c=\frac{v}{NB},\qquad \mu=\frac{NB}{4v}=\frac1{4c},
\end{equation}
and define
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:Rdef}
 \Rgs_{N,m}(\tau)=\frac{D_N^{(m)}(\tau/\mu,1)}{H_N^{(m+1)}}.
\end{equation}
Thus the original perturbation is $a=\tau/\mu$, not $a=\tau$.
The concentration and shape parameters are
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:beta-theta}
 \boxed{\quad
 \beta=\frac{1+c(v+3/2)}{v+1},\qquad
 \theta_{N,m}=\frac{1}{(v+1)\beta}.
 \quad}
\end{equation}
In particular, $0<\theta_{N,m}<1$.

\begin{theorem}[Main asymptotic picture]\label{gs:thm:overview}
Suppose $N,m\to\infty$. Then $\beta\to0$ and the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=8mm]
\item For $\tau\ge0$,
$\Rgs(\tau)/e^\tau\to1$ if and only if $\beta\tau^2\to0$.
If $\tau\sqrt\beta\to s\in[0,\infty)$, then
$\Rgs(\tau)e^{-\tau}\to e^{-s^2/2}$.
If $\tau\sqrt\beta\to\infty$, this ratio tends to zero.
\item Define $K_\tau$ by
$\PP(K_\tau=k)=[z^k]\Rgs(z)\,\tau^k/\Rgs(\tau)$.
If $\beta\tau\to0$ and $\beta\tau^{3/2}\to\sigma\in[0,\infty]$, then
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:TV-main}
 \TV\bigl(\Law(K_\tau),\Pois(\tau)\bigr)
 \longrightarrow 2\Phi(\sigma/2)-1,
\end{equation}
with the right side interpreted as $1$ at $\sigma=\infty$.
\item If $\theta_{N,m}\to\theta\in[0,1]$, then, for every $t\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:free-overview}
 \beta\log\Rgs(t/\beta)\longrightarrow\calF_\theta(t).
\end{equation}
The convergence is uniform on compact real $t$-intervals. The derivative
$L_\theta=\calF'_\theta$ is the positive solution of
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:algebraic-overview}
 \theta tL_\theta(t)^2+L_\theta(t)
       =\frac1{1+(1-\theta)t},\qquad \calF_\theta(0)=0.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

The proof is distributed across Sections~\ref{gs:sec:weights}--\ref{gs:sec:poisson}.
The more informative result is the inverse-zero law in
Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:spectral}, which also yields every intermediate critical
scale in Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:hierarchy}.

\section{An exact Jacobi reduction, with normalization checked}
\label{gs:sec:jacobi}

The Catalan numbers are the moments of
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:measure}
 \,\dd\omega(x)=\frac1{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{4-x}{x}}\,
             \mathbf1_{(0,4)}(x)\,\dd x.
\end{equation}
Indeed, $x=4u$ reduces the $k$th moment to a beta integral and gives
$4^k\Gamma(k+1/2)/(\sqrt\pi\Gamma(k+2))=C_k$.
Consequently, the Hankel matrix for $H_N^{(m)}$ is the Gram matrix of
$1,x,\ldots,x^{N-1}$ for $x^m\,\dd\omega(x)$. It is positive definite.

Let $p_N^{(m)}$ be the monic degree-$N$ orthogonal polynomial for this
positive measure. The one-factor Christoffel identity gives
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:christoffel}
 D_N^{(m)}(a,1)=(-1)^N H_N^{(m)}p_N^{(m)}(-a).
\end{equation}
For completeness, form the usual moment determinant with first $N$ rows
$(C_{i+j+m})_{j=0}^N$ and last row $(1,y,\ldots,y^N)$. Dividing by
$H_N^{(m)}$ gives a monic polynomial orthogonal to degrees below $N$.
Equivalently, integrating $\prod_{j=1}^N(y-x_j)$ against the squared
Vandermonde Gram integral gives that same polynomial. Substituting $y=-a$
proves \eqref{gs:eq:christoffel}, including its sign. This is the standard
identity, rederived only to fix conventions \cite{Krattenthaler2023}.

Under $y=1-x/2$, the weight is proportional to
$(1-y)^{m-1/2}(1+y)^{1/2}$ on $(-1,1)$. Thus the quotient of endpoint
values is a Jacobi quotient. The finite hypergeometric representation
\cite[18.5.7]{DLMF} yields
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:hypergeom}
 \frac{D_N^{(m)}(a,1)}{H_N^{(m+1)}}
 ={}_2F_1\!\left(\begin{matrix}-N,B\\v\end{matrix};-a/4\right).
\end{equation}
All normalizing factors cancel because the quotient equals one at $a=0$.

\begin{proposition}[Exact normalized coefficients]\label{gs:prop:coefficients}
One has
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:coefficients}
 \Rgs(\tau)=\sum_{k=0}^N A_{N,m}(k)\frac{\tau^k}{k!},\qquad
 A_{N,m}(k)=\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}
       \frac{(1-j/N)(1+j/B)}{1+j/v}.
\end{equation}
Every $A(k)$ is positive for $0\le k\le N$, and $A(0)=A(1)=1$.
Moreover $A(k)\le1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Expand \eqref{gs:eq:hypergeom} and use $a=4c\tau$. For $0\le j<N$,
\[
 (1-j/N)(1+j/B)
 =1-j(1/N-1/B)-j^2/(NB)\le1,
\]
where $B>N$. The denominator is at least one.
\end{proof}

The unnormalized coefficient formula is therefore
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:repository-coeff}
 D_N^{(m)}(a,1)=H_N^{(m+1)}
 \sum_{k=0}^N\binom Nk\frac{(B)_k}{4^k(v)_k}a^k.
\end{equation}
This is the repository's existing coefficient theorem, not a new
coefficient formula (Part~I, Theorem~\ref{thm:coeff}). It is useful here
because it is exact for every $N,m$ and remains meaningful when fixed-$m$
asymptotics do not.

\noindent[Merge note: \eqref{gs:eq:repository-coeff} is Part~I's
\eqref{eq:coeff} at $b=1$, since $B=N+m+1$ and $v=m+\frac12$; the
normalization \eqref{gs:eq:Rdef} divides by Part~I's $c_{N,m,0}=H_N^{(m+1)}$.
The measure $\omega$ of \eqref{gs:eq:measure} is Part~I's $\mu_0$
\eqref{eq:measure}, and $x^m\,\dd\omega(x)=\dd\mu_m(x)$; the monic polynomial
$p_N^{(m)}$ is Part~I's \eqref{eq:Jacobi}, whose value at $x=-a$ gives
\eqref{gs:eq:hypergeom}; and \eqref{gs:eq:christoffel} is the one-factor
Christoffel identity for $\mu_m$, of which Part~I's
Lemma~\ref{lem:Christoffel} (for $\mu_0$ and several factors) and its
successors in Parts~II, IV and~V are relatives.  The manuscript re-derives
all three without assuming Part~I.]

\begin{corollary}[An exact invisibility criterion]\label{gs:cor:invisibility}
For every $N,m$ and $\tau\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:invisible}
 1+\tau\le\Rgs(\tau)\le e^\tau.
\end{equation}
Along any parameter sequence with $a\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{D_N^{(m)}(a,1)}{H_N^{(m+1)}}\longrightarrow1
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \frac{aN(N+m+1)}{4(m+1/2)}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No condition that $m$ be fixed or tend to infinity is needed.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use the constant and linear coefficients for the lower bound and
$A(k)\le1$ for the upper bound. Apply the squeeze theorem in one direction
and $\Rgs\ge1+\tau$ in the other.
\end{proof}

\section{Inverse-zero weights and the exact concentration index}
\label{gs:sec:weights}

\subsection{Positive weights from orthogonality}
A monic orthogonal polynomial of degree $N$ for a positive measure on
$(0,4)$ has $N$ simple zeros in that interval. One elementary proof counts
its sign changes there. If it had fewer than $N$, multiply it by the
polynomial whose roots are precisely the sign-changing zeros. The product
would have constant nonzero sign almost everywhere, contradicting
orthogonality to that polynomial of degree less than $N$.
The degree bound then forces every zero to be simple and internal.

Write these zeros as $x_1,\ldots,x_N$, and put
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:weights}
 w_j=\frac1{\mu x_j}>0,\qquad
 S_r=\sum_{j=1}^N w_j^r,\qquad W=\max_j w_j.
\end{equation}
Equations~\eqref{gs:eq:christoffel} and \eqref{gs:eq:Rdef} imply
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:product}
 \Rgs(\tau)=\prod_{j=1}^N(1+\tau w_j),\qquad S_1=1.
\end{equation}
In particular, $W\le1$. The weights describe a deterministic zero
configuration; they are not random eigenvalues.

\subsection{Riccati recurrence}
The hypergeometric differential equation transforms into
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:ODE}
 \tau(1+c\tau)\Rgs''+
 [v+(v+3/2)c\tau]\Rgs'-v\Rgs=0.
\end{equation}
Set $L=\Rgs'/\Rgs$. Near the origin,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:logderivative}
 L(\tau)=\sum_{k\ge0}(-1)^kS_{k+1}\tau^k.
\end{equation}
Substitution in \eqref{gs:eq:ODE} gives the following identity.

\begin{proposition}[All inverse-zero moments]\label{gs:prop:moment-recurrence}
For every $k\ge1$,
\begin{align}\label{gs:eq:moment-recurrence}
 (v+k)S_{k+1}
 &=\sum_{i=1}^kS_iS_{k+1-i}
   +c(k+v+1/2)S_k
   -c\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}S_iS_{k-i},
 &S_1&=1.
\end{align}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:S2S3}
 S_2=\beta,\qquad
 S_3=\frac{2\beta+c(v+5/2)\beta-c}{v+2}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The coefficient of $\tau^k$ in
$\tau(1+c\tau)(L'+L^2)+[v+(v+3/2)c\tau]L-v$
is zero. Multiply that coefficient by $(-1)^{k-1}$ and collect the
$S_{k+1}$ terms. The derivative contributions combine to $v+k$;
the coefficient multiplying $cS_k$ is $k-1+v+3/2$.
This gives \eqref{gs:eq:moment-recurrence}. The cases $k=1,2$ give
\eqref{gs:eq:S2S3}.
\end{proof}

This recurrence is rational and requires no zero computation. Computing
$S_1,\ldots,S_r$ costs $O(r^2)$ rational arithmetic operations; this is
an arithmetic-operation count, not a bit-complexity claim.

\begin{lemma}[When the concentration index vanishes]\label{gs:lem:beta-small}
One has
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:beta-bounds}
 \max\left\{\frac1N,\frac1{v+1}\right\}
 \le\beta\le\frac1{v+1}+\frac4{3N}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, $\beta\to0$ if and only if $N\to\infty$ and $m\to\infty$.
If both tend to infinity, then
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:beta-asymp}
 \beta\sim\frac1N+\frac1{m+1/2}-\frac1{N+m+1}.
\end{equation}
If $m/N\to\rho\in[0,\infty]$, then
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:theta-rho}
 \theta_{N,m}\longrightarrow
 \theta(\rho)=\frac{1+\rho}{1+\rho+\rho^2},
\end{equation}
with $\theta(0)=1$ and $\theta(\infty)=0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Cauchy--Schwarz and $S_1=1$ give $S_2\ge1/N$. The exact formula for
$\beta$ gives the other lower bound. Since $c<1/N$ and
$(v+3/2)/(v+1)\le4/3$ for $v\ge1/2$, the upper bound follows.
For \eqref{gs:eq:beta-asymp}, put
$b_*=1/N+1/v-1/B$. Direct algebra gives
\[
 \beta=\frac{vb_*+c}{v+1},\qquad
 b_*-c=\frac1v+\frac1{2NB}.
\]
Thus $(b_*-\beta)/b_*\to0$. Finally
$\theta_{N,m}=[1+v(v+3/2)/(NB)]^{-1}$, which gives the asserted limits.
\end{proof}

\section{A uniform bound on the largest inverse zero}
\label{gs:sec:majorant}

Control of $S_2$ alone is not sufficient for the sharp critical limits:
one weight of order $\sqrt\beta$ could survive on the first critical scale.
The next theorem excludes that possibility uniformly, even when $m/N$
has no limit.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform inverse-zero majorant]\label{gs:thm:majorant}
For every integer $r\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:catalan-majorant}
 S_r\le C_{r-1}(2\beta)^{r-1}.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:Wbound}
 \boxed{\quad W\le\min\{1,8\beta\}.\quad}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Because $c<1/N\le\beta$, $1/(v+k)\le1/(v+1)\le\beta$, and
\[
 \frac{c(k+v+1/2)}{v+k}\le2\beta,
\]
we may discard the negative last term of
\eqref{gs:eq:moment-recurrence} and obtain
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:majorant-rec}
 S_{k+1}\le\beta\sum_{i=1}^kS_iS_{k+1-i}+2\beta S_k.
\end{equation}
The bounds for $r=1,2$ follow from $S_1=1$ and $S_2=\beta$.
Suppose the bounds hold through $r=k$, where $k\ge2$.
Using the Catalan convolution gives
\[
 \sum_{i=1}^kS_iS_{k+1-i}
 \le(2\beta)^{k-1}\sum_{i=1}^k C_{i-1}C_{k-i}
 =(2\beta)^{k-1}C_k.
\]
Also $C_k\ge2C_{k-1}$ for $k\ge2$. Equation~\eqref{gs:eq:majorant-rec}
is therefore at most $C_k(2\beta)^k$, completing the induction.
Finally $W^r\le S_r$ and $C_{r-1}^{1/r}\to4$. Taking $r$th roots
and passing to the limit proves $W\le8\beta$.
\end{proof}

The constant eight is a transparent bound, not a claim of optimality.
The limiting measure below suggests the much more precise edge
$W/\beta\to1+3\theta$, but weak convergence alone does not exclude
outliers. We leave that strengthening explicitly open here.

\section{The first critical scale and exact error control}
\label{gs:sec:gaussian}

\begin{theorem}[Finite-parameter logarithmic bounds]\label{gs:thm:logbounds}
For every $\tau\ge0$,
\begin{align}\label{gs:eq:logbounds}
 \tau-\frac{\beta\tau^2}{2}
 &\le\log\Rgs(\tau)
 \le\tau-\frac{\beta\tau^2}{2(1+W\tau)},\\
 0\le\log\Rgs(\tau)-\tau+\frac{\beta\tau^2}{2}
 &\le\frac{S_3\tau^3}{3}
 \le\frac83\beta^2\tau^3.\label{gs:eq:third-error}
\end{align}
For $8\beta|z|<1$, with the logarithm normalized by $\log\Rgs(0)=0$,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:complex-error}
 \left|\log\Rgs(z)-z+\frac{\beta z^2}{2}\right|
 \le\frac{8\beta^2|z|^3}{3(1-8\beta|z|)}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $x\ge0$,
\[
 \frac{x^2}{2(1+x)}\le x-\log(1+x)\le\frac{x^2}{2},
\]
by integrating $u/(1+u)$ from zero to $x$. Also
$0\le\log(1+x)-x+x^2/2\le x^3/3$.
Sum these inequalities at $x=\tau w_j$, using $S_2=\beta$ and
$S_3\le WS_2\le8\beta^2$.
For complex $z$, expand every $\log(1+zw_j)$ in its convergent Taylor
series. Since $S_r\le\beta W^{r-2}$ for $r\ge2$, the tail from $r=3$
is bounded by a geometric series. The disk is zero-free because
$|z|W<1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Sharp Gaussian relative correction]\label{gs:cor:gaussian}
As $N,m\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:gaussian-limit}
 e^{-z/\sqrt\beta}\Rgs(z/\sqrt\beta)
 \longrightarrow e^{-z^2/2}
\end{equation}
locally uniformly for $z\in\CC$. On the nonnegative real axis,
$\Rgs(\tau)/e^\tau\to1$ exactly when $\tau\sqrt\beta\to0$, and it
tends to zero when $\tau\sqrt\beta\to\infty$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For bounded $z$, the right side of \eqref{gs:eq:complex-error} after scaling
is $O(\sqrt\beta)$, proving \eqref{gs:eq:gaussian-limit}.
The ratio $e^{-\tau}\Rgs(\tau)$ decreases with $\tau\ge0$, since its
logarithmic derivative is
\[
 -\sum_j\frac{\tau w_j^2}{1+\tau w_j}<0\qquad(\tau>0).
\]
The bounded-scale limit and monotonicity show necessity of the
little-$o$ condition and give the limit zero beyond every fixed critical
multiple. Sufficiency also follows directly from \eqref{gs:eq:logbounds}.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering\includegraphics[width=.91\linewidth]{figures/06-growing-shifts-determinant_crossover.pdf}
\caption{The first critical scale, with $N=m$. The horizontal variable is
$s=\tau\sqrt\beta$. The dashed curve is the proved limit, not a fitted curve.
Finite-size curves are floating-point diagnostics.}
\label{gs:fig:crossover}
\end{figure}

\section{A size-biased inverse-zero law}
\label{gs:sec:spectral}

\begin{definition}
The normalized inverse-zero measure is
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:nu}
 \nu_{N,m}=\sum_{j=1}^Nw_j\delta_{w_j/\beta}.
\end{equation}
It is a probability measure, because $S_1=1$.
\end{definition}

The weighting by $w_j$ is essential: this is not the uniform empirical
measure $N^{-1}\sum\delta_{w_j/\beta}$. Its $(r-1)$st moment is
$S_r/\beta^{r-1}$, so the exact recurrence directly determines its limit.

Let $Y$ have the Marchenko--Pastur law of parameter one, defined here by
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:MP}
 \PP(Y\in\,\dd y)=\frac1{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{4-y}{y}}
       \mathbf1_{(0,4)}(y)\,\dd y.
\end{equation}
This is numerically the Catalan moment measure \eqref{gs:eq:measure}; the
name only identifies the standard law. Its moments are
$\Egs Y^j=C_j$ by the same beta-integral computation.
For $0\le\theta\le1$, put
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:limit-nu}
 \nu_\theta=\Law(1-\theta+\theta Y).
\end{equation}
Thus $\nu_0=\delta_1$, while $\nu_1=\MP$.

\begin{theorem}[The shifted Catalan law]\label{gs:thm:spectral}
If $N,m\to\infty$ and $\theta_{N,m}\to\theta$, then
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:weak}
 \nu_{N,m}\Longrightarrow\nu_\theta.
\end{equation}
More explicitly, for each fixed integer $r\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:dr}
 \frac{S_r}{\beta^{r-1}}\longrightarrow d_r(\theta),\qquad
 d_r(\theta)=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\binom{r-1}{j}
                 C_j\theta^j(1-\theta)^{r-1-j}.
\end{equation}
All measures $\nu_{N,m}$ are supported in $[0,8]$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $D_r=S_r/\beta^{r-1}$. The majorant theorem bounds every fixed
$D_r$ uniformly. From \eqref{gs:eq:beta-theta},
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:limit-parameters}
 \frac1{v\beta}\to\theta,\qquad
 \frac c\beta\to1-\theta.
\end{equation}
Indeed the first differs from $\theta_{N,m}$ by a factor $(v+1)/v$,
and the second differs from $1-\theta_{N,m}$ by a factor
$(v+1)/(v+3/2)$. Divide \eqref{gs:eq:moment-recurrence} by
$(v+k)\beta^k$. Induction on $k$ yields limits satisfying, with
$\gamma=1-\theta$,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:limit-recurrence}
 d_{k+1}=\theta\sum_{i=1}^kd_i d_{k+1-i}
       +\gamma d_k
       -\theta\gamma\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}d_i d_{k-i},\qquad d_1=1.
\end{equation}
This induction works unchanged at $\theta=0$ and $\theta=1$.

Let $M(z)=\sum_{k\ge1}d_kz^{k-1}$. Multiplication of
\eqref{gs:eq:limit-recurrence} by $z^k$ and summation gives
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:Mquadratic}
 M(z)=\frac1{1-\gamma z}+\theta zM(z)^2.
\end{equation}
If $C(z)=\sum C_jz^j$ is the Catalan generating function, the unique
formal solution with constant term one is
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:Msolution}
 M(z)=\frac1{1-\gamma z}
          C\!\left(\frac{\theta z}{1-\gamma z}\right).
\end{equation}
Expanding the denominator gives exactly \eqref{gs:eq:dr}; equivalently,
these are the moments of $\gamma+\theta Y$.
Finally $W/\beta\le8$ makes the measures uniformly compactly supported.
Polynomials approximate continuous functions on $[0,8]$, so convergence
of all moments implies the asserted weak convergence.
\end{proof}

The first coefficients, displayed without a change of normalization, are
\begin{align}\label{gs:eq:first-dr}
 d_1&=1,&d_2&=1,&d_3&=1+\theta^2,\\
 d_4&=1+3\theta^2+\theta^3,&
 d_5&=1+6\theta^2+4\theta^3+3\theta^4.\nonumber
\end{align}
This identifies the same quadratic index as universal at second order,
while the aspect ratio first becomes visible in the cubic correction.

\begin{remark}[What weak convergence does not say]
For $\theta>0$, the support of $\nu_\theta$ is
$[1-\theta,1+3\theta]$. The theorem gives convergence of weighted mass
and of fixed moments. It does not prove convergence of the largest atom's
location, a rate uniform in $r$, or an asymptotic for an individual Jacobi
zero. Those are stronger statements.
\end{remark}

\section{An all-orders hierarchy of critical scales}
\label{gs:sec:hierarchy}

The product representation makes the relation with asymptotic series
especially transparent:
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:logseries}
 \log\Rgs(z)=\sum_{r\ge1}\frac{(-1)^{r+1}}r S_rz^r,
 \qquad |z|<1/W.
\end{equation}
The coefficient $S_r$ is of order $\beta^{r-1}$ for fixed $r$.
Consequently, its first order-one contribution occurs at
$z\asymp\beta^{-(r-1)/r}$.

\begin{theorem}[Every fixed correction level]\label{gs:thm:hierarchy}
Assume the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:spectral}. Fix an integer $q\ge2$
and put $z_\beta=u\beta^{-(q-1)/q}$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:hierarchy}
 \Rgs(z_\beta)
 \exp\!\left\{-\sum_{r=1}^{q-1}
       \frac{(-1)^{r+1}}rS_rz_\beta^r\right\}
 \longrightarrow
 \exp\!\left\{\frac{(-1)^{q+1}}q d_q(\theta)u^q\right\}
\end{equation}
locally uniformly for $u\in\CC$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $W|z|<1$, the tail beyond degree $q$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:q-tail}
 \left|\log\Rgs(z)-\sum_{r=1}^q
        \frac{(-1)^{r+1}}rS_rz^r\right|
 \le\frac{\beta W^{q-1}|z|^{q+1}}
           {(q+1)(1-W|z|)}.
\end{equation}
Use $S_r\le\beta W^{r-2}$ and sum the geometric majorant.
At $z=z_\beta$, $W|z|=O(\beta^{1/q})$ and the bound is
$O(\beta^{1/q})$ on compact $u$-sets. The remaining degree-$q$ term
converges by \eqref{gs:eq:dr}. Exponentiation proves the statement.
\end{proof}

For $q=2$ this is the Gaussian correction. For $q=3$,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:cubic-limit}
 \Rgs(u\beta^{-2/3})
 \exp\{-u\beta^{-2/3}+u^2\beta^{-1/3}/2\}
 \longrightarrow \exp\{(1+\theta^2)u^3/3\}.
\end{equation}
For $q=4$, the limiting fourth term is
$\exp\{-(1+3\theta^2+\theta^3)u^4/4\}$ after subtracting the first
three \emph{exact} terms.

\begin{remark}[A necessary precision rule]
The lower-order $S_r$ in \eqref{gs:eq:hierarchy} cannot in general be replaced
by $\beta^{r-1}d_r(\theta)$ without an additional rate hypothesis.
Those lower-order contributions diverge on the $q$th scale. An $o(1)$
relative error in a coefficient can then become an order-one or unbounded
error in the exponent. The theorem deliberately keeps the finite-parameter
moments exact. It asserts fixed-$q$ limits, not a result with $q\to\infty$.
\end{remark}

\section{Resumming the hierarchy: the full nonlinear scale}
\label{gs:sec:freeenergy}

Define for $t\ge0$
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:F-integral}
 \calF_\theta(t)=\int\frac{\log(1+tx)}x\,\dd\nu_\theta(x),
\end{equation}
where the integrand at $x=0$ is defined as $t$.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform nonlinear logarithmic limit]\label{gs:thm:freeenergy}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:spectral},
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:F-limit}
 \beta\log\Rgs(t/\beta)\longrightarrow\calF_\theta(t)
\end{equation}
uniformly on compact subsets of $[0,\infty)$, together with every fixed
number of derivatives in $t$. Its derivative is
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:Fprime}
 L_\theta(t)=
 \frac{2}{\bigl(1+(1-\theta)t\bigr)
 \left(1+\sqrt{1+\dfrac{4\theta t}{1+(1-\theta)t}}\right)}.
\end{equation}
The formula includes $t=0$ and $\theta=0$ without singularities.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The exact finite-parameter identity is
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:F-exact}
 \beta\log\Rgs(t/\beta)
 =\int\frac{\log(1+tx)}x\,\dd\nu_{N,m}(x).
\end{equation}
On $[0,8]$ the integrand, continued at $x=0$, and all its $t$-derivatives
are continuous and uniformly bounded for $t$ in a fixed nonnegative
compact interval. Weak convergence and compactness give the claimed
uniform limits, including derivatives.

Differentiating \eqref{gs:eq:F-integral} gives
$L_\theta(t)=\int(1+tx)^{-1}\,\dd\nu_\theta(x)=M(-t)$ near zero.
Equation~\eqref{gs:eq:Mquadratic} therefore becomes
\eqref{gs:eq:algebraic-overview}. Its positive solution is
\eqref{gs:eq:Fprime}; the identity extends to all $t\ge0$, either by the
integral representation or by analytic continuation along that ray.
\end{proof}

At the two extreme aspect ratios the integral is elementary:
\begin{align}\label{gs:eq:extreme-F}
 \calF_0(t)&=\log(1+t),\\
 \calF_1(t)&=\sqrt{1+4t}-1-
       \log\!\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{1+4t}}2\right).
\end{align}
The Taylor series begins
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:Fseries}
 \calF_\theta(t)=t-\frac{t^2}{2}
       +\frac{1+\theta^2}{3}t^3
       -\frac{1+3\theta^2+\theta^3}{4}t^4+\cdots.
\end{equation}
It is convergent at least for $|t|<(1+3\theta)^{-1}$, with the convention
that the radius at $\theta=0$ is one. Thus the hierarchy is not merely a
list of unrelated corrections: its limiting coefficients belong to one
analytic function.

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering\includegraphics[width=.91\linewidth]{figures/06-growing-shifts-free_energy.pdf}
\caption{The limiting logarithm at the full scale. The middle curve has
$m/N\to1$, hence $\theta=2/3$. The endpoints correspond to $m/N\to\infty$
and $m/N\to0$, always with both $m,N\to\infty$. This is a smooth crossover,
not a claim of a singular phase transition at finite positive $t$.}
\label{gs:fig:freeenergy}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Mean and variance on the nonlinear scale}
Let $K_\tau$ be the coefficient distribution from
Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:overview}. Its probability generating function is
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:pgf}
 \Egs z^{K_\tau}=\frac{\Rgs(\tau z)}{\Rgs(\tau)}
 =\prod_{j=1}^N\left(1-p_j+p_jz\right),\qquad
 p_j=\frac{\tau w_j}{1+\tau w_j}.
\end{equation}
Hence $K_\tau$ is a sum of independent Bernoulli variables with these
parameters. In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:mean-var-exact}
 \Egs K_\tau=\tau\frac{\Rgs'(\tau)}{\Rgs(\tau)},\qquad
 \Var K_\tau=\tau\frac{\,\dd}{\,\dd\tau}\Egs K_\tau.
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[Nonlinear coefficient fluctuations]\label{gs:cor:nonlinear-CLT}
For fixed $t>0$, under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:freeenergy},
\begin{align}
 \beta\Egs K_{t/\beta}&\longrightarrow Q_\theta(t):=tL_\theta(t),\\
 \beta\Var K_{t/\beta}&\longrightarrow
 V_\theta(t):=t\int\frac1{(1+tx)^2}\,\dd\nu_\theta(x)>0.
\end{align}
Moreover
\begin{equation}
 \frac{K_{t/\beta}-\Egs K_{t/\beta}}
      {\sqrt{\Var K_{t/\beta}}}\Longrightarrow N(0,1).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The limits follow from \eqref{gs:eq:F-exact} by differentiation or directly
from the Bernoulli sums. The limiting variance is positive; thus the
unscaled variance diverges. For independent centered Bernoulli summands,
the sum of third absolute moments is at most their total variance.
After division by the variance to the power $3/2$, this tends to zero.
The usual characteristic-function proof of the Lyapunov central limit
theorem applies: the quadratic term tends to $-u^2/2$ and the total
third-order remainder tends to zero.
\end{proof}

Only exact centering is used here. Replacing the mean by
$Q_\theta(t)/\beta$ requires an error estimate stronger than the weak
convergence in Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:spectral}.

\section{The second critical scale: displacement of the nominal Poisson law}
\label{gs:sec:poisson}

Write $\lambda=\Egs K_\tau$. From \eqref{gs:eq:pgf},
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:lambda-delta}
 \lambda=\tau\sum_j\frac{w_j}{1+\tau w_j},\qquad
 \delta:=\tau-\lambda
 =\tau^2\sum_j\frac{w_j^2}{1+\tau w_j}.
\end{equation}
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:delta-bounds}
 \frac{\beta\tau^2}{1+W\tau}\le\delta\le\beta\tau^2,
 \qquad \lambda\ge\frac{\tau}{1+W\tau}.
\end{equation}
The shift $\delta$ becomes visible against a Poisson standard deviation
$\sqrt\tau$ when $\beta\tau^{3/2}$ becomes order one.

We use the standard Bernoulli-sum Poisson bound
\cite{BarbourHall1984}:
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:BH}
 \TV\left(\Law\!\left(\sum_j\mathrm{Bern}(p_j)\right),\Pois(\lambda)\right)
 \le\frac{1-e^{-\lambda}}{\lambda}\sum_jp_j^2,
 \qquad\lambda=\sum_jp_j.
\end{equation}
The convention for $\TV$ is one half the $\ell^1$ distance.
This external classical inequality is the only Poisson approximation
estimate used below; its provenance is not absorbed into the new claims.

\begin{theorem}[Quantitative corrected-mean approximation]\label{gs:thm:corrected-Pois}
For $\tau>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:corrected-bound}
 \TV(\Law(K_\tau),\Pois(\lambda))
 \le\beta\tau(1+8\beta\tau).
\end{equation}
Also
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:nominal-bound}
 \TV(\Law(K_\tau),\Pois(\tau))
 \le\beta\tau(1+8\beta\tau)+\beta\tau^{3/2}.
\end{equation}
Bounds exceeding one may of course be replaced by one.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
One has $\sum p_j^2\le\tau^2\beta$. Apply \eqref{gs:eq:BH}, then
$\lambda\ge\tau/(1+W\tau)$ and $W\le8\beta$.
For two Poisson laws, their Hellinger affinity equals
$\exp[-(\sqrt\lambda-\sqrt\tau)^2/2]$. Cauchy--Schwarz gives
\[
 \TV(\Pois(\lambda),\Pois(\tau))
 \le\sqrt{1-e^{-(\sqrt\lambda-\sqrt\tau)^2}}
 \le|\sqrt\lambda-\sqrt\tau|
 \le\frac\delta{\sqrt\tau}.
\]
Use $\delta\le\beta\tau^2$ and the triangle inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact transition profile]\label{gs:thm:TV-transition}
Let $N,m\to\infty$, $\tau\ge0$, and $\beta\tau\to0$.
If $\beta\tau^{3/2}\to\sigma\in[0,\infty]$, then
\eqref{gs:eq:TV-main} holds. If additionally $\tau\to\infty$ and
$\sigma<\infty$, then
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:shifted-normal}
 \frac{K_\tau-\tau}{\sqrt\tau}\Longrightarrow N(-\sigma,1).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \eqref{gs:eq:corrected-bound}, $K_\tau$ is asymptotically indistinguishable
in total variation from $\Pois(\lambda)$. Equations~\eqref{gs:eq:delta-bounds}
give $\lambda/\tau\to1$ and
$\delta/\sqrt\tau\sim\beta\tau^{3/2}$ whenever the latter is nonzero.
If $\sigma=0$, \eqref{gs:eq:nominal-bound} already proves the result.

For $0<\sigma<\infty$, necessarily $\tau\to\infty$.
The likelihood ratio of $\Pois(\lambda)$ to $\Pois(\tau)$ is
$e^\delta(\lambda/\tau)^k$, decreasing in $k$. Its crossing point is
\[
 k_*=\frac{\delta}{\log(\tau/\lambda)}
     =\tau-\frac\delta2+O(\delta^2/\tau).
\]
The total variation distance is the difference of the two probabilities
of $k\le\lfloor k_*\rfloor$. The Poisson central limit theorem gives
limits $\Phi(\sigma/2)$ and $\Phi(-\sigma/2)$, respectively, proving
\eqref{gs:eq:TV-main}. The same theorem, $\lambda/\tau\to1$, and
$\delta/\sqrt\tau\to\sigma$ give \eqref{gs:eq:shifted-normal}.

If $\sigma=\infty$, the two means are separated by much more than
$\sqrt\tau$. At the midpoint of the two means, Chebyshev's inequality
shows that the corresponding Poisson probabilities tend to one and zero.
Their total variation distance tends to one; the corrected-mean error
still tends to zero. This completes every case.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Do not conflate the two thresholds]
At $\tau=s\beta^{-1/2}$ the determinant has a nontrivial factor
$e^{-s^2/2}$, yet the nominal Poisson error tends to zero.
At $\tau=u\beta^{-2/3}$, that nominal error tends to
$2\Phi(u^{3/2}/2)-1$, but the corrected law $\Pois(\lambda)$ is still
accurate. The second threshold is not a failure of all Poisson
approximation. Higher-order Bernoulli corrections are part of the broader
mod-Poisson literature \cite{MeliotNikeghbaliVisentin2022}; the point here is their exact scale
and constants for this determinant family.
\end{remark}

\section{A sharp prefactor in proportional growth}
\label{gs:sec:saddle}

The inverse-zero method controls the scaled logarithm for every aspect
ratio. When $m/N$ stays bounded away from zero and infinity, the exact
coefficient formula yields a stronger multiplicative asymptotic.
This section gives a second route to the nonlinear regime.

Set
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:saddle-params}
 r=\frac{m+1/2}{N},\qquad b=\frac{N+m+1}{N},\qquad \tau=Nt.
\end{equation}
Here $b=1+r+1/(2N)$; retaining this finite-$N$ difference matters for the
prefactor. For $0<x<1$, define
\begin{align}\label{gs:eq:f}
 f(x;r,b,t)
 &=x\log(t/x)-(1-x)\log(1-x)\nonumber\\
 &\quad +(b+x)\log(1+x/b)-(r+x)\log(1+x/r),
\end{align}
continued continuously at the endpoints. Its second derivative is
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:h}
 -f''(x)=h(x):=\frac1x+\frac1{1-x}
                    +\frac1{r+x}-\frac1{b+x}>0.
\end{equation}
The derivative tends to $+\infty$ at zero and $-\infty$ at one.
There is therefore a unique maximum $q\in(0,1)$, characterized by
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:q}
 tr(1-q)(b+q)=qb(r+q).
\end{equation}
It is the positive root of a quadratic. A cancellation-resistant formula is
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:q-stable}
 q=\frac{2rtb}{\ell+\sqrt{\ell^2+4(rt+b)rtb}},\qquad
 \ell=br-rt(1-b).
\end{equation}
Finally define
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:prefactor}
 \mathcal A(r,b,t)=
 \sqrt{\frac{b(r+q)}{q(1-q)r(b+q)h(q)}}.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform leading prefactor]\label{gs:thm:prefactor}
Let $m/N$ and $t$ range over fixed compact subsets of $(0,\infty)$.
Then, uniformly over these parameters,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:prefactor-thm}
 \Rgs_{N,m}(Nt)=\mathcal A(r,b,t)
       \exp\{Nf(q;r,b,t)\}\bigl(1+O(N^{-1})\bigr).
\end{equation}
For the coefficient distribution at $\tau=Nt$,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:saddleCLT}
 \frac{K_{Nt}-Nq}{\sqrt{N/h(q)}}\Longrightarrow N(0,1),
\end{equation}
uniformly on such compact parameter sets.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $T_k=A(k)(Nt)^k/k!$, with $x=k/N$.
The exact gamma-function expression for $T_k$ is
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:Tk}
 T_k=\frac{(Nt)^k}{k!}\,
 \frac{N!}{(N-k)!N^k}\,
 \frac{\Gamma(Nb+k)}{\Gamma(Nb)(Nb)^k}\,
 \frac{\Gamma(Nr)(Nr)^k}{\Gamma(Nr+k)}.
\end{equation}
For $x$ in any compact subinterval of $(0,1)$, Stirling's formula, including
its square-root factors, gives uniformly
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:Tk-Stirling}
 T_k=\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi N}}
 \sqrt{\frac{b(r+x)}{x(1-x)r(b+x)}}
 e^{Nf(x;r,b,t)}\bigl(1+O(N^{-1})\bigr).
\end{equation}
The factorial ratio contributes $(1-x)^{-1/2}$; the two rising-factorial
ratios contribute $(b/(b+x))^{1/2}$ and $((r+x)/r)^{1/2}$.
These factors account for all terms in \eqref{gs:eq:prefactor}.

On the stated compact parameter sets, $q$ stays uniformly inside $(0,1)$,
and $h(q)$ is bounded above and below by positive constants. Strict
concavity gives a uniform exponential gap outside any fixed neighborhood
of $q$. Endpoint versions of the rough Stirling bounds, with $O(\log N)$
in the logarithm, show that the sum of the terms outside that neighborhood
is exponentially smaller than $e^{Nf(q)}$.

Inside it, put $k=Nq+y\sqrt N$ and Taylor-expand $f$ and the smooth
amplitude in \eqref{gs:eq:Tk-Stirling}. The leading exponential is
$e^{-h(q)y^2/2}$. Terms of order $N^{-1/2}$ are odd polynomials in $y$
times that Gaussian, and their integrals vanish. Gaussian tails permit
termwise summation after truncation at $|y|\le N^{1/12}$; the omitted tails
are exponentially small in a positive power of $N$. The Gaussian sums on
a mesh of width $N^{-1/2}$ differ from their integrals by exponentially
small errors, uniformly in the fractional part of $Nq$ (for example, by
the Fourier series of a periodized Gaussian). Thus the relative error is
$O(N^{-1})$, not merely $O(N^{-1/2})$.

The leading sum multiplies \eqref{gs:eq:Tk-Stirling} at $x=q$ by
$\sqrt{2\pi N/h(q)}$. This is \eqref{gs:eq:prefactor-thm}.
Normalizing the same local expansion, with the exponentially small tails,
proves \eqref{gs:eq:saddleCLT}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The limiting saddle and the coefficient rate function}
If $m/N\to\rho\in(0,\infty)$, then $r\to\rho$, $b\to1+\rho$,
and \eqref{gs:eq:q} simplifies to
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:q-rho}
 q(q+\rho)=\frac{\rho(1+\rho)t}{1+\rho+\rho t}.
\end{equation}
Writing $f_{\rho,t}(x)=f(x;\rho,1+\rho,t)$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:free-saddle}
 \frac1N\log\Rgs(Nt)\longrightarrow f_{\rho,t}(q).
\end{equation}
For any integers $k_N$ with $k_N/N\to x\in[0,1]$,
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:mass-rate}
 \frac1N\log\PP(K_{Nt}=k_N)
 \longrightarrow f_{\rho,t}(x)-f_{\rho,t}(q).
\end{equation}
The endpoint cases follow directly from \eqref{gs:eq:Tk} using the continuous
extension of $f$; the interior cases use \eqref{gs:eq:Tk-Stirling}.
Thus the nonnegative function $f_{\rho,t}(q)-f_{\rho,t}(x)$ is the
pointwise coefficient rate function.

There is an exact consistency check between the two nonlinear descriptions.
Put
\begin{equation}
 b_\rho=\frac{1+\rho+\rho^2}{\rho(1+\rho)},\qquad
 \theta=\frac{1+\rho}{1+\rho+\rho^2}.
\end{equation}
Then $N\beta\to b_\rho$, and the two proven limits imply
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:consistency}
 b_\rho f_{\rho,t}(q)=\calF_\theta(b_\rho t).
\end{equation}
It can also be checked by differentiating both sides, using
\eqref{gs:eq:q-rho} and \eqref{gs:eq:algebraic-overview}, and matching the value
zero at $t=0$.

\section{Boundary regimes and the meaning in the original variables}
\label{gs:sec:boundaries}

\subsection{What happens when only one parameter grows?}\label{gs:sec:onlyone}
The requirement $N,m\to\infty$ is genuine, not a convenience.
From \eqref{gs:eq:coefficients}, dominated coefficient convergence gives
locally uniformly in $\tau\in\CC$:
\begin{align}\label{gs:eq:fixed-m}
 \Rgs_{N,m}(\tau)&\longrightarrow
 {}_0F_1(;m+1/2;(m+1/2)\tau)
 &&\text{if $m$ is fixed and $N\to\infty$},\\
 \Rgs_{N,m}(\tau)&\longrightarrow(1+\tau/N)^N
 &&\text{if $N$ is fixed and $m\to\infty$}.\label{gs:eq:fixed-N}
\end{align}
In the first line, each finite coefficient tends to $v^k/(v)_k$;
in the second it tends to $N^{\underline k}/N^k$ and the series is finite.
The bound $|A(k)\tau^k/k!|\le|\tau|^k/k!$ supplies domination.
The fixed-$m$ function is a standard hypergeometric/Bessel limit; no novelty
is claimed for that special-function phenomenon.

If $N,m\to\infty$, the same argument gives $\Rgs(\tau)\to e^\tau$
locally uniformly. Conversely, convergence to $e^\tau$ locally uniformly
forces $S_2=\beta\to0$ by comparing second derivatives at zero, hence
forces both parameters to diverge. Even convergence at a single fixed
positive $\tau$ is enough: the upper bound in \eqref{gs:eq:logbounds}, using
$W\le1$, forces $\beta\to0$.

\subsection{Physical sizes of the perturbation}\label{gs:sec:physical}
Remember $a=\tau/\mu$. For $1\ll m\ll N$,
$\mu\sim N^2/(4m)$ and $\beta\sim1/m$; for $m\gg N\gg1$,
$\mu\sim N/4$ and $\beta\sim1/N$.
The following table records \emph{orders of magnitude}; constants depend
on the aspect ratio in the proportional regime.
\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
\toprule
Regime & First $O(1)$ response & Relative exponential threshold
 & Nominal Poisson threshold\\
\midrule
$1\ll m\ll N$ & $m/N^2$ & $m^{3/2}/N^2$ & $m^{5/3}/N^2$\\
$m\asymp N\to\infty$ & $N^{-1}$ & $N^{-1/2}$ & $N^{-1/3}$\\
$m\gg N\gg1$ & $N^{-1}$ & $N^{-1/2}$ & $N^{-1/3}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The full nonlinear scale has $a\asymp m^2/N^2$ in the first regime and
$a\asymp1$ in the other two. The usual fixed-multiplicity $N^{-2}$ scale
therefore does not remain the correct first-response scale when the
multiplicity grows. Corollary~\ref{gs:cor:invisibility} gives the exact
criterion, without relying on this table.

For $b>0$, factor $b$ from every row:
\begin{equation}
 D_N^{(m)}(a,b)=b^N D_N^{(m)}(a/b,1).
\end{equation}
All conclusions translate by replacing $a$ with $a/b$. The probabilistic
statements require $a/b\ge0$; complex versions retain only the analytically
stated conclusions, not positivity or ordering assertions.

\section{Reproducible checks and computational methods}
\label{gs:sec:checks}

The package includes \texttt{code/verify.py}. Its exact layer uses rational
arithmetic and its numerical layer uses NumPy and SciPy. The latter does
not perform outward rounding and is \emph{not} an interval certificate.
The all-parameter results rest on the proofs above, not on the size of the
finite test grid.

\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{.63\linewidth}r@{}}
\toprule
Executed audit & Count\\
\midrule
Direct rational Hankel determinants versus the coefficient formula
($1\le N\le6$, $0\le m\le8$, five rational $a$ values) & 270\\
Parameter pairs comparing logarithmic-series extraction with the
Riccati recurrence, through moment 12 & 420\\
Individual exact moment equalities within those pairs & 5,040\\
Individual exact Catalan-majorant inequalities on the same grid & 5,040\\
Limiting-moment recurrence checks at five rational shapes,
through twelve recurrence steps & 60\\
Floating-point Jacobi-root parameter pairs & 16\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The 5,040 moment equalities are components of the 420 parameter-pair checks,
not 5,040 additional independently selected parameter pairs.
All assertions passed in the delivered run. The environment and detailed
counts are recorded in \texttt{data/verification\_summary.json}.

\noindent[Merge note: in this report the program is shipped as
\nolinkurl{code/06-growing-shifts-verify.py} and the summary as
\nolinkurl{data/06-growing-shifts-verification_summary.json}; see
Section~\ref{gs:sec:source}.]

For positive $\tau$, stable numerical evaluation uses the term ratio
\begin{equation}\label{gs:eq:numeric-ratio}
 \frac{T_{k+1}}{T_k}
 =\frac{\tau}{k+1}
   \frac{(1-k/N)(1+k/B)}{1+k/v}.
\end{equation}
The program accumulates logarithms of this ratio, applies log-sum-exp, and
then normalizes the terms. No ill-conditioned Hankel determinant or
approximate zero is needed for those tables. The exact moment recurrence
is used separately, so its checks are independent of floating-point
Jacobi-root finding.

\subsection{Two thresholds on a common sequence}\label{gs:sec:twothresholds}
For $N=m$, the following values use the exact $\beta$ in
\eqref{gs:eq:beta-theta}. The determinant column has $\tau=\beta^{-1/2}$;
the last column has $\tau=\beta^{-2/3}$.

\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrr@{}}
\toprule
$N=m$ & $\beta$ & $e^{-\tau}\Rgs(\tau)$ &
$\TV(\Law(K_\tau),\Pois(\tau))$\\
\midrule
64    & $2.3139313\cdot10^{-2}$ & 0.64422016 & 0.31341650\\
256   & $5.8404126\cdot10^{-3}$ & 0.62704465 & 0.33303790\\
1024  & $1.4636534\cdot10^{-3}$ & 0.61723707 & 0.34952143\\
4096  & $3.6613646\cdot10^{-4}$ & 0.61200022 & 0.36109809\\
16384 & $9.1548078\cdot10^{-5}$ & 0.60929502 & 0.36871668\\
\midrule
Limit & 0 & $e^{-1/2}=0.60653066$ & $2\Phi(1/2)-1=0.38292492$\\

The nominal-Poisson convergence is visibly slower. The finite values are

The files \texttt{spectral\_moments.csv}, \texttt{free\_energy.csv}, and
\texttt{saddle\_prefactor.csv} give additional checks of the Catalan moment
profile, the algebraic free energy, and the multiplicative saddle formula.
For the last, the program records both the relative error and $N$ times
that error, rather than only a visually agreeable plot.

\section{Proof dependencies and limitations}
\label{gs:sec:scope}

The dependency structure is short enough to audit explicitly:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.44\linewidth}
 >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.48\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Result & Required ingredients\\
\midrule
Exact normalized polynomial & Catalan beta integral; one-factor
Christoffel identity; Jacobi finite series\\
Moment recurrence and $W\le8\beta$ & Hypergeometric ODE; coefficient
comparison; Catalan convolution\\
Inverse-zero limit law & Moment recurrence; compact moment convergence\\
Critical hierarchy and free energy & Product representation;
logarithmic Taylor bounds; inverse-zero law\\
Nominal-Poisson transition & Bernoulli factorization; classical
bound \eqref{gs:eq:BH}; elementary Poisson comparison\\
Proportional-growth prefactor & Exact coefficients; uniform Stirling
bounds; one-dimensional discrete Laplace expansion\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
No unrefereed asymptotic assertion from another repository article is
needed as an unproved premise. The already-known coefficient identity is
rederived in the needed normalization. The exact numerical tests are
consistent with every result, but they do not replace any analytic step.

There are several important boundaries. The general theorems concern
one extra factor $x+a$ with a growing fixed-endpoint power $x^m$.
They do not settle moving clusters of growing cardinality, simultaneous
arbitrary shifts at both endpoints, or arbitrary polynomial multipliers.
The sharp-order and probability results are for $\tau\ge0$. Negative
perturbations can meet zeros, so analogous ratio criteria need not hold.
The critical hierarchy fixes its order $q$ before taking limits.
The saddle prefactor is uniform only when the aspect ratio and $t$ stay
in positive compact sets; it is not asserted uniform as those compact
sets approach zero or infinity.

Finally, this is an AI-assisted, unrefereed manuscript. No formal
verification in Lean or Rocq has been performed. The repository was
read, not modified. The literature audit establishes relevant precedents,
not worldwide novelty of each corollary.

\section{Further research questions}
\label{gs:sec:future}

\subsection{A sharp extreme-zero theorem}\label{gs:x:edge}
Does $W/\beta\to1+3\theta$ hold under exactly the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:spectral}, including the extreme ratios $\theta=0,1$?
An affirmative answer would replace the coarse constant eight by the
correct asymptotic edge. A proof needs an outlier-exclusion argument, not
just fixed-moment convergence. Quantitative Jacobi zero bounds or
recurrence-matrix comparisons are natural routes.

\subsection{Finite-size corrections to the inverse-zero measure}\label{gs:x:finite}
The exact Riccati recurrence gives every fixed moment with rational
coefficients in $N,m$. Determine a signed-measure or resolvent expansion
around $\nu_{\theta_{N,m}}$, retaining separate $N^{-1}$ and $m^{-1}$
effects. This would produce a multiplicative correction to
Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:freeenergy} beyond the proportional-growth case and give
rates for exact centering in the nonlinear central limit theorem.

\subsection{A uniform bridge from fixed to growing multiplicity}\label{gs:x:bridge}
The fixed-$m$ limit is ${}_0F_1(;v;v\tau)$, whereas growing $m$ gives the
hierarchy above. Construct a single two-parameter approximation with a
remainder uniform for $1\le m\le N$, not merely an iterated limit.
The exact coefficient product suggests a route, but uniform control near
its large-parameter transition requires more than dominated convergence.

\subsection{Several moving factors and their interactions}\label{gs:x:several}
For fixed $s\ge2$, study
$H_N(x^m\prod_{j=1}^s(x+a_j))/H_N(x^{m+s})$ as $N,m\to\infty$.
The one-factor theory does not imply a product of one-factor answers:
Christoffel determinants can contain mixed corrections. Determine the
first interaction term and whether a matrix-valued counterpart of
$\nu_\theta$ governs the joint hierarchy. Then let $s$ grow as well.

\subsection{Both endpoint exponents growing}\label{gs:x:both}
Replace the weight by $x^m(4-x)^\ell\,\dd\omega(x)$ with $m,\ell\to\infty$.
Its Jacobi differential equation still provides a rational Riccati
recurrence, but the shape space now has more than one parameter.
Identify the corresponding normalized inverse-zero law and the sharp
small-perturbation scales without assuming $m,\ell\asymp N$.

\subsection{Complex perturbations at the first zero edge}\label{gs:x:complex}
The current complex limits stay far inside a shrinking zero-free disk in
the original perturbation variable. Near the full negative scale,
$1+tx$ can vanish on the limiting support. Determine the local Airy or
Bessel regimes, the zero spacing, and the analytic continuation of the
free energy there. Positive-axis estimates cannot simply be continued
through a zero.

\subsection{Growing truncation order}\label{gs:x:order}
Theorem~\ref{gs:thm:hierarchy} fixes $q$. Determine the largest $q=q(N,m)$
for which exact moment subtraction remains uniformly useful, and compare
it with direct evaluation of the algebraic limit. This asks for moment
asymptotics uniform in the order, a problem not solved by the current
fixed-order induction.

\subsection{A direct combinatorial coefficient model}\label{gs:x:combinatorial}
The Bernoulli representation uses generally algebraic Jacobi zeros.
Find a combinatorial model for the normalized coefficients in which the
shape parameter and the mean displacement are visible without those
zeros. A positive integer interpretation of the repository's coefficient
triangle would be especially useful for this purpose.

\subsection{Certified numerical evaluation}\label{gs:x:certified}
Turn \eqref{gs:eq:numeric-ratio} into an interval algorithm with rigorous
finite tail bounds, and combine it with rational moment subtraction.
The delivered program is a diagnostic implementation, not that certified
algorithm. An efficient certificate should avoid forming large Hankel
matrices and should report bit complexity, not only operation counts.

\subsection{A staged formalization}\label{gs:x:formal}
A practical Lean project could first formalize the finite hypergeometric
polynomial, its ODE as a coefficient identity, and the rational recurrence.
The next stage is the positive-root factorization and the Catalan
majorant. The logarithmic estimates and the compact moment limit can
then be separated from the external Poisson theorem. This is a proposed
formalization architecture, not a claim that the necessary code has
already been written or checked.

\section{Conclusion}\label{gs:sec:conclusion}

The growing-shift question has a useful answer in a variable not visible
in fixed-$m$ recurrence formulas: the exact second inverse-zero moment
$\beta$. It identifies when both size parameters are genuinely large,
controls the largest inverse zero uniformly, and sets the critical scales.
The corresponding size-biased zero measure converges to a shifted
Catalan-moment law, which supplies every fixed-order logarithmic correction
and an algebraic resummation at the nonlinear scale.

The main distinction is operational as well as mathematical. A determinant
can already differ from its exponential approximation by a fixed factor
while its coefficient law is still close to the nominal Poisson law.
Later, the nominal mean becomes detectably wrong while a corrected-mean
Poisson approximation remains valid. Only at the full scale does the
algebraic nonlinear law replace the perturbative description.
These statements resolve the specified growing-shift continuation of the
repository problem, while leaving the stronger multi-factor, edge,
quantitative, and formalization questions explicitly open.

\section{A compact formula sheet}\label{gs:app:formulas}

This section is Appendix~A of the manuscript.

\begin{align*}
 v&=m+\tfrac12,& B&=N+m+1,&c&=\frac v{NB},&\mu&=\frac{NB}{4v},\\
 \Rgs(\tau)&={}_2F_1(-N,B;v;-c\tau),&
 \beta&=\frac{1+c(v+3/2)}{v+1},&
 \theta&=\frac1{(v+1)\beta}.
\end{align*}
The moments start with $S_1=1$, $S_2=\beta$ and satisfy
\eqref{gs:eq:moment-recurrence}. If $S_r\sim\beta^{r-1}d_r(\theta)$,
then
\[
 d_r(\theta)=\Egs(1-\theta+\theta Y)^{r-1},\qquad Y\sim\MP.
\]
The three central comparisons are
\begin{align*}
 \tau=s\beta^{-1/2}:\quad&
 e^{-\tau}\Rgs(\tau)\to e^{-s^2/2},\\
 \tau=u\beta^{-2/3}:\quad&
 \TV(\Law(K_\tau),\Pois(\tau))\to2\Phi(u^{3/2}/2)-1,\\
 \tau=t\beta^{-1}:\quad&
 \beta\log\Rgs(\tau)\to\calF_\theta(t).
\end{align*}
For positive real $\tau$, the first finite correction has the explicit
error certificate
\[
 0\le\log\Rgs(\tau)-\tau+\tfrac12\beta\tau^2
 \le\tfrac83\beta^2\tau^3.
\]
This is a proved analytic bound. Floating-point evaluation of its entries
still needs outward rounding before it becomes a numerical interval
certificate.

\section{Reproduction instructions}\label{gs:app:reproduction}

This section is Appendix~B of the manuscript.

The delivered archive has one top-level directory,
\texttt{Catalan\_Growing\_Shifts}. From that directory, run
\begin{verbatim}
python code/verify.py --output data
python code/make_figures.py
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
\end{verbatim}
The exact audit uses only the Python standard library. The full script
also imports NumPy and SciPy for diagnostics; figure generation uses
Matplotlib. The delivered run used Python 3.13.5, NumPy 2.3.5 and SciPy
1.17.0. These are recorded environment versions, not a requirement to
install those exact releases. No external data download is needed.

manuscript's appendix lists two \texttt{pdflatex} runs; its delivered
README and Makefile list three.]

\appendix
\section{The precise correction to the printed conjecture}\label{app:barry}

This appendix distinguishes two kinds of index.  The source uses a
size-$(n+1)$ determinant.  We write $N=n+1$.  To avoid silently
identifying incompatible shift conventions, write $s$ for the source's
symbol in its displayed coefficient formula, and reserve $m$ for
our actual Catalan shift.

For $s\geq2$ and $0\leq k\leq n+1$, the coefficient expression printed
in Conjecture~2 of~\cite{Barry2020} can be written as
\begin{align}
 T_{n,k,s}
 &=\frac{C_s}{2s-2}
   \binom{s+k-2}{s-2}\binom{n+k+2s-2}{2k+2s-3}\nonumber\\[-1pt]
 &\quad\times
  \prod_{j=0}^{\lfloor(s-1)/2\rfloor-1}
       \frac{\binom{2n+2s-2j-1}{2s-4j-5}}
            {\binom{2s-2j-1}{2s-4j-5}}
  \prod_{j=0}^{s-3}\frac{2s-j-2}{n+k+2s-j-2}.
 \label{eq:BarryT}
\end{align}
Only the natural, nonambiguous range $s\geq2$ is needed here.  Empty
products have value~$1$.

\begin{proposition}[Correct shift]\label{prop:Barry}
For $N\geq1$, $s\geq2$, and $0\leq k\leq N$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Barrycorrect}
 T_{N-1,k,s}=c_{N,s-1,k}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, the polynomial using $T_{N-1,k,s}$ is
$D_N^{(s-1)}(a,b)$, not $D_N^{(s)}(a,b)$.  Equivalently, to use that
printed expression for an actual shift $m\geq1$, replace its shift
symbol by $s=m+1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Taking a ratio of successive terms in \eqref{eq:BarryT} and cancelling
factorials gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Tkratio}
 \frac{T_{n,k+1,s}}{T_{n,k,s}}
 =\frac{(n+1-k)(n+k+s+1)}{2(k+1)(2k+2s-1)}.
\end{equation}
This agrees with \eqref{eq:coeff-update} after $N=n+1$ and $m=s-1$.
It remains to match the initial coefficient.
Putting $n=N-1$, $k=0$, and simplifying the factorials gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:TB0}
 T_{N-1,0,s}=C_s\frac{(N+s-1)!}{N!s!}
 \prod_{j=0}^{p-1}
 \frac{\binom{2N+2s-2j-3}{2s-4j-5}}
      {\binom{2s-2j-1}{2s-4j-5}},
 \qquad p=\left\lfloor\frac{s-1}{2}\right\rfloor.
\end{equation}
At $N=1$ this is $C_s=H_1^{(s)}$.  Its ratio at successive $N$ is
\begin{align}
 \frac{T_{N,0,s}}{T_{N-1,0,s}}
 &=\frac{N+s}{N+1}
   \prod_{j=0}^{p-1}
   \frac{(2N+2s-2j-1)(2N+2s-2j-2)}
        {(2N+2j+4)(2N+2j+3)}\nonumber\\
 &=\frac{(2N+2s)!(2N+1)!}
         {(2N+s)!(2N+s+1)!}.
 \label{eq:TNr}
\end{align}
In the product, the numerator factors form a consecutive interval of
integers, as do the denominator factors; the last equality holds for
either parity of $s$.  The shifted-product formula gives the identical
ratio
\[
 \frac{H_{N+1}^{(s)}}{H_N^{(s)}}
 =\prod_{i=1}^{s-1}
     \frac{(2N+2i+1)(2N+2i+2)}{(2N+i+1)(2N+i+2)}
 =\frac{(2N+2s)!(2N+1)!}{(2N+s)!(2N+s+1)!}.
\]
Induction in $N$ therefore proves $T_{N-1,0,s}=H_N^{(s)}$.
The ratio \eqref{eq:Tkratio} then proves \eqref{eq:Barrycorrect}
for every $k$.
\end{proof}

The discrepancy is visible in the smallest nontrivial instance.
With $N=1$ and $s=2$, the printed expression is
\[
 T_{0,0,2}b+T_{0,1,2}a=2b+a,
\]
whereas the determinant with the stated shift $s=2$ is
\[
 D_1^{(2)}(a,b)=aC_2+bC_3=2a+5b.
\]
At $a=b=1$, these are $3$ and $7$.  The expression $a+2b$ is exactly
$D_1^{(1)}(a,b)$, so the correction is systematic rather than an isolated
numerical exception.  The original PDF page was inspected as well as
its HTML rendering; this is not an artifact of an HTML conversion.
The denominator-exponent statement is separate and uses the actual
shift $m$ correctly.

\section{Compact reference and archive conventions}\label{app:reference}

\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.22\textwidth}p{0.72\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Symbol & Meaning\\
\midrule
$N$ & Determinant size, with $D_0=1$\\
$m$ & Actual Catalan shift in $aC_{i+j+m}+bC_{i+j+m+1}$\\
$H_N^{(m)}$ & Pure shifted Catalan determinant\\
$d,e,K$ & $\binom m2$, $\binom m2+1$, and $(m-1)^2$\\
$P_m$ & Uniform numerator, not automatically reduced at exceptions\\
$q$ & $1-(a+2b)t+b^2t^2$\\
$M_{m,\ell}$ & Minor with column $\ell$ deleted from the binomial block\\
$Q_m(N;r)$ & Signed generating polynomial of those minors\\
$r_\pm$ & Roots of $r^2-(a/b+2)r+1$\\
$\lambda_\pm$ & $br_\pm$, the characteristic roots in determinant size\\
$s$ in Appendix A & Source shift symbol, equal to actual shift plus one\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\noindent[Added 28 September 2026, batch 39: this table covers Part~I.
Part~II reuses several of these letters with other meanings, among them
$q$, $s$, $\ell$, $d$, $K$, $P$, $M$ and $r$; its symbols are listed in
Table~\ref{rc:tab:notation}, and its data files in
Section~\ref{rc:app:reproduce}.]

The JSON files use ascending polynomial powers.  In
\texttt{coefficient\_rows.json}, position $k$ is the coefficient of
$a^kb^{N-k}$.  In \texttt{minor\_polynomials.json}, position $j$ is the
coefficient of $N^j$.  In \texttt{two\_branch\_polynomials.json}, the
arrays \texttt{P\_plus} and \texttt{P\_minus} describe the polynomial
multipliers of $4^N$ and $1^N$ for $(a,b)=(1,2)$; they are not the
ordinary-generating-function numerator $P_m$.

The source audit records exact URLs, source versions, and search
limitations.  The tests establish reproducibility of the computation,
not priority or formal verification.  The archive intentionally contains
no third-party article PDFs, no font files, no compiler auxiliaries,
and no Python bytecode.
